===PAGE 3=== (ocr, 27 lines)
   1|                                                                                IN MEMORIAM
   2|              Stanley Barney passed away on July 12, 2023. Stan was born to the late Wilbur Walker Barney and Therese
   3|              Annette (LaChance) Barney on August 14, 1950.
   4|              Stan is survived by his loving wife Suzanne (Anderson) Barney, daughter Stephanie Barney and her partner
   5|              Alyssa Sharpe, sister Marie Barney, brother Michael G. Barney and wife Janice, nephew Michael R. Barney
   6|              and his fiancée Jillian Doucette, and several nieces, nephews, cousins and extended family. A son, Patrick M.
   7|              Barney, predeceased him in 2019.
   8|              Stan was blessed with a circle of lifelong friends, many of whom were at his side at the time of his passing.
   9|              In his litetime, Stan was involved in a wide range of activities: working at the family business (Barney's Res-
  10|              taurant and Dairy Bar), caring for animals, gardening, woodworking, and serving as captain of the Blue
  11|              Knights Football Team (#61!). He was an avid Boston sports fan, and was a Celtics season ticket holder for
  12|              many years. An avid gardener, Stan maintained beautiful flower and vegetable gardens. He gave away most of
  13|              the produce and flowers he grew, including pumpkins, jars of pickles he made with his mother and sister, and
  14|              fresh cut flowers. He loved the outdoors and even built his own lumber mill at his residence. Stan loved fishing
  15|              and snowmobiling with his friends. Stan was a life member of the Eastwood Club.
  16|              A 1968 graduate of Lunenburg High School, Stan attended Merrimack College and graduated from Fitchburg
  17|              State University. He married the former Suzanne Anderson of Great Barrington Massachusetts on October 6,
  18|              1973. Devoted to the Town of Lunenburg, Stan served for 38 years on the Lunenburg Police Department,
  19|              moving through the ranks to Sargent in 2000 and finally Lieutenant in 2013. He cherished serving the commu-
  20|              nity as a police officer along with his son. Stan was also a lifetime member of the Lunenburg Police Relief As-
  21|              sociation, serving as its President in 1984. He also served several terms as a Cemetery Commissioner for the
  22|              Town of Lunenburg, overseeing the maintenance and care of town cemetery grounds.
  23|              Stan was also employed by the law enforcement division of the Massachusetts Society for the Prevention of
  24|              Cruelty to Animals from 1986 to 1991, serving throughout 40 cities and towns. Devoted to animal rescue, Stan
  25|              built a barn/stable at his Lunenburg residence and provided a safe residence for rescued horses, including draft
  26|              horses and Clydesdales.
  27|                                                                                                3

===PAGE 6=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                                                                     LUNENBURG PROFILE
   2|                                                                    YEAR INCORPORATED-1728
   3|                                                                  REGISTERED VOTERS- 9,041
   4|                                                                        FY'23 TAX RATE- $14.62
   5|                                      OPEN TOWN GOVERNMENT- 5 MEMBER SELECT BOARD
   6|                                           ANNUAL TOWN ELECTION- 3RD SATURDAY IN MAY
   7|                                            ANNUAL TOWN MEETING- 1ST SATURDAY IN MAY
   8|                                                                     TOTAL AREA- 26.63 MILES
   9|                Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It occupies a land area of approximately 26.4
  10|                  quare miles. Lunenburg is locatea in north central Massachusetts approximately 43 miles trom Bo in, It is bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and the City of Fitchburg, on the south by th
  11|               Town of Lancaster and the City of Leominster, on the east by the Town of Shirley and on the north by
  12|                the Town of Townsend.
  13|                Local legislative decisions are made by an open town meeting consisting of all registered voters in
  14|               the town. Subject to the legislative decisions made by town meeting, the affairs of the town are gen-
  15|                erally administered by an elected five member Select Board and a Town Manager, Local school af-
  16|               fairs arc administered by an elected seven member School Committee while local taxes are assessed
  17|                by an elected three member Board of Assessors.
  18|                Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County. Lake Whalom and the for-
  19|               mer Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District from the remainder of Lunenburg. This
  20|                district was developed as a housing base for many factory workers working in the mills and factories of
  21|                Leominster and Fitchburg. In many ways the characteristics of the Whalom District closely reflect charac-
  22|               teristics of these cities more than of the rest of Lunenburg. The Hickory Hills Lake area was developed as
  23|                a private community comprised largely of vacation cottages. Shirley Reservoir is a public water body also
  24|                developed with mainly vacation cottages that have now been moditied to year-round residences.
  25|                Commercial and industrial development is permitted in several areas of town, primarily along the two
  26|                 major arteries (Routes 2A and 13) and the town's peripheries, due largely to the close proximity of
  27|                 Leominster's and Fitchburg's sewer and water systems. Although many farms and orchards had slow-
  28|                ly developed into single family housing, farming continues on in Lunenburg, In 2013 the Annual
  29|                 Town Meeting voted to establish an Agricultural Commission to represent, preserve and enhance
  30|                farming in the community and to administer the "Right to Farm" bylaw,
  31|                 The 2015 Lunenburg Town Meeting established the Architectural Preservation District ("District")
  32|                 bylaw to regulate construction, demolition or renovation of existing "Contributing Property" struc-
  33|                tures as defined by the bylaw within an established bounded district. The bylaw was introduced at
  34|                 Town Meeting along with thẹ Village District Bylaw, which created an overlay zoning district to en-
  35|                 courage mixed-use commercial and residential development. While the Village District Bylaw en-
  36|                 courages the economic vitalization of downtown Lunenburg, the Architectural Preservation District
  37|                 Bylaw ensures that it will not come at the expense of the town's historical character and integrity.
  38|              Scan the QR Code to visit our website at https://www.lunenburgma.gov
  39|              Follow us on social media on Facebook at https://www.facebook.com/lunenburgma
  40|                                                                                                6

===PAGE 18=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|                                                                  REPORT OF THE TOWN MANAGER
   2|                                                                                             FY23
   3|               It is my honor to submit the annual report on the activities of the Office of the Town Manager during Fiscal Year 2023.
   4|                There was a substantial amount of progress during Fiscal Year 2023, yet the year proved to be a continuation of the
   5|               effects of the pandemic and a mid-year tax assessment crisis that affected lakefront property owners occurred that im-
   6|               pacted the capacity to do more during Fiscal Year 2023. Since 2020, the Town has struggled with staffing levels in var-
   7|               ious departments, including attracting and retaining employees. The Town Manager and Assistant Town Manager have
   8|                worked on various ways to address staffing, including recruitment strategies and employee engagement. An interim
   9|               valuation adjustment through the Assessors triggered hundreds of abatement applications to be filed and the effects
  10|               were widespread. In the aftermath of the increases in lakefront valuations, certain processes and corrective actions have
  11|                been undertaken by the Select Board, Town Manager and Board of Assessors that will prevent a similar occurrence
  12|               from happening again.
  13|               Below I would like to highlight the topics that were addressed during Fiscal Year 2023 and the accomplishments with-
  14|               in the Office of the Town Manager:
  15|               • In July of 2022, as part of the annual goal setting process, the Selectmen reviewed the and voted to accept the
  16|                     Town Manager's annual goals for FY23. The Town Manager's goals for FY 2023 included continuing to take the
  17|                     necessary steps towards the redevelopment/reuse of 925 Massachusetts Avenue, enhancement of long term plan-
  18|                     ning in the areas of capital assets, staffing and infrastructure, and focusing on employee recruitment, recognition,
  19|                     and retention. A majority of the action items related to these three goals were completed.
  20|               • In regards to 925 Massachusetts Avenue, the Town Manager continued to work Joe McLaughlin of BETA Group,
  21|                     MassDEP, the EPAand MassDevelopment about possible cleanup funding. In FY 2023 the EPA paid for removal
  22|                     of the fuel tanks and canopy as part of the Targeted Brownfields Assessment funding. The Town Manager also
  23|                     applied for funding through MassDevelopment's Real Estate Technical Assistance funding to create conceptual
  24|                     plans, perform market analysis, and develop a Request for Proposals in order to dispose of the property. As it relat-
  25|                     ed to one of the action items under the Town Manager's annual goals for road and bridge planning, the DPW Di-
  26|                     rector worked with BETA Group to update the Town's five year pavement management plan and identified the
  27|                     costs of creating a similar plan for bridges and culverts, that would be undertaken in FY24.
  28|               • In July 2022, the Select Board set their goals for the upcoming year: they amended their FY22 goal/work on the
  29|                     old Primary School at 30 School Street, work with the Town Manager and Finance Director to establish a means-
  30|                     tested senior citizen property tax exemption or abatement, and lastly to review and update Select Board related by-
  31|                     laws and policies.
  32|               • The Town Manager reported to the Board in July that the $500,000 Small Bridge Grant that she worked with the
  33|                     DPW Director and engineering firm on submitting was awarded to the Town for the Flat Hill Culvert project. This
  34|                     funding was needed to supplement the Town's local appropriation of funds, due to the estimated construction costs
  35|                     for the project.
  36|               • The Town Manager worked with town counsel and Until to finalize the Purchase & Sale and License Agreement
  37|                     in order to purchase the streetlights from Until. This was a necessary step as well before going out to bid to con-
  38|                     vert the streetlights to LED.
  39|                                                                                               18

===PAGE 19=== (ocr, 35 lines)
   1|                   In August of 2022 the Town Manager notified the Board that the Town was awarded the $187,140 for
   2|                         the competitive Green Communities Grant she submitted, which included $172,140 for the conver-
   3|                         sion of the streetlights to LED.
   4|              • As previously mentioned, Fiscal Year 2023 proved to be another difficult year for retaining staff and fill-
   5|                   ing vacancies. The pandemic created opportunities for employees to move to new careers and made it
   6|                   more difficult to attract and retain employees. In addition to this, the Town identified that wages needed
   7|                   a market adjustment to attract candidates that ultimately would need to be addressed through the Salary
   8|                   Administration Plan Bylaw for non-union employees and collective bargaining agreements for union po-
   9|                   sitions. The Assistant Town Manager/HR Director, who is responsible for advertising vacancies and
  10|                   working with the relevant departments to bring on new employees was constantly reviewing applications,
  11|                   interviewing, and trying to fill vacancies throughout the year. The following are a list of positions that
  12|                   were filled, either due to vacancies or creation of a new position, during FY 2023: Police Officers (3),
  13|                   Reserve Police Officer, Facilities Director (2), Conservation Administrator, Public Access Videographer,
  14|                   Public Safety Desk Officer, Civilian Traffic Members, IT Administrative Assistant (new position), Rec-
  15|                   reation Director, Tax Collector Principal Clerk (2), Alternate Electrical Inspector, Heavy Equipment Op-
  16|                   erators (4), Public Access Production Technician (new position), Building Commissioner, Planning
  17|                   Board Administrative Assistant, Seasonal Beach Staff, part-time Police Administrative Assistant, Con-
  18|                   servation Administrative Assistant (2), and Meal Site Manager.
  19|              • The Town Manager, the Assistant Town Manager, and the relevant staff worked together on putting a
  20|                   variety of projects out to bid in FY 2023, including the DPW Road Materials Bid, the Hollis Road Win-
  21|                   dow Replacement, Master Planning Consulting Services, Wetland & Professional Land Surveyor Ser-
  22|                   vices for Marshall Park, 30 School Street Hazardous Materials Abatement and Demolition, Cell Tower
  23|                   Lease for Town Hall, the Page Street Culvert Construction, Auditing and Consulting Services for the
  24|                   LED Streetlights project, the Removal of Solid Waste Materials at the Woodruff Property (no bids re-
  25|                   ceived), Town Hall Building Envelope Repairs (all bids rejected), Tax Assessment Services (to handle all
  26|                   the inspections related to tax abatement applications), Real Property Data Collection Services, Five Year
  27|                    Assessing Revaluation Services, Roadway Maintenance for Fog Sealing, Public Safety Carport Project
  28|                   (rejected all bids), Guardrail Repairs, and the Senior Center ADA Outdoor Patio.
  29|              • The Town Manager had a Fraud Risk Assessment performed that was partially paid for with a MIIA Flex
  30|                   Grant and $7,800 for a Recycling Dividends Grant that helped fund the Trash & Recycling Coordinator
  31|                    position.
  32|              • Over the course of the fiscal year the Board discussed evaluation criteria and development of a process to
  33|                   review and commit the $3.5M in ARPA (American Rescue Plan Act) funding through the Coronavirus
  34|                   State and Local Fiscal Recovery Funds program.
  35|                                                                                               19

===PAGE 20=== (ocr, 34 lines)
   1|                   By the end of the fiscal year the Board had committed funds for the Public Safety Building Security
   2|                  Camera System ($81,054), funds for tax assessment services and related investigation ($150,000), funds
   3|                  to purchase a new ambulance ($506,000), two large DPW trucks ($480,000), funding for the Marshall
   4|                  Park project ($1,000,000), funding for consulting services for ARPA reporting ($60,000), funding for an
   5|                  LPS Athletic Field Study ($50,000), and supplemental funding for the five year revaluation ($30,000).
   6|             • The Special Town Meeting in November 2022 included a number of articles such as amending the FY23
   7|                  budget, payment of prior year bills, funding for the first year of the collective bargaining agreement with
   8|                  the Police Union, amending the Sewer Enterprise budget, transfer available funds to the Sewer Capital
   9|                   Reserve Stabilization Fund, funding for engineering for Marshall Park, amending the Salary Administra-
  10|                  tion Plan Bylaw to add certain positions, acceptance of MGL Ch. 32 sec. 4(2)(b), an article from the
  11|                  Planning Board, an article from the APDC to amend the bylaw, an article to override the denial of the
  12|                  certificate to alter the Ritter Building by the APDC, an article to amend the bylaw on capital appropria-
  13|                   tions, and two citizen petition articles.
  14|             • In December, the Board approved all the annual liquor licenses, common victualler, weekday entertain-
  15|                  ment, automatic amusement, jukebox, and motor vehicle licenses. Throughout the year, the Board re-
  16|                  views Earth Removal permits, liquor license transfers, flammable storage licenses, pole petitions, toll-
  17|                  booth requests, and First Right of Refusal Requests for Chapter 61 properties.
  18|             • In January of 2023 the Town Manager presented her preliminary FY23 budget priorities. Her recommendation
  19|                   was to address staffing needs conservatively due to the projected revenues and non-discretionary costs.
  20|                   This would mean pausing the 10-year staffing plan for the Police Department due to the impact that year
  21|                  2 of the Police Union Collective Bargaining Agreement would have on the budget. The 5-year Fire/EMS
  22|                   plan to get to a full time Paramedic Level Service was realized in FY23, so there were no plans to add
  23|                  staff to the Fire Department in FY24. Other priorities included additional IT equipment, cemetery im-
  24|                  provements, constructing a small kennel at the Public Safety Building, hazardous tree removal, addition-
  25|                  al funding for ADA public rights of way improvements, drainage improvements, and additional funding
  26|                   for library programming. At that point in time health insurance costs were estimated to be an 8.5% in-
  27|                   crease, the retirement assessment would be increasing by 13%, and other insurances would be increasing
  28|                   by 5%.
  29|             • In January the Town Manager presented the Preliminary Capital Plan for FY24 for the funding of 6 pro-
  30|                  jects totaling $1,360,500 and a 10 year look ahead at projected capital needs. The capital plan was modi-
  31|                   fied to include an additional 6 projects prior to town meeting, as the Select Board decided to fund three of
  32|                  the projects using ARPA funds. The final plan included 12 projects for a grand total of $2,025,010
  33|                  (including $986,000 from ARPA funding).
  34|                                                                                               20

===PAGE 21=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|                        At the January 10th meeting, a large number of residents around Lake Shirley, Hickory Hills, and Lake
   2|                    Whalom attended the Select Board meeting to speak during public comment about their 3"d and 4" quarter
   3|                    tax bills increasing significantly. Many residents voiced that their property valuations increased more than
   4|                    50%. On January 17* the Select Board held a special meeting at the Middle/High School Auditorium and
   5|                    hundreds attended. The meeting was scheduled to allow the public to ask questions to the Board, Principal
   6|                    Assessor, and Board of Assessors. Ultimately the Select Board voted to conduct an investigation into the
   7|                    effects that the pandemic had on property values in 2021, the corresponding impact on the FY 2023 tax
   8|                    assessments, and the process by which the Town conducted property valuations and distributed tax assess-
   9|                    ments to residents. The Chairman of the Board led the investigation, with the assistance of the Vice-
  10|                    Chairman, and in coordination and collaboration with the Town Manager. The following six months of
  11|                    the fiscal year were significantly impacted by the large increase in lake property valuations, including the
  12|                    need to hire an outside consulting firm to conduct the over 400 property inspections that resulted in
  13|                    $1,317,690 in abatements that were issued in the fourth quarter of FY23. The consultant issued a report to
  14|                    the Board of Assessors in FY23, and reported that the Principal Assessor found that two-thirds of the sales
  15|                    that occurred in calendar year 2021 had an ASR (Assessment to Sale Ratio) of .40, indicating that a major
  16|                    adjustment was needed in order to bring values in line with the Department of Revenue guidelines (.90-
  17|                    1.10). When the Principal Assessor made this interim year adjustment, it created an increase in land val-
  18|                    ues in some cases of around 135%. The consultant recommended creating a new adjustment factor that
  19|                    addressed that not every lakefront property is the same, including "Good, Average, Below Average". The
  20|                    Board of Assessors endorsed this recommendation and this determined the revised valuation and the
  21|                    abatements issued.
  22|              • The Town Manager presented a balanced budget for Fiscal Year 2024 to the Finance Committee and Se-
  23|                    lectmen in February. Part of the Town Manager's recommendation was to continue to fund the Other
  24|                    Post-Employment Benefits Trust Fund for the unfunded liability of retiree health insurance in the amount
  25|                    of $282,855, an additional $50,000 towards Pavement Management, fund the regular Stabilization fund in
  26|                    the amount of $100,000, $250,000 towards the Special Purpose Stabilization Fund, and use $1,500,000 in
  27|                    free cash towards the Overlay account to fund the "worst case scenario" for FY23 property tax abate-
  28|                    ments. The FY2024 budget also included the hiring of a part time COA Outreach Assistant and increase
  29|                    in hours for different positions including the Assistant Town Manager, the COA Outreach Coordinator,
  30|                    Treasurer/Collector, and funding for recreational programming salaries. The budget also included a
  31|                    $40,000 increase for Solid Waste/Recycling Program, a decrease of $979,710 in maturing interest and
  32|                    debt payments, 2.5% increase for Lunenburg Public Schools and a 12% increase for the Montachusett
  33|                    Technical School Assessment. Health Insurance rates increased by 6.2% for active employee plans and
  34|                    $271,000 from the Health Insurance Stabilization Fund was used to offset the health insurance costs. The
  35|                    total omnibus budget for FY24 that was approved in May was $42,584,048.
  36|                                                                                               21

===PAGE 22=== (ocr, 35 lines)
   1|              • In March of 2023 Select Board member Todd Dwyer submitted his resignation due to his moving out of
   2|                    town. The Board did not hold a special election to fill the vacancy.
   3|              • In April of 2023 Powers and Sullivan presented the results of the FY 2022 Annual Audit and
   4|                    presented that the Town had addressed any prior year comments.
   5|              • In May, following the annual election, the Board reorganized and voted Michael-Ray Jeffreys as the
   6|                    Chair, Lou Franco as Vice Chair and the newest member of the Board Caesar Nuzzolo as Clerk.
   7|                    Renee Emkey was elected at the May 2023 Annual Town Election as well.
   8|              • The Selectmen and the Town Manager made their annual appointments in June for FY 2024.
   9|              • In FY 2022, the Town had submitted a certificate to alter for the Ritter Building that included the
  10|                    replacement of all the windows with historic looking windows to become more energy efficient and
  11|                   install an ADA compliant ramp to the entrance of the building and the APDC denied the application.
  12|                    The Facilities Director Chris Ruth and hired architect worked on resubmitting a Certificate to Alter
  13|                    with the APDC for this project during FY 2023. Ultimately, this project would be approved by the
  14|                    APDC early in FY 2024.
  15|               Seven and a half years has passed since I was appointed your Town Manager and I am thankful for the
  16|              many residents I have met, work with on various committees, and represent on a daily basis overseeing the
  17|              operations of the Town.
  18|              I have the privilege of working with many talented individuals that are committed to helping the residents
  19|              and business owners of Lunenburg and are consistently looking for ways to better serve the public. The
  20|              accomplishments noted above, among the numerous day-to-day responsibilities, could not happen without
  21|              the cooperation of dedicated staff, appointed, and elected officials that make the wheels of town government
  22|              turn. My Executive Assistant Elaine Peterson retired in March of 2024 and I am thankful for her service to
  23|              the town and keeping our office running smoothly. Annie Aubrey has stepped into the Executive Assistant
  24|              position and is already doing a great job. Thank you to the Assistant Town Manager Julie Belliveau for
  25|              juggling the multitude of projects that require to be put out to bid, as well all her HR Director duties that
  26|              include trying to fill the many positions this past year. I would also like to thank the committee and board
  27|              members, department heads and staff for the high level of service they provide to the residents of town.
  28|              Specifically, I would like to express gratitude to the current and former department heads that led our
  29|              various departments in Fiscal Year 2023: Thomas Gammel, Police Chief; Patrick Sullivan, Fire Chief; Bill
  30|              Bernard, DPW Director; Karen Brochu, Interim Town Accountant/Finance Director; Ezequiel Ayala, current
  31|              Town Accountant/Finance Director; Myleen Mallari, Treasurer/Collector; Rena Swezey, Interim Principal
  32|              Assessor; Lynda McQuade, current Principal Assessor; Adam Burney, former Land Use Director; Richard
  33|              Harris, Interim Land Use Director; Sue Doherty, COA Director; Muir Haman, Library Director; Kathy

===PAGE 45=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                                                                        BOARD OF ASSESSORS
   2|                    The Mission Statement of the Board of Assessors is to accurately determine the value of all Real
   3|             and Personal Property within the Town of Lunenburg, as outlined in Massachusetts General Law
   4|             Chapter 59. The assessed value in every community in the Commonwealth must reflect fair market
   5|             value as of January 1 prior to the start of any given fiscal year.
   6|                    The Assessors are also responsible for the administration of all property tax data records. It
   7|             maintains accurate parcel ownership data based on recorded property transactions at the Worcester
   8|             North Registry of Deeds. The Assessors process and administer auto excise as well as personal real
   9|             estate tax exemptions for the Senior and Surviving Spouse, Blind, and Disabled Veterans.
  10|                    The Board of Assessors is elected and consists of three members. Each Assessor must complete a
  11|             required DOR Training (Course 101, Assessment Administration: Law, Procedures, and Valuation for
  12|             assessing board members). In the Town of Lunenburg, the Assessor's office staff consists of a full
  13|             time Principal Assessor and a full time Assessors Clerk.
  14|             The last 18 months have been tumultuous for the Board of Assessors.
  15|                    There has been a 100% turnover of the Board of Assessors' membership. 1 new Member was
  16|                    elected in May 2023, while 2 new Members were appointed to fill vacant slots until May 2024.
  17|                    The Principal Assessor resigned and was replaced with an Interim Lead Assessor. After a lengthy
  18|                    search process, a full time Lead Assessor has been identified and will start late March 2024.
  19|                    The Town has issued a historic number of Property Tax abatements in FY 2023. We handled a 30
  20|                    x increase in Abatement applications (440 vs 14). The overwhelming majority of the abatements
  21|                    were settled in a timely fashion.
  22|                    FY 2024 is a state-mandated 5-Year Revaluation for the every property in Lunenburg. The Prop-
  23|                    erty Tax abatement effort led to an unprecedented delay in completing that revaluation and de-
  24|                    termining the tax rate for FY 2024. This resulted in a delay in distribution of the final FY 2024 tax
  25|                    bills. Because of the unusual and significant work load, two outside agencies, Vision Government
  26|                    Solutions, Inc. and Municipal Financial Services (MFS), have been engaged to provide assessment
  27|                    personnel for the abatement process and DOR filings for the 5-year Revaluation.
  28|                    The Assessor Operations continue be resource-constrained. Currently, we only have 1 Interim
  29|                    Lead Assessor, and are temporarily short the Assessor Clerk. In the MFS report on the FY2023
  30|                    Abatement process, it was recommended that Lunenburg add a part time person to help per-
  31|                    form the normal Assessor Office work load. This included site inspections, office coverage, data
  32|                    analytics and records maintenance. Currently, the resource constraint is being partially ad-
  33|                    dressed by the external contractors previously mentioned.
  34|                    The Board of Assessors is committed to restoring the trust of Lunenburg tax-payers. We hope to
  35|             accomplish this through improving transparency, better out-reach, and enhancing visibility and ac-
  36|             cess.
  37|                    We have begun this by scheduling all meetings during evenings (with both PACC coverage and
  38|             Zoom access), so everyone can participate more easily. We created a Data Collection Manual for use
  39|             during the 5-year Revaluation. We are also putting the finishing touches on a guide for "How to Read
  40|             Your Vision Property Card", that will be available in hard-copy and via the internet.
  41|                                                                                               45

===PAGE 46=== (ocr, 28 lines)
   1|                    To be more inclusive of Lunenburg's tax-payers, we are planning to create an ad hoc, Citizens' Ad-
   2|             visory Committee to help us determine to best way to answer citizens questions, prioritize initiatives,
   3|             data analysis and generally hold the Board's collective feet to the fire. The Board of Assessors has de-
   4|             veloped a Property Tax orientation for use at the Senior Center to explain the options that are availa-
   5|             ble to reduce the tax burden for our Senior Citizens.
   6|                    We are improving visibility and access by embarking on a redesign of the Website. This will in-
   7|             clude the resources mentioned above, as well as Frequently Asked Questions. Other information will
   8|             include analytics and explanations provided by the Massachusetts DOR/ Division of Local Services,
   9|             Massachusetts Association of Assessing Officers, and UMass among others.

  18|               The Town of Lunenburg Building Department is Located in the Ritter Memorial Building located at 960 Massachu-
  19|               setts Avenue. The Building Department works to efficiently, effectively, and consistently ensure a safe, sustainable,
  20|               and vibrant community through effective oversight of construction and development activities by enforcing the Mas-
  21|               sachusetts state Building, Plumbing, Gas, and Electrical Codes, The Architectural Access Board Regulations, and
  22|               Lunenburg's Zoning Bylaw and Town Code. The Building Department also plays a critical role in assisting business
  23|               through the process of obtaining permits and ensuring compliance with all relevant codes and regulations. A commit-
  24|               ment to being accessible, responsive and to providing a high level of customer service empowers us to be a partner
  25|               the community, property owners, businesses, contractors, and developers can rely on when needed. The Building De-
  26|               partment maintains this commitment by:
  27|                  • Ensuring the public has consistent access to effective and courteous assistance in obtaining information, permits
  28|                    or approvals related to construction, building or zoning regulations and providing clear information about regula-
  29|                    tions, permitting requirements and associated fees.
  30|                 • Maintaining a consistent level of communication by responding to all inquiries and applications in a timely man-
  31|                    ner.
  32|                 • Enforcing building codes, regulations, local codes, and safety standards to guarantee the safety of occupants and
  33|                     the community.
  34|                 • Improving and optimizing the permitting process to facilitate efficient and timely approvals for construction pro-
  35|                    jects while maintaining thorough and consistent review procedures.
  36|                 • Utilizing technology to streamline internal processes, enhance data management, and provide better online ser-
  37|                     vices for applicants and property owners.
  38|                 • Regularly reviewing and assessing departmental processes and policies, seeking opportunities for improvement,
  39|                    efficiency, and effectiveness in serving the community.
  40|                 • Coordinating with other departments to ensure a streamlined approach to permitting and enforcement as needed.
  41|                 • Receiving and evaluating feedback from the public and other interested parties, and implementing changes as
  42|                    needed to ensure the department goals are being met.

===PAGE 62=== (ocr, 53 lines)
   1|                                                                 CONSERVATION COMMISSION
   2|               The Town of Lunenburg Conservation Commission is a seven-member commission, each appointed to 3-year terms by the
   3|               Select Board, whose duties are codified under the Conservation Commission Act MGL chapter 40 s 8C. The Commission
   4|              enforces the Massachusetts Wetland Protection Act MGL chapter 131 s 40 and Chapter 239 of the local bylaws for the pro-
   5|              tection of wetlands. The Commission also manages, and controls town owned lands retained for conservation and passive
   6|              recreation use.
   7|               During 2023 the commission had a very busy and active agenda of public hearings for a wide range of projects from com-
   8|              mercial development, subdivisions and small single family upkeep projects and larger scale industrial/commercial pro-
   9|               posals.
  10|               The Commission also held many inspections with the Commission Administrator on weekends prior to meetings to famil-
  11|              iarize themselves with the upcoming proposals on the agenda. The Commission continues to work with the town on famil-
  12|              iarizing itself with the continually changing storm water mandates from the EPA and working towards compliance both by
  13|               procedure and with any necessary codification as well as aiding with the community implementations the "MS4" regula-
  14|               tions.
  15|               The Conservation Commission continues to implement forest stewardship plans developed using grants as outlined in the
  16|              last three annual reports. The upcoming management effort will be concentrated on the Rayno property on Northfield
  17|              Road. The Hollis Road property stewardship plan is oriented toward enhancing bird wildlife habitat with the Mass Audu-
  18|              bon Society as part of the DCR "Forestry for the Birds" program. The funding from the sale of harvested timber provides
  19|              the seed money to make it possible for the Commission to apply for grants to maintain and improve Lunenburg's conserva-
  20|               tion lands. In 2023, the Conservation Commission was the beneficiary of the Shawn H. Kelley Estate which will further
  21|               support ongoing maintenance and improvements.
  22|              The Pollinator Habitat portion of the Hollis Rd property provides for a diversity of native flowers throughout the growing
  23|              season. The Habitat promotes insect egg laying and nesting sites for pollinators. The Habitat provides food sources for
  24|               birds and numerous insects including native bees and butterflies. This unique "Hidden Gem" helps control soil erosion and
  25|               oxygenates our air.
  26|              The Commission has made significant progress on "You Are Here" trail map signs at trail heads and trail intersections to
  27|               expand the safe use of Lunenburg's existing trail network. In April of 2023, Conservation partnered with Parks to coordi-
  28|               nate a month of Earth Day events across Lunenburg. The joint project improved 13 properties and brought 361 man-hours
  29|              of improvements to Lunenburg. In June of 2023, the Conservation Commission was awarded a MassTrails grant for fund-
  30|              ing planning of parking areas in Table Rock and Rayno Conservation Area, and improved access to the Harold Harley
  31|               Wildlife Viewing Platform in the Cowdrey Conservation Area. The Lunenburg Snow Riders continue to support the
  32|               maintenance and improvement of the trail system on Lunenburg's conservation lands. As part of their Recreational Trails
  33|               grant award from 2022, the Snow Riders recently completed the Lane Conservation Area trail improvement project with
  34|               aid from Baer Construction, Dan Deleo Plumbing, Gatsby Grounds, M. Proctor Masonry, and the assistance of 17 volun-
  35|              teers, including numerous volunteer tractors. The Commission would like to express our gratitude for all the hard-working
  36|               volunteers that work so enthusiastically in maintaining and improving Lunenburg's conservation lands!
  37|              The Commission has also continued to work closely with Lake Shirley and Hickory Hills on their lake management plans.
  38|              These plans set goals and action items designed to give a better picture of the overall health of the lakes and define various
  39|               strategies to ensure continued protection of these valuable resources.
  40|              During the year 2023, there were changes of personnel along creating staffing shortages. In July, Conservation Administra-
  41|              tor Anna Petrie resigned. Approximately May, Conservation Administrative Assistant, Rebecca Manley resigned. In July,
  42|              Ashleigh Denio was hired to backfill Rebecca. In October, the Land Manager, Adam Burney resigned. Adam was the front
  43|              -line management support for the Conservation Commission staff and had been providing direction to the interim Conser-
  44|              vation Administrator. There have been several interim administrators hired to bridge the gap in support since Anna's depar-
  45|              ture. There has also been an interim clerical resource provided to mitigate the backlog in Meeting Minutes. There is still a
  46|              backlog of Conservation Administrator actions, and in some cases significant delays in the issuance of documentation, ex-
  47|               amples: Certificates of Compliance, Orders of Conditions, Administrative Determinations, etc.
  48|               The Commission thanks all the town agencies for a great year and look forward to many more!
  49|              Respectfully Submitted,
  50|              Mary Wilson, Chair                                                 Robert Pease, Vice-Chair                      Richard Bursch
  51|              Chris St. Germain                                                  Michael Larouche                              Jeffrey Viviano
  52|              Jenny Pewtherer                                                    Sophia Dorow, Conservation Administrator

   1|                                                                           COUNCIL ON AGING
   2|             The mission of the Council on Aging is to advocate for the improved quality of
   3|             life for all seniors through supportive services, programs, and education. Coun-
   4|             cils on Aging were established under Massachusetts General Law Chapter 40,
   5|             section 8B, to include setting policy for the administration of elder programs or
   6|             services and to develop, coordinate, and conduct such activities which advocate
   7|             for elder issues.
   8|             The FY23 Council on Aging staff consisted of Susan Doherty, Director; Aman-
   9|             da Koeck, Administrative Assistant and Transportation; Pauline Roy, Outreach
  10|             Coordinator; Pamela Myllykangas, Meal Site Coordinator; Sandra Ricci, Asst.
  11|             Meal Site Coordinator; Elsa Watson and Ann Penney, Meal Site Assistants;
  12|             Jim McGuigan, David Gallagher, Michele Beauvais, & Kimberly Moore, MART Van Drivers.
  13|             The Council on Aging Board is an eleven-member board appointed to 3-year terms by the Lunenburg Select Board. The
  14|             FY23 Council on Aging Board members were Chairperson Deb Lincoln,
  15|             Vice-Chairperson Jane Rabbitt, Barbara Brown, Betty DiGiacomo, Pete Lincoln, Cheryl Moisan, Diane Nowd, John Pe-
  16|             senti, Susan O'Sullivan, Judith Tarbell & Jacquelyn Dwyer.
  17|             The Town budget alone does not support this senior center. Other sources of funding are the Eagle House Supporters
  18|             Inc., a non-profit 501c3 group, Formula Grant funding from the Commonwealth of Massachusetts, and Montachusett
  19|             Regional Transit Authority.
  20|             In FY23 the Eagle House Senior Community Center rebranded its name to the Lunenburg Adult Activity Center. The
  21|             change has encouraged all person's age 50+ to visit the center and participate in programming.
  22|             Construction began on a new ADA compatible patio/recreation/pavilion area at the center in June 2023. Funding was
  23|             obtained through a $100,000 State Earmark, $100,000 in town capital funding, $7500 Community Foundation Grant, and
  24|             approximately $63,099 in donations to the Council on Aging Gift Fund. The construction was 95% completed in Octo-
  25|             ber 2023 and a grand opening was held on October 11, 2023.
  26|             We are incredibly lucky to have a large volunteer force at the Council on Aging. In FY23 we had fifty-six volunteers
  27|             who contributed 1604 hours of their time to help us out by doing things, such as driving for the meals on wheels pro-
  28|             gram, program leaders, kitchen helpers, Memory Café, office help, library, Discovery Center navigators, and friendly
  29|             visitors/phone calls. We greatly appreciate their service.
  30|             In FY23 we offered a variety of programs including Meals on Wheels, congregate lunches and breakfast programs, trans-
  31|             portation, health and wellness, arts and crafts, bocce, cards, and games, cultural, Veterans Breakfast, movies, Historical
  32|             Society presentations, information and referral, Memory Café, health insurance counseling, and social events both at the
  33|             senior center and offsite to people age 50t.
  34|             There were approximately 1124 people who utilized the senior center in FY23. Those 1124 people compiled 25,387
  35|             units of duplicated service by participating in the programs listed above.
  36|             The Lunenburg Council on Aging newsletter lists current events and is available for you online at
  37|             www.mycommunityonline.com, or you can pick up a copy at the Lunenburg Adult Activity Center, Town Hall, Library,
  38|             Post Office, local churches, and various businesses throughout the community.
  39|             We are truly fortunate to have a Town Government that supports our center and understands the importance of support-
  40|             ing the Council on Aging to maintain quality services for our residents as they age.
  41|             Special thanks go to our 501c3 fundraising group, Eagle House Supporters Inc. This group provides funding for new
  42|             living room furniture, office/kitchen supplies, various programs & entertainment, and garden upkeep at the Eagle House
  43|             Senior Community Center. Also special thanks to all our outside agencies, especially the Lunenburg Police and Fire De-
  44|             partments, for their support of programming for our clients.
  45|             I want to thank the staff and volunteers at the Lunenburg Adult Activity Center for their caring and dedication to our cli-
  46|             ents. Moving forward, the Lunenburg Council on Aging remains committed to bringing new and innovative program-
  47|             ming for those aged 50+ in our community while maintaining long lasting relationships with those who we serve now
  48|             Respectfully Submitted
  49|             Susan Doherty, Director
  50|             Lunenburg Council on Aging
  51|                                                                                               63

===PAGE 64=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                                                                               CULTURAL COUNCIL
   2|             The mission of the Lunenburg Cultural Council is to nurture and promote a diverse range of artistic and cultural pro-
   3|             grams for the benefit of Lunenburg and surrounding communities. These include: programs that support science and
   4|             nature education, performing arts, and townwide cultural enrichment. We accomplish this through annual allocation of
   5|             state grants for local projects and by producing cultural events using locally-raised funds. We are committed to nurtur-
   6|             ing our artistic community through supporting events that are diverse, accessible, collaborative, and fun for Lunenburg
   7|             residents and visitors of all ages with priority given to in-person, live events.
   8|             The Lunenburg Cultural Council, with grants from the Massachusetts Cultural Council, funded twenty-two art, cultural,
   9|             music, and science programs for the benefit of Lunenburg residents and members of the surrounding community in
  10|             2023.
  11|             Some of the programs funded include: Clear Path for Veterans New England, Discovery Museum, Fitchburg Art Muse-
  12|             um's Regional Art Show, the Lunenburg Public Library, MUSIC Dance.edu, Nashoba Symphonic Band, N. Worcester
  13|             County Symphony Orchestra & School of Music, Cannon Theatre, Sizer Foundation, Special Needs Advocacy Network,
  14|             StageWorks, Virginia Thurston Healing Garden, Winchendon Music Festival, Winchendon Winds, Worcester Chamber
  15|             Music Society, and individual artists who brought programs to our community.
  16|             In addition to grant allocations, the Lunenburg Cultural Council sponsored drawing and pastel workshops with local
  17|             artist Jill Pottle at the Lunenburg Public Library.
  18|             We worked to increase visibility and awareness of both our town's council and the availability of funding through the
  19|             Massachusetts Cultural Council, for artistic and cultural programs. In 2023 the council created post cards to distribute
  20|             throughout Lunenburg and the immediate area and posters for events. Our hope is to encourage new grant applicants
  21|             to apply with innovative, varied program proposals for upcoming grant cycles, open each year from September Ist to
  22|             mid-October, as well as inform the community of the support available for local artists.
  23|             In 2023, under the guidance of former council member, Dr. Robin Chandler, with support from the Town Selectboard
  24|             and a grant from ARPA, the Lunenburg Cultural Council began a partnership with Lunenburg schools to pilot a program
  25|             to bring artists into the schools. This program solicits professional artists to participate in a one-day residency at Lunen-
  26|             burg High School to share their knowledge and skills with participating students. The first two artists were Rob Gibbs
  27|             (Problak), a visual mural artist from Boston, and Elizabeth James-Perry, an Aquinnah Wampanoag artist from Massa-
  28|             chusetts.
  29|             Problak was named one of Boston's Most Influential People in 2022. His strong commitment to creativity and youth
  30|             education was the basis for his Lunenburg residency. He has created such murals as the Dewey Square Mural on the
  31|             Rose Kennedy Greenway in Boston as well as others in major cities. He recently finished 'Breathe Life V', part of his
  32|             Breathe Life series, on one wall of the Boston Medical Center.
  33|             Award winning Native artist and 2023 NEA Heritage Fellow, Elizabeth James-Perry, was the second artist to work with
  34|             Lunenburg High School students in 2023. Her exquisite beading and wampum shell-carving jewelry, along with her ad-
  35|             vocacy for growing the plants that allows local pollinators and butterflies to thrive is deeply rooted in her heritage
  36|             where milkweed stems are still used in fine weaving. Her newest work is a Sea Turtle Mound garden created with Na-
  37|             tive students at Amhurst College.
  38|             The Lunenburg Cultural Council is looking forward to providing more programming, and increasing community involve-
  39|             ment in the arts in 2024.

===PAGE 65=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|                                                                   DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   2|             The Department of Public Works is comprised of the Highway, Cemetery, Sewer, and Vehicle Maintenance Divisions.
   3|             The Department maintains roads, bridges, sewer infrastructure, and provides services essential to the welfare and quality
   4|             of life for the citizens of Lunenburg. The staff consists of one Director, one Executive Assistant (shared with the Facili-
   5|             ties Department), one Highway Superintendent, 5 Heavy Equipment Operators, one Mechanic, one Cemetery Superin-
   6|             tendent, two Seasonal Cemetery Laborers, one Sewer Business Manager, and one Assistant to the Sewer Business Man-
   7|             ager.
   8|             Departmental Mission:
   9|             The mission of the Department of Public Works is to provide high quality, cost effective, efficient, and safe services to
  10|             protect, preserve and improve the Town's roadway infrastructure and related assets in a manner which meets and en-
  11|             hances the current and future social and economic needs of the community; to contribute to a healthy, safe and quality
  12|             environment for the Town's citizens and visitors; and to provide environmentally sound means of solid waste and
  13|             wastewater disposal.
  14|             Departmental Goals and Objectives
  15|             • Be prepared to respond to any weather, roadway infrastructure, or accident-related emergency that may arise on a
  16|                  twenty-four hour per day, three hundred and sixty-five day per year basis.
  17|             • Maintain and enhance a highly qualified and dedicated staff of public works professionals.
  18|             • Provide preventative maintenance in a proactive manner to reduce reactive maintenance as follows:
  19|                        • Maintain and update the sewer system and associated pumping stations.
  20|                        • Maintain and repair roadways in accordance with the 2022 pavement management plan.
  21|                        • Maintain and repair sidewalks in accordance with the 2020 PROW ADA Transition plan.
  22|                        • Increase the number of roadsides mowed by developing a roadside maintenance plan.
  23|                        • Increase the number of catch basin and stormwater structures cleaned and the number of miles of roadways
  24|                            swept per the June 2020 Operations and Maintenance Plan prepared by Comprehensive Environmental, Inc.
  25|                        • Establish a comprehensive evaluation of bridges and culverts, and prioritize by condition, use, and plans for
  26|                            rehabilitation.
  27|             • Increase training offered by our insurance provider to obtain credits to lessen premiums.
  28|             • Identify new sources (Federal, State, and other grants) of funding to supplement the operational budget.
  29|             • Continue to maintain a high level of courteous communication and productive interaction between members of
  30|                   boards and commissions as well as Town residents.
  31|             • Strive to modernize the operations of the Department by obtaining and using the most up to date equipment and
  32|                   methods, including asset management.
  33|             Highway Division
  34|             The highway division provided daily maintenance and repairs of roads, drainage, signs, trees, roadsides, sewers, and
  35|             more. They assisted all other Departments as requested and responded to emergencies 24 hours per day as needed. The
  36|             DPW Director reviewed and approved applications for permits for Road Openings, Street Improvements, Trench Safety,
  37|             Sewer Connections, and Sewer Extensions. He reviewed applications for driveway installations, site plans, stormwater
  38|             BMP's, building demolition, and sign installations for DPW related issues.
  39|                                                                                               65

===PAGE 66=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                                                                      DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   2|               Pavement Management
   3|               The Town first developed a Pavement Management Plan in 2009. This plan was updated in 2015 and defined a yearly
   4|                aving plan through Fiscal Year 2023 and included a $4,000,000 voter authorized debt exclusion. The goal of the plar
   5|                vas to achieve an overall town wide Road Surface Rating of 72-75 out of 100. In July of 2022 the DPW contracted witk
   6|               BETA Group, Inc to have them provide a new pavement management plan as the current plan was ending and the debt
   7|               exclusion funding had been exhausted. BETA Group surveyed our roads using LIDAR and reported an overall Road
   8|               Surface Rating of 84.75. The Project Manager for BETA Group commented "it is one of the highest scores I've seen in
   9|               New England over the past 12 years that I've been doing this". The DPW is proud to have successfully administered the
  10|               The following roads were paved with hot mix asphalt this year:
  11|                           Prospect Street                         West Townsend Road                     Upland Avenue                           Cross Street
  12|               Fog Seal was applied to the following roads this year:
  13|                             Beachview Road                       Cove Road                            Cove Terrace                         Crescent Road
  14|                              Crescent Terrace                     Horizon Island Road                  Island Road                          Peninsula Drive
  15|                             Sandy Cove Road                       South Row Road                       Wildwood Road                        Williams Drive
  16|               Fog seal is a type of pavement preservation treatment applied to an existing asphalt pavement surtace to preserve its
  17|               functional and structural integrity and delay a more costly rehabilitation treatment in the future. Fog seal penetrates up
  18|               to 3/8 inch into asphalt pavement surfaces, softening the asphalt binder as it goes. This improves cohesion and slows
  19|               cracking, reducing the loss of fines and aggregates. It is a simple, low-cost spray application which extends road life by
  20|               up to 3 years.
  21|               Storm Water
  22|               Staying in compliance with our EPAINPDES MS4 Permit continues to remain an important function of the DPW. Along
  23|               with the Storm Water Task Force, Land Use Director, and our Storm Water Consultant the DPW works to meet the obli-
  24|               gations of the permit and required reporting each year. The DPW provides maintenance and care of the Town's storm
  25|               water management systems. This includes, but is not limited to catch basin cleaning, street sweeping, pipe jetting, re-
  26|               placing pipes, and swale cleaning. The Department responds to calls for clogged catch basins and culverts to help con-
  27|               trol ponding and flooding. Storm water related issues continue to rise.
  28|               Tree Removal
  29|               A Tree Risk Management Inventory of almost 1,200 trees on the roads in Lunenburg was completed in 2015. The inven-
  30|               tory prioritizes the removal and/or pruning of the trees. However, storm damage and natural attrition creates a backlog
  31|               of tree work that exceeds the Department's allotted budget for tree removal. The Department strives to fulfill each resi-
  32|               dent's request for tree work but must prioritize work based upon the determined degree of danger to person and property
  33|               and location. The DPW continued to act in a reactionary manner as needs arose. All tree work other than brush remov-
  34|               al, chipping, and the removal of small diameter trees will continue to be outsourced due to the lack of a bucket truck or
  35|               crane and a licensed tree professional. Each spring and after storms, the DPW staff remove and dispose of woody debris
  36|               within the Town's right-of-way. An aggressive program by Unitil in recent years has lessened the burden of the Town
  37|               by removing those trees that may cause an interruption of electrical service to the community. The DPW appreciates and
  38|               thanks Unitil for their responsiveness in removing hazardous trees when asked to do so.
  39|               Roadside Vegetation Maintenance
  40|               The DP W staff responded to many requests to cut and clear overgrown vegetation from roadsides. This work was done
  41|               using lawn mowers, saws, and other hand tools as the existing roadside mower was disabled. A new utility tractor with
  42|               a boom rotary mower attachment (22-foot horizontal reach and 19.5-foot vertical reach) was ordered in September of
  43|               2022 and arrived in December of 2023. The DP W is developing a town-wide program of selective curbside mowing
  44|               and vegetation clearing using the new tractor and boom mower that will significantly enhance our roadside vegetation
  45|               maintenance program.
  46|                                                                                               66

===PAGE 67=== (ocr, 47 lines)
   1|                                                                   DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   2|             Landfill
   3|             The closed and capped landfill off Youngs Road is inspected by a consultant semiannually and remains in compliance
   4|             with the requirements of the Massachusetts Division of Environmental Protection (DEP). Gas monitoring and ground-
   5|             water contamination sampling and testing is conducted by a consultant. The landfill site including the perimeter fencing
   6|             is inspected on a regular schedule by DPW personnel.
   7|             Adjacent to the closed landfill is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings both determined to be haz-
   8|             ardous waste by the EPA. The DEP has issued a Beneficial Use Determination (BUD) permit which allows the storage
   9|             ot these materials. A consultant performs the yearly testing of these materials.
  10|             On seven Saturdays in the fall and the spring, DPW personnel oversee and manage Yard Waste Days. Residents may
  11|             drop off brush, leaves, and other organic materials for composting.
  12|             Solid Waste
  13|             Solid waste (trash) is collected curbside weekly under the Pay as You Throw (PAYT) program by our vendor E.L. Har-
  14|             vey & Sons, Inc. Recyclable materials are also collected curbside weekly by Harvey. Pay as You Throw bags may be
  15|             purchased at Hannaford Supermarket, Smokestack Roasters, Maki Building Center, Honey Farms, Jaxx Country Variety,
  16|             Lunenburg Gult, and Baker's Whalom Variety. Residents may call Harvey to arrange the pickup of bulky items for a
  17|             fee. The DPW remains the point of contact between residents and Harvey. Residents may dispose of household hazard-
  18|             ous wastes for a fee at the Devens Regional Household Hazardous Waste Collection Center on the published dates and
  19|             Lake Shirley Dam
  20|             In the early 1990's, with the structure in need of repair, The Lake Shirley Association turned over ownership of the dam
  21|             to the Town. After the completion of the total reconstruction of the dam, the responsibility for the maintenance, moni-
  22|             toring, and record keeping were completed by a small number of volunteers from the Lake Association. Compliance
  23|             with State dam regulations, the establishment of an operations manual as well as an emergency action plan was placed
  24|             under the umbrella of the Department of Public Works in 2004-2005. The dam is inspected by a professional engineer
  25|             licensed in Massachusetts. The most recent inspection resulted in a positive report, and we remain in compliance with
  26|             regulations.
  27|             Winter Operations
  28|             The DPW was prepared for and responded to all snow and ice weather events with the goal of providing a high level of
  29|             service in a timely manner while at the same time trying to minimize costs. Lunenburg joins with 17 other towns in a
  30|             cooperative bid to obtain the best possible pricing for rock salt. However, the price of rock salt continues to steadily in-
  31|             crease every year. The DPW maintains a small fleet of snow removal equipment (trucks, plows, spreaders, loader, back-
  32|             hoe, and sidewalk plow). The fleet is supplemented with vendors as needed. Due to breakdowns of the Town's aged
  33|             fleet of large dump trucks, more vendors were needed; thus, adding to costs. Four new snow fighter trucks (large trucks
  34|             with a salt spreader, front plow, and wing plow) have been ordered and are expected to be available in Fiscal Year 2025.
  35|             The clearing of snow and ice from roadways is a critical operation of the DPW as it is a matter of public safety.
  36|             The Town and its residents are fortunate to have a dedicated group of DPW personnel and private vendors who tirelessly
  37|             and successfully challenge the worst Nature has to offer regardless of the severity or duration of a storm. "DPW work-
  38|             ers are the original Storm Troopers. "
  39|             Cemetery
  40|             The maintenance of the Town's cemeteries was performed by the Cemetery Division Superintendent, and two seasonal
  41|             cemetery laborers. The DPW thanks the Massachusetts Department of Corrections for providing crews for the fall clean-
  42|             up. The Superintendent coordinates burials with families and funeral homes and meets regularly with the three-member
  43|             Cemetery Commission. The Highway Division provides equipment and staff for maintenance and burials, which on av-
  44|             erage is forty per year. The DPW thanks Beaulac Brothers Landscaping and Tree Service, Lakeview Landscaping, Dillis
  45|             and Roy Civil Design Group, Leominster Monument, Helen Balcom, Robert Ullo, and David Passios for their service to
  46|             the cemeteries this year.

   3|             The DPW is responsible for the day-to-day operation and maintenance of the wastewater collection system. A specialty

   5|             Sewer Business Manager, Barbara Lefebvre, retired. Thank you, Barbara, for your dedicated service to the Town of
   6|             Lunenburg. Paula Bertram, the Assistant Sewer Business Manager, was promoted to Sewer Business Manager. The
   7|             Sewer Business Manager manages the administrative functions of the Sewer Division including billing, customer ser-
   8|             vice, permitting and budgets. The Sewer Business Manager and DPW Director worked closely with the Sewer Commis-
   9|             sion to ensure sewer rules, regulations, and policies are followed and to assure the future long-term operation of the
  10|             wastewater collection system.
  11|             Fleet Maintenance
  12|             The DPW Mechanic is responsible for the preventative maintenance and repair of all Town vehicles. The vehicles in-
  13|             clude over 30 pieces of highway construction equipment, high performance police vehicles, Fire and Rescue first re-
  14|             sponder apparatus, mowers and more. Highly technical repairs and repairs that cannot be completed in-house are out-
  15|             sourced to specialty vendors as needed. A fleet maintenance software program continues to be a valuable tool as each
  16|             repair is logged by vehicle identification number and stored in a comprehensive database and required services, repair
  17|             costs over the life of the equipment, and part numbers are easily obtained.
