===PAGE 69=== (ocr, 50 lines)
   1|                                                                          FINANCE COMMITTEE REPORT
   2|                                                                                      Fiscal Year 2023
   3|             Annual Town Meeting is an important event for community members to come together and vote on warrant articles that
   4|             directly impact how we manage our town. Amongst these articles is the FY23 budget, capital plan, authorizations for
   5|             various enterprise and revolving funds, and appropriations relating to stabilization funds and reserves. Throughout the
   6|             year the Town Manager and department heads carefully review expenses and have thoughtful conversations about how
   7|             to best serve and manage the town. While developing the budget they considered funding, contracts, and mandates, as
   8|             well as what the community wants and needs to thrive. The Town Manager formulated a recommended budget, which
   9|             she presented to the Finance Committee on February 17th. Over six subsequent Finance Committee meetings, depart-
  10|             ment heads presented overviews and plans relating to their budgets and capital requests. A Public Hearing was held on
  11|             March 31st, as required by the Town Charter, and the Finance Committee voted on recommendations pertaining to fi-
  12|             nancially relevant articles.
  13|             Armed with all the information gathered during this process, the Finance Committee recommends approval of the Town
  14|             Manager's omnibus budget, as outlined in this warrant.
  15|             Overall, our financial condition is good. Revenues are stable, aided by strong collections, increases in state aid, new
  16|             growth, and a variety of federal and state grants that the town has secured. On the expense side, while not meeting every
  17|             need outlined by the department heads, this budget does begin to address several emerging and previously unmet needs.
  18|             The total in requested expenditures is $49,953,531.76. While we are pleased with our current fiscal standing, we do face
  19|             challenges ahead; especially with regard to a number of our municipal buildings, which we outline in more detail below.
  20|             Notably, our certified free cash this year is $2,666,962, which is substantially higher than in previous years, a temporary
  21|             benefit resulting from appropriations that were not fully expended in FY21 as a result of COVID-related restrictions and
  22|             impacts. Town fiscal policy directs portions of free cash to accounts such as stabilization, the OPEB allocation (a fund to
  23|             pay for future retiree benefits), and the capital plan.
  24|             Accordingly, this year's recommended capital plan (funded by free cash, tax revenues, and unspent capital appropria-
  25|             tions from previous years) is $2,162,849. It is an unprecedented investment of available funds (without incurring any
  26|             additional debt) into the capital infrastructure that supports our town. As of the printing of this warrant, the Finance
  27|             Committee is seeking to answer a few specific questions pertaining to the capital plan. We will have a recommendation
  28|             on the capital plan, Article 6, at Town Meeting on May 7.
  29|             Other notable items:
  30|             • For FY23, the proposed appropriation for education is $23,397,371, 56.2%% of the Town's total budget, and a
  31|                  3.78% increase over last year. This includes our Monty Tech assessment at $1,054,376, which is a 20.4% increase
  32|                  for FY23, mainly due to an increase in enrollment from 83 to 94 students. Additionally, the town budget includes
  33|                  $1,207,421 in School Retiree Health Insurance. Fortunately, this year the town benefited from an $898,610 increase
  34|                  in Chapter 70 aid, which more than offsets the $673,617 increase in the Lunenburg School District budget.
  35|             While our Chapter 70 increase was welcome news, the increase was partly a result of formula changes that recognized
  36|             more low-income students at a higher tier of funding. Our vulnerable populations are of top concern for all of our depart-
  37|             ment leaders.
  38|              An area of concern for the Finance Committee are several personnel positions that the School District is funding through
  39|             ESSER 2 and ESSER 3 federal grants. These grants, totaling $1,939,868 will expire in FY24. Many of these positions
  40|             are addressing acute health, academic, and social/emotional needs that have arisen from COVID-19. Over the next two
  41|             years the School Committee will need to determine what positions we need to maintain, and how to fund them.,
  42|             • This year's budget supports an increase of $53,443 for the library budget to support the addition of two part-time
  43|                  personnel and additional expenses. This increase will enable the library to extend its operating hours to include open-
  44|                  ing on Fridays.
  45|             • This year's budget continues to support long-term staffing and investment programs that we committed to in previ-
  46|             ous years, including an additional full-time firefighter and police officer. We are again increasing road pavement man-
  47|             agement funding by $50,000. We are also contributing $450,000 to the special-purpose stabilization fund, which should
  48|             give us the option to pay cash for ambulance and fire truck replacements in coming years, as opposed to borrowing or
  49|             using free cash.
  50|                                                                                               69

===PAGE 70=== (ocr, 49 lines)
   1|             • This year's budget includes a 4.71% increase in IT spending, for a total of $316,022.89. In addition, the Lunenburg
   2|                   School District has budgeted $330,514 for technology-related administration and educational expenses. In late
   3|                  FY21, the town began the process of transferring the function of IT strategy and leadership to a private firm, which
   4|                   works in close consultation with town and school department leadership. Following a comprehensive review, an up-
   5|                   grade of networking and cloud services has commenced, which will continue into FY23. Additionally, the town is
   6|                   instituting rotational upgrades of computers and peripherals throughout our operations. The town website will also
   7|                   get regular upgrades. These programs are drastically improving the quality and reliability of our IT infrastructure and
   8|                   improving our security posture. The Finance Committee was pleased with the 'out-of-the-box' thinking and interde-
   9|                   partmental cooperation. We have encouraged all departments to find similar synergies and opportunities for collabo-
  10|                   ration and cost sharing.
  11|             • As we have highlighted in previous years, Lunenburg's debt ratio has been identified by an independent credit-rating
  12|                   agency as the sole reason that we are unable to achieve the highest-available bond rating. The good news is that our
  13|                  total debt and our debt service are declining. Our Excludable Debt Service to Levy Ratio is 9.18%, nearly 2% below
  14|                  the 11% cap. Our Net Regular Debt Service to Levy Ratio is 1.22% in FY 23, well-below the 4% cap in our fiscal
  15|                   policy. Our debt service will continue to decline over the next two years as bonds for the Public Safety Building,
  16|                  Primary School, and Library come off the debt schedule. However, this bit of good news could be temporary. There
  17|                  are several projects currently under discussion, which if acted upon and funded by borrowing, could exceed our debt
  18|                  limits in coming years. The Municipal Building Design Committee will likely have recommendations in coming
  19|                  months pertaining to Town Hall, Ritter, and the TC Passios building. The Select Board is considering re-
  20|                  development of the old Primary School property at 30 School Street. The Parks Commission is formulating a master
  21|                  plan for Marshall Park and is investigating issues with the Town Beach building. And the Lunenburg School District
  22|                   anticipates a renovation or replacement of Turkey Hill Elementary School within the next ten years.
  23|             • The warrant includes two articles that, if approved as presented, will make progress in addressing the old Primary
  24|                   School property at 30 School Street. Article 6, the capital plan, appropriates $371,000 for hazardous materials abate-
  25|                  ment. Article 19 appropriates $450,000 for the demolition of the building and engineering plans for the property's
  26|                  redevelopment into a municipal park.
  27|               During FY22, the town received $3,507,971 from the federal government resulting from The American Rescue Plan
  28|                   Act (ARPA). These funds are not accounted for in the FY23 budget. Recent guidance from the U.S. Treasury gives
  29|                  municipal executives (in our case the Select Board) fairly wide latitude for appropriating these funds. The Select
  30|                   Board will soon be gathering opinions and ideas pertaining to how we should use these funds. Potentially, some of
  31|                   this money could be used to offset costs relating to some of the projects mentioned above.
  32|             Lunenburg's overall good fiscal condition is the result of adherence to conservative fiscal policy recommended by the
  33|             Town Manager, enacted by the Select Board, and enabled by Town Meeting appropriations. There is no doubt that we
  34|             will face new challenges in the coming years. In addition to some of the concerns outlined above, inflation is a looming
  35|             headwind that will likely drive-up labor, capital, and materials costs. We believe that the investments we are making to-
  36|             day will give us greater flexibility and capacity to address future challenges.
  37|             The Finance Committee thanks Town Manager Heather Lemieux and Finance Director, Karen Brochu for their tireless
  38|             dedication and leadership throughout the year, and especially during budget season. We also thank our department heads
  39|             and all of our municipal and school employees for their exemplary service to the citizens of Lunenburg and stewardship
  40|             of our town.

   1|                                                        Lunenburg Public Library
   2|             "To build an effective community, everyone must realize that their community is bigger
   3|             than them."                                                                                                      — Idowu Koyenikan
   4|             On most afternoons, you can enter our Lunenburg Public Library and see dozens of students, families, adults,
   5|             and retirees enjoying the welcoming atmosphere. A library is an ideal location to witness and participate in
   6|             connection. Libraries house myriad ideas in its books, movies, and music. Library events entertain, educate,
   7|             and inspire. Leading thousands of active community members to come and go through the doors.
   8|             More than 65,000 visitors came to the library during Fiscal Year 2023. From the vantage of our entryway it is
   9|             a convenient inference for visitors to realize why the library is so successful at its mission:
  10|                        Lunenburg Public Library welcomes all and serves residents of the Lunenburg area as a hub for the community, enriching
  11|                        our lives through its collections, programs, services, facility, and people.
  12|             The friendly face of the librarian standing at our front desk greets all who enter. That welcoming stance stands
  13|             as analog for the efforts of both the staff and the library trustees to build a safe, inclusive, and welcoming envi-
  14|             ronment here at 1023 Massachusetts Avenue. Those efforts have succeeded in centering the library in the
  15|             hearts and lives of so many residents.
  16|             One important step in this effort during FY23 was the addition of two part-time positions. Those two positions
  17|             enabled the library, in September of 2022, to open between 2 and 6pm on Fridays for the first time. Almost
  18|             immediately, this time became some of the most popular hours to visit the library.
  19|             Friday has also become a very popular time for families to visit the library, too. Afternoon and evening hours
  20|             seem very conducive to working adults.
  21|             Most Fridays, you will find dozens of teens at the library, perhaps making more noise than you would hope.
  22|             There is no homework help on Fridays, yet these students come to the library. Some come to work on their
  23|             studies, others to play video games, borrow materials, or await a ride. Others bring snacks or whole meals, and
  24|             some volunteer to help library staff as they prepare for programs. A crowd also arrives in the evening to attend
  25|             monthly video game night.
  26|             Our Pocket Pollinator Garden near the front entrance has also brightened the library grounds with blooms, in-
  27|             sect activity, and even more educational opportunities.. Thanks to many, many volunteers, especially the lead-
  28|             ing forces of Robin Venezia and Lydia Henshaw, this garden will offer blooms from Spring to Fall and support
  29|             the many native species vital to our local ecosystem. The garden layout was created following ecological de-
  30|             sign principles, and throughout the process the library offered programs which enable attendees to learn those
  31|             concepts and then utilize those same principles at home. During April, as part of the Parks Department's cele-
  32|             bration of Earth Day, we offered volunteer opportunities, so that individuals could learn and practice the skills
  33|             and concepts needed to repeat the process on their own, while they helped put in the garden.
  34|             Volunteers also helped put in three new trees on library property. One Tulip Tree will provide shade near our
  35|             patio area, and two American Redbud trees were planted on either side of the front entrance to help support
  36|             native species in memory of John Henshaw, longtime library supporter.
  37|                                                                                               71

===PAGE 72=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                                                        Lunenburg Public Library
   2|             In addition to the new gardens at the front of the library, we have also begun the process of reinvigorating the
   3|             Proctor Memorial Garden in the rear of the library. This space has been so important over the years, bringing a
   4|             meditative, calm sitting area. During the years after the pandemic, we began hosting more programs in the pa-
   5|             tio area to help encourage foot traffic to this space. The patio is particularly supportive of storytime events, and
   6|             when the weather cooperates, all involved enjoy using this outside space for library programs.
   7|             As a final highlight of our FY23, the library has achieved the award of a nearly $13,000 Library Services and
   8|             Technology Act Grant to support STEM learning in the community. This grant is a high point in the recent
   9|             stewardship of the library trustees and their vision of the library as a community hub. The strategic planning
  10|             process led to eligibility for this grant, and after the staff created an innovative approach to deliver STEM edu-
  11|             cation to all ages, the funding arrived during FY23. The library has initiated regular STEM programming for
  12|             children, trained staff to incorporate more STEM learning opportunities in their regular programming, and
  13|             constructed a number of innovative and fun circulating kits to help teach STEM concepts at home and in class-
  14|             rooms across the community. The final few months of the grant stretch into FY24 and promise to deliver some
  15|             of the most exciting and engaging programs funded by the grant.

  10|             • In response to local readers' needs, we added a new book format to our shelves, a product called "Vox

  12|                  while they read the book. Our current collection of 60 books has already been borrowed more than 350

   1|             LUNENBURG PUBLIC ACCESS (PAC)
   2|                                 Mission: Establish, develop, modernize, and operate the Town of Lunenburg's
   3|               FY23              Public, Government, and Educational (PEG) Access Channels to reflect Lunen-
   4|                                burg
   5|             Public Access Cable Committee (PACC)
   6|             Steve Walker, Chair
   7|             Faith Beall, Vice Chair
   8|             Steve Archambault, Secretary
   9|             Pete Lincoln, Member
  10|             Paul Sarapas, Member
  11|             The Lunenburg Public Access Cable Committee (PACC) is responsible for the long-term plan to manage the
  12|             Public Access Channels available to the Lunenburg community through Comcast, the town's only cable-
  13|             service provider.
  14|             The overall strategy includes providing and improving equipment to acquire, record, broadcast and re-
  15|             broadcast programming. Original content is also conceived and produced by PAC. Town events/meetings
  16|             and school activities, as well as outside sources of programming, are all provided to increase the viewer's
  17|             knowledge of what is happening around town.
  18|             Programming
  19|             CH8 - Government
  20|             Most content on the Government Channel (Ch8) is locally broadcasted meetings. In addition to several live
  21|             weekly municipal meetings and re-broadcasts, other government programs downloaded from MassAccess
  22|             MMX (massaccess.telvue.com), include Massachusetts State Agency PSAs and programs of a political nature.

   1|             ARPA FUNDS
   2|             In FY23, the Town of Lunenburg received ARPA funding. PAC presented an outline of potential spending to
   3|             the Select Board, to be awarded a portion of the available funding. The PAC request was in three parts: Vid-
   4|             eographer, Broadcast and Studio. The Videographer section included the purchase of products such as au-
   5|             dio recorders and portable lighting to better equip the videographers. Improvement of the Bilotta room
   6|             audio was the intent of the Broadcast section with a focus on new hall speakers. And, the Studio part tar-
   7|             geted significant improvements to the current studio, including audio and broadcast equipment, as well as
   8|             set furniture. PAC's proposal for ARA funding was approved for over $50,000, the full amount requested.
   9|             Studio and Equipment
  10|             PAC has had studio space in the TC Passios school since 2013. We encourage the public to take advantage
  11|             of the facilities and equipment offered for creative productions. The PAC manager also supports various
  12|             town departments in the use of public access' resources for their communication needs.
  13|             In FY23 PAC made an investment in 3 new MACBook Pros and editing software totaling almost $15,000.
  14|             There was a need to replace the old laptops, as they were obsolete. With the addition of new computers
  15|             videographers could efficiently produce quality content in the studio. Two HP laptops were also purchased
  16|             for administrative and production use.
  17|             New camera transmitters were also bought to increase camera usage for multi-camera productions. PAC
  18|             already had 2 available transmitters, however the demand for higher quality content necessitated the need
  19|             for more cameras. These transmitters have proven useful in the LHS auditorium for large Town Meetings,
  20|             where PAC uses 4 cameras to record. Two of the 4 transmitters are used for the inputs that broadcast the
  21|             presentations and voting results.
  22|             A large air conditioning unit was purchased and installed in the studio to keep equipment and work envi-
  23|             ronment cool during high temperature conditions.
  24|             Staff
  25|             In FY23, there was one full-time Manager, 5 part-time videographer employees and a part-time Bulletin Ad-
  26|             ministrator. There were openings for 2 part-time videographers and a Production Technician.

  12|             team relies on the Production Technician to oversee and assist with troubleshooting issues that arise. He al-

===PAGE 81=== (ocr, 33 lines)
   1|             Summary
   2|              FY23 was an outstanding year for PAC. Both local programming and the overall quantity of programs in-
   3|              creased. The temporary addition of a videographer helped to boost content, while we continued to im-
   4|              prove and grow current offerings. PAC services to the community improved through the addition govern-
   5|              ment zoom meeting coverage and improvements in video production through updated editing equipment.
   6|              Staffing was below current needs, as PAC searched to fill 2 videographer and a Production Technician posi-
   7|              tion. There was no progress in finding the much-needed videographers. However, a Production Technician
   8|             was hired at the end of FY23. With the new Production Technician, videographers were given both the
   9|              technical and on-hand support they required. Fortunately, even with limited staff resources, the current
  10|              videographers continued to step up to fill in the gaps. They covered numerous meetings and events includ-
  11|             ing more LHS sports. In fact, sport coverage was a leading category on Channel 9 with 24 games in FY23, an
  12|             increase of 242% over FY22. PAC has kept up with the demand for recording despite the level of staffing.
  13|             With the introduction of ARPA, PAC was able to prioritize and map out how best to use available funding
  14|             for FY24. With the approval of the PAC Committee, the focus was in 3 parts; to equip the Studio, Videogra-
  15|              phers and Town Hall. A plan was devised at the end of FY23 to best fulfill equipment needs in each catego-
  16|              ry.
  17|              Overall, PAC experienced tremendous growth in FY23. From additional affiliates on MMX downloading PAC
  18|              program content, to a marked increase in total programs offered. A new show, "Movie Talk," was added,
  19|              as well as many new downloaded programs from other sources. The government Channel 8 has seen a
  20|              spike in state programs and additional Zoom meetings.
  21|              PAC produced local programs on Channel 9 ran the gamut from town events to department PSAs. There is
  22|              programming content for everyone in the family. PAC will continue to increase programming as new vide-
  23|              ographers are added, and new producers come forward. PAC is always looking for new producers to utilize
  24|             the outstanding staff and studio.
  25|             Won't you join us as a producer or volunteer? PAC's supportive staff
  26|              offers production assistance, making it easy to produce your own show. Our community is thriving with tal-
  27|              ented and creative individuals - now is your time to shine! If producing doesn't appeal to you, then consid-
  28|              er "behind the camera" options. Become a volunteer. Volunteers are the "life blood" of media centers
  29|              around the state, and without that involvement PAC will not be able to grow. Consider volunteering to
  30|              help provide the necessary resources needed for PAC's continued success.
  31|              We need you!
  32|             Jo Toale, Manager jtoale@lunenburgma.gov 617-763-3018

===PAGE 82=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                                                                  SEWER COMMISSION - FY23
   2|             The Sewer Commission bears the responsibility of overseeing the Sewer Enterprise Fund, planning for the future, and
   3|             developing and implementing rules, regulations, and policies. Under the Town's Charter, responsibility for the day-to-
   4|             day operation, care and maintenance of the sanitary sewers are under the supervision of the Town Manager who dele-
   5|             gates the responsibility to manage and maintain the operational side of the system to the Department of Public Works'
   6|             Director. The Town of Lunenburg does not operate a sewer treatment plant. Approximately 87% of Lunenburg sewer is
   7|             discharged to the City of Leominster, and the other 13% is discharged to the City of Fitchburg.
   8|             In FY23, the following members served on the Sewer Commission: John Reynolds-Chair, Brett Ramsden-Vice Chair,.
   9|             Mike Deveikis-Clerk and Members Dave MacDonald and Mike Nault. The Commission continued to meet virtually via
  10|             Zoom for all meetings.
  11|             There are currently 1212 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 284 which have access to Town
  12|             Sewer but are not yet connected. During FY23, 6 new accounts connected to Town Sewer. The Commission billed out
  13|             $1,058,041 in quarterly sewer bills and had $33,257 of prior year delinquencies added to the owners' property tax bills
  14|             for non-payment.
  15|             In FY23, Sewer Business Manager Barb Lefebvre announced her retirement after 15 years of service in the Sewer De-
  16|             partment and 6 years in the Assessor's office. The Commission thanks Barb for her dedicated work in running the office,
  17|             budgeting, and giving guidance to the Commissioners throughout the years. Assistant manager, Paula Bertram, took over
  18|             as Sewer Business Manager in March 2023.
  19|              During FY23, CTE (Chad Empey) continued to provide excellent service for the Operation & Maintenance of the Sewer
  20|             Stations and completed the 3rd            year of their contract. The Commission approved funds for upgrades to the pump sta-
  21|             tions, to replace cranes, block heaters, dialers, seals, and replacement pumps. These upgrades are important to keep the
  22|             stations running smoothly. A Request for Proposals also went out for a new O&M contract for the pump stations.
  23|             DP W Director, Bill Bernard and Paula Bertram, Business Manager, worked with the Commission to carry out the
  24|             Maintenance Plan for the pump stations, setting up jetting for 20% of the lines each year and overseeing the operation
  25|             and maintenance of the pump stations. The Commission worked on a proposal for possible ARPA funds of $300K for
  26|             upgrades to the pump stations and $200K for repairs to 84 manholes. Also, a similar proposal was sent for state earmark
  27|             funds.
  28|             A proposed 40B project on Howard Street, seeking expansion of sewer outside of the Sewer Service Area, was discussed
  29|             multiple times during FY23. An extensive list of concerns was sent to the ZBA, stating the estimated costs and impacts
  30|             to the system if the project proceeds. The Commission also met several times with the developer, Kevin O'Brien, and
  31|             maintained that the expansion of the Sewer Service Area requires a vote by residents at a Town Meeting.
  32|             The Commission continued the annual permit program for the 16 Food Service Establishments with grease traps and in-
  33|             terceptors on Town Sewer. FY23 Annual Permit renewals will have no fee for establishments in compliance. Those who
  34|             are not in compliance will pay $350 for internal traps and $650 for external trap renewals.
  35|             The "Rules & Regulations for Oil & Grease Traps/Interceptors for Food Establishments" continues to be a focus of the
  36|             Commission as Fats, Oils and Grease (FOG) can negatively affect the smooth operation and maintenance costs of the
  37|             sewer system. While the Grease Trap Regulation focuses on commercial and public disposal, individual homeowners
  38|             can also contribute a significant amount of grease into the system which increases everyone's costs. Whether on sewer or
  39|             septic, grease should not be put down the drain.
  40|                                                                                               82

===PAGE 84=== (ocr, 28 lines)
   1|                   TOWN OF
   2|             Veterans Services Office Annual Report 2023 The Commonwealth of Massachusetts has the most comprehen-
   3|             sive system of assistance offered to Veterans, their families and surviving spouses in all of the United States.
   4|             Every city or town in Massachusetts, in accordance with chapter I15 of the Massachusetts General Laws, must
   5|             have available to its citizens a Veterans' Services Officer (VSO), either full- or part-time, as determined by the
   6|             community population, in order to serve the needs of its Veterans. Lunenburg has a part-time VSO at the Ritter
   7|             Building at 960 Massachusetts Ave. The Office hours are Thursdays from 8:00 AM thru 1:00 PM.
   8|             Veterans, families, or widows who qualify can be provided monthly financial assistance (Chapter 115 Bene-
   9|             fits), which are reimbursements for specific living expenses such as heating fuel, medical care, and pharma-
  10|             ceutical co-payments. With the VSO's approval, the Town of Lunenburg provides the financial assistance, but
  11|             then the town is reimbursed by the state's Department of Veterans' Services (DVS). It is the function of the
  12|             Veterans' Services Office to identify qualifying members of the community and assist with enrollment into the
  13|             DVS program. Additionally, certain Veterans and/or widows may also be eligible for federal benefits from the
  14|             Veterans Administration (VA). Only Massachusetts offers both of these services to every community.
  15|             During Fiscal Year 2023 (July 2022- June 2023), Lunenburg is serving and average of 10 recipients (Veterans,
  16|             Families, or Widows) per month on Chapter 115. Lunenburg paid out $71,041.50 in benefits, or an average of'
  17|             $5,920.13 per recipient per month. Lunenburg was reimbursed $53,281.16 by DVS, which is typically 75% of
  18|             what was given as assistance, the maximum allowable share. There are a couple of services that the Town is
  19|             reimbursed 100%
  20|             Some examples of Veterans' Service Office assistance services include:
  21|            • Enrollment in chapter 115, State Veterans benefits
  22|            • Assistance obtaining military service records or replacement medals/decorations
  23|             • Tuition waivers at Massachusetts state colleges
  24|             • Housing assistance, including Veterans Shelters and Veterans Homes
  25|             • Payments of "Welcome Home" bonuses for deployed Veterans
  26|             • Assistance obtaining certain Massachusetts automobile and motorcycle license plates
  27|             • Employment assistance
  28|                                                                                               84

===PAGE 85=== (ocr, 13 lines)
   1|             The Veterans' Service Office also provides information, advice, and assistance to any citizen interested in
   2|             learning about their qualifications for federal benefits, such as:
   3|             • VA disability payments and property tax exemptions
   4|               Tax-exempt annuities for parents or spouses of those killed in Action or those who are declared 100% disa-
   5|                   bled from service-connected conditions.
   6|             • VA pensions, Aid and Attendance assistance, and death benefits
   7|             • VA medical care for both physical and mental needs
   8|             • Vocational and Educational opportunities (the GI Bill)
   9|             • VA Loans
  10|             • Burial Assistance and Grave Markers (Veterans or Civilian cemeteries)
  11|             Michelle Durkee
  12|             Veteran's Service Agent

   1|                                                                           BOARD OF HEALTH
   2|             The Board of Health Office is located in the Ritter Building on the corner of Mass. Ave and Lancaster Ave. Our office is
   3|             on the upper level at the end of the hall on the right. We can be reached at 978-345-4146, extension 430.
   4|             The Board of Health, with the assistance of its Agent, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health, continued its mission to
   5|             maintain its focus on public health and safety issues during 2022, reviewing Title V Inspection Reports, building per-
   6|             mits, septic and well permits, nuisance investigations, housing inspections, complaints and general public health related
   7|             issues as well as resident concerns.
   8|             In addition, our local Board continued to be tasked with responding to the emerging COVID-19 pandemic. The Board of
   9|             Health assisted residents and businesses in understanding ever-changing guidance and compliance with those orders. We
  10|             continued to conducted compliance checks and responded to complaints. As the pandemic continued, our office assisted
  11|             residents and businesses in understanding testing, quarantine and necessary compliance. Through Nashoba Associated
  12|             Boards of Health, regional Covid Vaccine Clinics were held. Wash hands frequently, wear a mask, and keep six feet rule.
  13|             Homeland Security and Emergency Preparedness planning is ongoing. Meetings continue between our agents, other
  14|             Town Departments and regional entities to develop a plan for not only Lunenburg but with our partner towns in our re-
  15|             gion.
  16|             This year, we processed applications for Septic Permit Lot Tests, permits for new construction, new wells, repairs or up-
  17|             grades to septic systems, well permits and food service permits. The Board continues its vigilance with regard to septic
  18|             systems, wells, food service inspections, housing and nuisance complaints and its general mandate to protect the envi-
  19|             ronment and groundwater of the Commonwealth.
  20|             The Board of Health provides numerous other services in cooperation with our Health Agents, the Nashoba Associated
  21|             Boards of Health. In addition to our environmental services, nursing services is provided to our resident. This year, clin-
  22|             ics provided our residents with inoculations for Flu as well as Senior well adult clinics.
  23|             The Town of Lunenburg participates in the Central Mass. Mosquito Control Project. Mosquito Control monitors the
  24|             mosquito population in Lunenburg and advises of any potential diseases such as EEE and West Nile Virus and sprays
  25|             frequently to control the population during the warmer times of the year. Residents who want to have their property
  26|             With our Nashoba Nursing and Hospice partners we provide in home visits to ill residents recovering from illness and
  27|             Hospice care and counseling to those in need of this service.
  28|             Please contact our office in the Ritter Building for more information.
  29|             Please remember that our Board Members volunteer their time and efforts on behalf of you, the Citizens of Lunenburg.
  30|             If you have something that you think we can help you with, we are at your service. Contact the Board office at 582-4146
  31|             ext. 430.

===PAGE 89=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|             Food Service Licensing and Inspections
   2|                                                          39
   3|             This office licenses, inspects, responds to complaints and conducts follow up on concerns in licensed operations. Some of the
   4|             inspections done this year were in response to COVID complaints.
   5|             Recreational Permitting and Inspections
   6|                                                          54
   7|             This office licenses, inspects and responds to complaints regarding recreational camps for children, bathing, beaching and
   8|             public and semi-pools. We sample bathing beaches weekly, providing the results to community partners to ensure swim-
   9|             ming only occurs at the public and semi-public beach which meet State regulations.
  10|             Housing & Nuisance Investigation
  11|                                                                                 17
  12|             This office inspects dwelling units for compliance with the State Sanitary Code upon compliant and prior to occupancy. We
  13|             issue orders to affect corrective actions, re-inspect and initiate legal action if necessary. Similarly, complaints from residents
  14|             concerning unsanitary conditions or pollution are investigated.
  15|             Title 5 related work - On-site Sewage Disposal
  16|             We received 50 applications for soil testing (testing necessary for the design of a sewage disposal systems) and wit-
  17|             nessed 147 tests performed (perc tests and soil evaluation holes).
  18|             We received 54 applications for sewage disposal systems and reviewed 91 plans for new and replacement systems.
  19|             Our office prepared 58 sewage disposal system permits for the Board's review and approval: 32 for new construction
  20|             and 26 for the replacement of failed systems.
  21|             We conducted 150 inspections during the construction of sewage disposal systems.
  22|             We reviewed 78 Title 5 inspection reports and we were available to provide consultation to your residents on matter re-
  23|             lating to sewage disposal systems.
  24|             Private Well related services
  25|             Private wells are regulated by local regulation and we issued 20 well permits for new and replacement private water supply
  26|             wells.
  27|             We review water quality and quantity reports and interpret the results for residents.
  28|             Rabies Clinics - Animals Immunized (Due to COVID-19, clinics were limited in 2022)
  29|             Nashoba Nursing Service & Hospice
  30|             Nashoba's Certified Home Health Registered Nurses visit and provide skilled services to patients in their home under physi-
  31|             cian's orders. These services include assessment of physical, psychological, and nutritional needs. Skills include teaching
  32|             supervision of medications, wound care, ostomy care, IV therapy on a 24-hour basis, catheter care, case management and
  33|             referral to other services as needed.
  34|             Nashoba's Certified Home Care Aides provide assistance with daily activities of daily living including bathing dressing, ex-
  35|             ercises and meal preparation.
  36|             Nashoba Therapists provide skilled physical, occupational, speech, and dietary therapeutic interventions through assessment,
  37|             treatment and education. Their integration of client, caregiver, and medical outcomes aims at attaining maximum functional
  38|             dependence.
  39|             Nursing Visits

  44|             Nashoba's Community Health Nursing program provides an essential public health service to its member communities
  45|             guided by the American Nurses Association (ANA) 16 Standards of Public Nursing Practice.

  22|             In addition to the mandated disease surveillance and reporting, our staff serves as a resource to the community, address-
  23|             ing questions and concerns from the public on a variety of health and safety issues (immunizations, head lice, chronic
  24|             disease management, safe drug sharps disposal etc.) in a variety of ways including:
  25|                 Monthly well adult clinics have been & continue to be offered throughout the district. Nashoba nursing staff con-
  26|                   ducted 159 total clinics this year, including 10 clinics in Lunenburg. Clinics provide the opportunity for residents to
  27|                   meet with the nurse for blood pressure checks, blood sugar screening, general health counselling, resource infor-
  28|                  mation and referrals as needed. Districtwide flu clinics are held in the fall.
  29|             • Nashoba conducted seasonal flu clinics in each community to make vaccinations available to everyone, including
  30|                  homebound residents. We administered 2130 flu shots to community members, including 98 Lunenburg residents.
  31|             • We conducted regionalized COVID-19 vaccine clinics the first quarter of the year until the public demand/interest in
  32|                  the vaccine waned. In an effort to increase vaccine accessibility, clinics were also held at local food pantries, shel-
  33|                  ters, churches, schools, businesses, etc. A total of 509 district community members were vaccinated for COVID-19
  34|                   in the 2022 calendar year.
  35|             • The nurses are available for & have made home visits to make general assessments, assist with coordinating care and
  36|                   services, provide support/teaching and make referrals to other local community resources.
  37|             • Nashoba has offered community outreach and educational programs covering topics such as disease prevention,
  38|                  chronic disease management, emergency preparedness, mental health awareness, and overall wellness.
  39|             • Nashoba staff attend and regularly participate in collaborative meetings, trainings and conferences to keep current
  40|                   with the latest developments in local public health.

  42|                   gional communication, and foster working relationships. This has helped to identify common goals and guide future
  43|                   public health initiatives and outreach.

===PAGE 91=== (ocr, 27 lines)
   1|             Community Health- Communications and Public Health Education Summary
   2|             The Public Health Educator/Communications Specialist (PHE/CS) role has evolved since its inception in Sept 2021 to
   3|             best support the needs of the District. In the past year, the PHE/CS has been communicating timely and relevant public
   4|             health information to towns and stakeholders, and providing educational opportunities across the District with a social
   5|             determinants of health (SDOH) lens. This work has included:
   6|             • Developing educational programs and presentations both independently and collaboratively with the PH nursing
   7|                   team- Harm reduction, emergency preparedness, etc.
   8|             • Providing education and advocacy related to treatment and de-stigmatization of substance use disorder
   9|             • Maintaining up-to-date distribution lists, communicating within the district via channels such as quarterly newslet-
  10|                  ters, regular email updates, etc. pertaining to public health news, information and events such as flu and rabies clin-
  11|                  ics. Exploring ways to diversify communication methods to reach as many community members as possible.
  12|             • Building and maintaining relationships with stakeholders through meetings and attending community events to rep-
  13|                  resent NABH
  14|             • Serving as a resource for community members with public health related questions across a broad range of topics;
  15|                  developing a robust, in-depth Resources page for the website (which is currently being finalized)
  16|             • Attending trainings with an emphasis on health equity and public health service regionalization. MDPH has priori-
  17|                  tized these areas because research has shown they have the biggest impact on health outcomes for the broadest range
  18|                  of people.
  19|             • PHE/CS has utilized an SDOH lens to identify health inequities that most impact the NABH region to focus efforts
  20|                   and collaborate with community partners to lessen these gaps.
  21|             • Developing educational and communications materials for district and individual towns by request including print,
  22|                   electronic, presentation, and TV/news media materials
  23|             • Maintaining and improving upon NABH website to keep info relevant and accessible. Maintaining agency social
  24|                  media to reach more people in district with current, relevant and seasonal public health information.
  25|             • Organizing events to facilitate the exchange of information such as a Spring Wellness Festival and first regional in
  26|                  formal local board of health gathering

===PAGE 92=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                                                                           FIRE DEPARTMENT
   2|              The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are dedicated to providing the highest quality,
   3|                                     most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to the citizens of the town of Lunenburg.
   4|                        In 2023, your Fire Department responded to 2033 calls for service, broken down as follows:
   5|                        Fires, Alarms & Hazardous Conditions                     356                   Emergency Medical            1193
   6|                        Inspections                                             366                    Other                         18
   7|                        This represents a 7% increase in emergency calls for service over 2023, mostly in Emergency Medical calls.
   8|             The Department consists of 10 Career and 30-35 On Call/Per Diem Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians, plus a
   9|             full time Administrative Assistant/EMS Coordinator. Three Firefighter/EMT or Paramedics are on duty 24/7 at the Station.
  10|             This coverage is provided by a combination of our Career Staff and Per Diem Firefighters. Staffing the Station with dual
  11|             trained Fire/EMS personnel ensures that we are ready to respond to whatever emergency occurs. As many calls require
  12|             more personnel than are on duty, we continue to rely heavily on the dedication and commitment of our On Call Firefighters
  13|             and Emergency Medical Technicians.
  14|             After five years of preparation, the Department Ambulances began operating at the Paramedic Level on January 1,
  15|             2023. This allows us to provide advanced lifesaving care at the local level, and no longer rely on other agencies
  16|             for assistance. The Department now has six Full Time and seven On Call/Per diem Firefighter/Paramedics. Para-
  17|             medic training is more than two thousand hours and requires anywhere from 9-18 months of college level class-
  18|             room education, clinical time, and field internships to obtain certification. The Department continues to train our
  19|             newly hired full-time staff to this level with Firefighter Jennie Smith completing her training in September. Achiev-
  20|             ing Paramedic Level service, along with 24/7 coverage in the Station are the most significant advancements to the
  21|             Department in the last 50 years,
  22|             Lieutenant Christos Lekaditis and Firefighter Carl Gamberdella make up the Department's Fire Prevention Divi-
  23|             sion and in 2023 conducted 366 Inspections. This is in addition to multiple fire drills and public education initia-
  24|             tives. By law, we are required to inspect Smoke and Carbon Monoxide Detector installations when a home is sold.
  25|             We are also required to inspect all new oil burners and propane gas installations. Any business holding a liquor
  26|             license also requires a yearly inspection for fire safety issues. In addition, all new construction projects must
  27|             have their plans reviewed for proper fire protection, as well as ongoing inspections during the construction pro-
  28|             cess. Other Fire Safety Initiatives undertaken during 2023 include:
  29|             • Continuation of a Department of Fire Services Grant in the amount of $6953 to assist with Fire Safety Educa-
  30|                  tion in both the schools and with Senior Citizens. This Grant provides funding for both materials and person-
  31|                   nel to bring fire safety education to our most vulnerable populations. Firefighter Olivia Hayes serves as our
  32|                   Coordinator for this program.
  33|             • Participation in the Senior Center's "Sand for Seniors" Program. This program provides buckets of walkway
  34|                  sand for Senior Citizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in the winter months. In partnership with
  35|                  this program, Firefighter Olivia Hayes delivers the sand to the residents and when possible, inspects their
  36|                   homes for Smoke and Carbon Monoxide Detectors. If the resident was lacking detectors, Firefighter Hayes
  37|                   can install free detectors obtained through the Senior SAFE Grant.
  38|                        It should be noted that each year, 50% of fatal fires are found to have nonfunctional or nonexistent smoke
  39|                              detectors.
  40|              Fire Prevention has become increasingly complex as codes change and new technologies appear. To keep pace,
  41|             both Inspectors hold certifications with the State as Fire Prevention Officers- Level 1 or 2. In addition two other
  42|             members have also completed the Level one training and other staff have completed the Basic Level training.
  43|                                                                                               92

===PAGE 93=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                                                                           FIRE DEPARTMENT
   2|             Eight Firefighters from Lunenburg continue to be members of the Fire District 8 Technical Rescue and Dive
   3|             Teams. These regional teams respond to high risk and specialized rescue situations, such as Confined Space,
   4|             Rescue from Heights, Building and Trench Collapse, Swift/Floodwater and Underwater Search and Recovery. In
   5|             conjunction with these Teams, Lunenburg operates both a Water Rescue Unit and a Collapse/Trench Rescue
   6|             Trailer.
   7|             Our Apparatus fleet is currently in very good condition, with two pumpers and the Ladder truck all under 10 years
   8|             old. Our Rescue Pumper, Squad 3 remains in good condition though it is nearing the end of its projected life
   9|             span. This unit is scheduled for Replacement in FY'27. This unit is a dual-purpose Pumper and Rescue Unit, car-
  10|             rying the Jaws of Life, High Pressure Air Bags, and other specialized rescue tools.
  11|             The Ambulances are experiencing increased wear and tear as we see not only more calls for service, but longer
  12|             transport times to more distant hospitals and critical care facilities. 30 years ago, we had four hospitals within a
  13|             10-15-minute transport time. Today there are two. In addition, many cardiac and trauma patients require definitive
  14|            treatment in specialty facilities. This often requires transport to Worcester or occasionally even Boston. Given
  15|            lengthy delays in obtaining new units, a replacement for Rescue 1, our 2014 ambulance was ordered in March of
  16|             2023. Delivery is not anticipated until early 2025.
  17|             Replacement of our outdated Forest Fire protective clothing with a dual-purpose type of clothing that is suitable
  18|            for both brush fires and motor vehicle/technical rescue situations was completed This project was funded
  19|            through a State Grant for Safety Equipment. Though supply chain issues slowed the process, the equipment fi-
  20|             nally arrived in Spring of 2023. Several hundred feet of hose and some outdated nozzles were also replaced
  21|            through a State Grant in 2023. A Grant to replace several power saws and some portable radios was applied for in
  22|            late 2023 and is anticipated for early 2024.
  23|             Captain James Ricci retired in March of 2023 after 32 years of Career and Call service to the Town. Jim served as
  24|             our Fire Prevention Officer for 17 years and was well known for his conscientious and down-to-earth approach to
  25|             code enforcement. Jim was instrumental in developing the "Sand for Seniors" program in conjunction with the
  26|             Eagle House Staff. He was also awarded the "Norman Knight Excellence in Community Service Award" in 2014
  27|            for his fire prevention and community education work. While Jim's retirement left big shoes to fill, we are fortu-
  28|             nate that he has chosen to continue to serve the Town as an On Call Firefighter/EMT.
  29|            In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their outstanding commit-
  30|             ment to the Town. Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication continue to impress me every day. In addi-
  31|             tion, I would like to express my gratitude to the members of the Department who have helped make the Paramed-
  32|            ic Program a reality. From those who helped develop policies and those who helped procure equipment, to those
  33|             members who endured the challenges of Paramedic training. Your efforts have helped bring about a massive im-
  34|             provement in service to our residents. My thanks also to the Town Manager for her unwavering support for this
  35|             vital project.
  36|            I would also like to thank the other department heads, boards, and commissions for their continuing help. The
  37|            level of interdepartmental cooperation that occurs in the daily course of town business is invisible to many peo-
  38|             ple, but it is critical to all operations. Finally, my thanks also go out to the citizens of Lunenburg for their ongoing
  39|             support of public safety. Such support allows us to continue providing the high level of service you have come to
  40|             expect from us.
  41|                                                                                               93

   6|                                                                      2023 Police Department Town Report
   7|                     Mission Statement:
   8|                     We, the members of the Lunenburg Police Department, strive to provide the highest quality of police services to
   9|                     our community members and visitors alike. We believe in Community Policing, and all of the benefits that are
  10|                     realized from this concept. We will strive to maintain the strong partnerships that have been developed with our
  11|                     community, and continuously look for ways improve that relationship. We will strive to deliver police services
  12|                     in a fair and impartial manner, consistent with all applicable laws, while respecting all individual rights. We
  13|                     Will use innovative and modern ideas and techniques to solve crimes, apprehend criminal offenders, and help
  14|                     resolve quality of life issues.
  15|                     Values:
  16|                          • We are committed to ensuring that all of our actions reflect the utmost integrity, and honesty.
  17|                          • We are committed to respecting individual rights, human dignity, and the values of all members of the
  18|                               community and members of the Lunenburg Police Department.
  19|                          • We are a proactive and problem solving agency.
  20|                           • We strive to serve our citizens in a professional manner with compassion, respect, and empathy.
  21|                          • We are committed to providing the highest quality of police services to our community.
  22|                          • We recognize the value of our employees, and recognize they are our most valued resource. We will
  23|                               strive to provide our employees with the necessary resources, training, and career development to
  24|                               enhance their professionalism.
  25|                          • We recognize that the wellbeing of our employees is the upmost importance. We will strive to provide
  26|                                the necessary assistance to assure our employees enjoy a long and satisfying career.

   1|                   Department Review:
   2|                   It is an honor to again provide the Lunenburg Police Department's Annual report as the Chief of Police. In
   3|                   2023, the Department continued to face challenges associated with the economy, population growth, and
   4|                   increased traffic within the community. A nationwide trend of recruiting and retaining Police Officers continue
   5|                  to have an effect on our Department, but it appears that we have stabilized in this area. Although faced with
   6|                   these challenges, the Department remains committed to being pro-active, and strives to make the Community
   7|                   safe for all.
   8|                   Sgt. Sean Connery retired in July after over 30 years of service. Sgt. Connery will remain on the Department as
   9|                   a Reserve Otficer. We wish Sgt. Connery the best of luck, good health, and a long and prosperous retirement.
  10|                   Off. Matthew Pingitore accepted a position with the Massachusetts State Police in February of 2023. We thank
  11|                   Matthew for his service to the Town and wish him the best of luck with MSP.
  12|                   K9 Update:
  13|                   KO Jerry officially retired in July of 2023. KO Jerry is enjoying retirement in the care of Sgt. Tocci. We thank
  14|                   K9 Jerry for his service to the Town. He was the first official K9 for the Department and he served us very
  15|                   well.
  16|                   Upon the retirement of K9 Jerry, the K9 Handler position was re-assigned. Off. Wagner was selected as the new
  17|                   K9 Handler and was tasked with researching and finding a new K9 for the Department. After an extensive
  18|                   search Off. Wagner located a German Shorthaired Pointer, who is named Cody, to be the next K9. K9 Cody
  19|                   began training at the beginning of summer and graduated in November of 2023. K9 Cody is currently in service
  20|                   and works a split day/eve shift with Off. Wagner. Be sure to say hi to them if you see them around Town.

===PAGE 100=== (ocr, 23 lines)
   1|                    Accreditation:
   2|                    On October 4, 2023, the Department became fully accredited and received an award for Certificate of
   3|                    Accreditation through the Massachusetts Police Accreditation Commission (MPAC). The Department is
   4|                    recognized as a best practice organization. Accreditation is a coveted award that symbolizes professionalism,
   5|                    excellence, and competence.
   6|                    Through the tireless work of Sgt. Morreale, and the cooperation and collaboration with members of our
   7|                    Department, we were able to complete the full accreditation process. The Department was recognized and
   8|                    received the MPAC award on October 4, 2023, at the Commission's award ceremony held at the Marlborough
   9|                    Country Club.
  10|                    Program Updates:
  11|                    The Co-Response Model approach to mental health type of calls is moving forward. We were able to partner
  12|                    with Lancaster Police Department and Open Sky Community Services to bring the program to reality. The
  13|                    Town has initially funded the program through the Opioid Relief Settlement. The Department will be seeking a
  14|                    grant through DMH to fully fund the program.

   1|                    Academy Program during the summer and school vacation week(s). We are also planning a Citizen's Police
   2|                    Academy for the fall of 2024.
   3|                    2023 Statistical Information:
   4|                    The overall goal of the Lunenburg Police Department is a reduction of crime, assure quality of life issues are
   5|                    addressed, and provide a safe community to live, work and visit. The Department evaluates effectiveness, in
   6|                    part, by statistical information that is generated through the Records Management System.
   7|                    The following statistical information is to provide some insight into our officers' activity for 2023.

===PAGE 102=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                       License to Sell/Ammunition/Gunsmith                                                                    N/A
   2|                       Machine Gun License                                                                                    N/A
   3|                  As result of the above calls our officers generated a total of 1,355 written reports. There were 379 reports
   4|                  involving Incident Based Reporting (IBR), and National Incident Based Reporting (NIBRS), which is a State
   5|                  and Federal Crime reporting data base. The Officers also generated an additional 774 written reports that did
   6|                  not tall into the IBR/NIBRS category.
   7|                  According to NIBRS, reported crimes are broken down into three categories, Crimes Against Persons, Crimes
   8|                  Against Property, and Crimes Against Society. These are further broken down into Group A and Group B
   9|                  categories to closely match the Uniform Crime Reporting (UCR) program. The Lunenburg Police Department
  10|                  regularly reports IBR information to the State, which in turn reports the information to the Federal Bureau of
  11|                  Investigation.
  12|                  In 2023, there has been a 3% decrease in Group A, Crimes Against Persons, with Simple Assaults and
  13|                  Intimidation (Witness/victim) accounting for the majority of the incidents with 40 out of 60 total reported
  14|                  incidents. We have observed an increase in Simple Assaults and Intimidation cases with a 19% and 7%
  15|                  increase, respectively.
  16|                  In 2023, there has been a 14% increase in Group A, Crimes Against Property. The area we have observed an
  17|                  increase is related to shoplifting, accounting for a majority of the incidents with 59 out of 161 total reported
  18|                  incidents. There has also been an increase in Fraud type of cases, accounting for the second largest increase
  19|                  with 45 incidents.
  20|                  In 2023, there has been a 41% increase in Group A, Crimes Against Society. The largest increase has been in
  21|                  drug/narcotic violations, which is directly related to having the Detective proactively investigating drug/narcotic
  22|                  related cases.
  23|                  Training:
  24|                  Training continues to be a priority for the Department. We now have In-house instructors for specialized areas
  25|                  including Firearm, Less Than Lethal, Defensive Tactics, Active Shooter, and Resiliency (Officer Well Being).
  26|                  The Department's in-house trainers look for ingenuitive ways to complement our annual in-service training.
  27|                  In addition, several officers have attended specialized training in various areas of Policing as a part of
  28|                  protessional development. Trainings include Crisis Intervention Team, Critical Incident Management System,
  29|                  Servant Officer and Intentional Leadership Programs.
  30|                  The Department also provides training designed for our non-sworn members, such as Police Executive
  31|                  Assistant Conference, and Massachusetts Certified Public Purchasing Official.
  32|                  Use of Force:
  33|                  The Department conducts an annual analysis of Use of Force incidents, as well as review(s) of the incidents as
  34|                  they occur. In 2023, officers were involved in eight (8) use of force incidents, which is a reduction in incidents
  35|                  compared to 2022. In each Use of Force incident de-escalation tactics, including the use of verbal tactics, were
  36|                  first utilized prior to any Use of Force application. Officers continue to strive to de-escalate confrontations
  37|                  prior to any application of Use of Force.
  38|                  One officer was injured prior to the Use of Force. There was one minor injury reported to an individual during
  39|                  the application of Use of Force.

   1|                   Motor Vehicle Pursuits:
   2|                   The Department conducts an annual analysis of Motor Vehicle Pursuits, as well as review(s) of pursuits as they
   3|                   occur. In 2023, officers were involved in three (3) motor vehicle pursuits, which is a reduction in incidents as
   4|                   compared to 2022. There were no accidents, and no injuries to the public, officers or suspects) involved in the
   5|                   pursuits that were reported.
   6|                   Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District:
   7|                   The Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District (NVRDD) is a Regional Emergency Communications Center
   8|                   located in Devens, MA. NVRDD serves as the 911 public safety answering point and dispatch center for Police,
   9|                   Fire and EMS for the communities of Lunenburg, Berlin, Bolton, Boxborough, Havard, Lancaster, Devens, and
  10|                    Department of Defense at Devens. NVRDD serves a total population of just over 35,000 residents and
  11|                   thousands more visitors to the orchards, farms, businesses, and attractions within its six communities.

  13|                   In 2023, NVRDD handled over 76,982 phone calls coming in on 911 and business lines. NVRDD also
  14|                   coordinated the response to over 81,121 calls for service. NVRDD's staff are highly skilled in call-handling and
  15|                   dispatching, and are all able to perform both functions simultaneously. These first of the first-responders
  16|                   provide a vital and seamless link between the community and their local police, fire, and emergency medical
  17|                   services. While the focus is on handling emergencies, their staff also serves the public and our responders by
  18|                   providing other administrative and non-emergency dispatch tasks. NVRDD staffs the communications center
  19|                   with multiple dispatchers and supervisors each shift to handle calls and requests.

  23|                   best in policing to our Community.

   1|                                                                           CAPTIAL PLANNING REPORT
   2|                                                                          Fiscal Year 2024 Capital Plan
   3|             The Capital Planning Committee met several times during the fall and early winter months to consider capital
   4|             requests that were submitted by department heads. Following extensive dialogue with department heads and
   5|             revisions to some requests, the committee considered 28 requests and ultimately voted to rank 27 items for
   6|             consideration by the Town Manager, Select Board, and Finance Committee.
   7|             On January 28th        ', the Town Manager presented her preliminary Capital Plan. Her recommendation included the
   8|             first six items recommended by the Capital Planning Committee, totaling $1,360,500.
   9|             On February 13",         ', the Capital Planning Committee met to consider the Town Manager's preliminary capital
  10|             plan and some differences in her ranking of projects. No action was taken, however at the time there were two
  11|             outstanding issues to be addressed before a final capital plan recommendation could be submitted for the war-
  12|             rant.
  13|             The first was a request for American Rescue Plan Act (ARPA) funds to purchase three vehicles (an ambulance
  14|             and two 6-wheel dump/spreader/plow trucks). On February 21st                                   " the Select Board committed $986,000 of
  15|             ARPA funds for these projects. This action accelerated ordering the vehicles by several months (demand for
  16|             these items and supply chain issues have resulted in extended delays) and eliminated a need to tap the Special
  17|             Purpose Stabilization Account to fund these items.
  18|             The second issue was the availability of free cash, which the Town Manager had reserved to fund the overlay
  19|             account, relating to the high number of property tax abatement requests. On March 28"h                                             , the Town Manager
  20|             presented a revised recommendation that eliminated the $986,000 from the Special Purpose Stabilization Ac-
  21|             count, added $467,268.98 from free cash, added $356,789 from tax revenues, and reduced the amount from
  22|             unexpended capital by $2,758.98 to her recommendation for the FY24 Capital Plan to fund six additional pro-
  23|            jects. The Town Manager's new recommendation also removed two items from the priority list (TCP Ansul
  24|             Inspection and Public Safety Building Downspouts) after consultation and further assessment by the Facilities
  25|             Director. The following outlines the Town Manager's final Capital Plan Recommendation with associated
  26|             Capital Planning Committee rankings and scoring derived from the UMass Collins Center for Public Manage-
  27|             ment. There are 12 items, totaling $2,025,010.00. As referenced above, $986,000 of that total does not require
  28|             an Annual Town Meeting appropriation or approval.
  29|             Capital Planning Committee Members:
  30|             Tom Alonzo, Chair, Select Board Representative
  31|             David Passios, Vice-Chair, Finance Committee Representative
  32|             Peter Beardmore, Clerk, School Committee Representative
  33|             Matthew Brenner, Planning Board Representative
  34|             George Martin, Member At Large

   1|                                                                            PLANNING BOARD
   2|             Nine (9) ANR Plans (land division plans not requiring approval under the Subdivision Control Law, but still
   3|             requiring Board endorsement) were approved.
   4|             Lunenburg's Stormwater Bylaw requires Board review and approval for any development/redevelopment pro-
   5|             ject that disturbs one acre or more of land. This Bylaw safeguards the public health, safety, welfare and the
   6|             environment by minimizing adverse impacts of contaminated stormwater both off-site and downstream. These
   7|             updates are more likely to affect large commercial projects and multi-family dwellings; not the average home-
   8|             owner wanting to put in a shed, garden, pool, etc. The Board approved stormwater permits for the following.
   9|                                   • Development of 5 lots on Holman Street and Northfield Rd
  10|                                   • Construction of 367,000 sq ft warehouse on Fitchburg/Lunenburg land
  11|                                   • Construction of a single-family home on Reservoir Rd
  12|                                   • Construction of a single-family home on Highland St
  13|                                   • Construction of a contractor yard, personal improvement building, and office building on
  14|                                         Massachusetts Ave
  15|             Lunenburg has four designated Scenic Roads; Lancaster Avenue, Flat Hill Road, Northfield Road, and Le-
  16|             ominster Road. Over the past year you may have noticed building lots, such as some of those mentioned in the
  17|             paragraph above, being created on some of these roads. As these are Scenic Roads, any repair, maintenance,
  18|             re-construction, or paving work in the Town's right-of-way cannot involve the cutting or removal of trees or
  19|             the tearing down or destruction of stone walls without consent of the Planning Board.
  20|             The Board submitted four articles for November 2022 special town meeting.
  21|             • Two of the articles, which passed, updated the zoning bylaw to reflect the current zoning;
  22|             • Two articles were submitted on behalf of the Water District to update the Watershed Protection Map. DEP
  23|                  requested additional changes to the proposed bylaw which Board members were not comfortable with;
  24|                  therefore, the Board chose to pass over the Water District articles.
  25|             The Land Use Director worked on a new draft for 2023 annual town meeting. The two Water District articles
  26|                   were submitted for May 2023 town meeting, but did not pass.
  27|             A third article was also submitted for 2023 annual town meeting which added clarification to the permitting
  28|                  process for non-medical marijuana establishments. That article passed.
  29|             An application was received for construction of a 367,000 square foot warehouse and distribution building at
  30|             260 Falulah Road. The majority of the building was located on Airport Road/Crawford Street in Fitchburg,
  31|             but a portion extended into Lunenburg.
  32|             Approval was given for the construction of a 372,000 square foot warehouse and distribution building off Le-
  33|             ominster-Shirley Road near the Shirley town line. As a result of this approval, improvements were made to
  34|             Leominster-Shirley Road with the addition of an eastbound left-turn lane into the project and the widening of
  35|             the road to the north (towards Reservoir Road) on the project side of the street.
  36|             The Planning Board requested and was approved for Capital Plan Funding to begin an update of the 2002 Mas-
  37|             ter Plan. The process began with selecting outside consultants for Master Planning Services. OverUnder, RKG
  38|             Associates Inc. and Innes Associates joined forces to create an experienced and dynamic team to take on the
  39|             task of updating the Master Plan. They have experience in economic planning and real estate consultancy.
  40|             They have a strong understanding and guiding physical use of land and its impact of regulations on communi-
  41|             ties. They stressed their importance of public engagement to reflect the values of community which was
  42|             demonstrated in conducting public surveys to hear from Lunenburg residents how they would like to influence
  43|             the development of their Town in the years to come.
