===PAGE 111=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                                                MASTER PLANNING STEERING COMMITTEE
   2|             The Master Plan Steering Committee (MPSC) advises on the development of the Master Plan
   3|             document. The committee also fosters openness and inclusion in the Master Plan process
   4|             through dialogue and communication with the consulting team OverUnder, RKG, Innes Asso-
   5|             ciates, and various stakeholders in the Lunenburg Community. The Master Plan will develop a
   6|             physical and digital document for the Town of Lunenburg that optimally reflects and supports
   7|             the community's priorities and goals. A comprehensive plan takes a long range view of the
   8|             Town's land use, housing, open spaces, natural and cultural resources, public facilities, eco-
   9|             nomic development, and transportation —and tries to shape the direction of the Town for the
  10|             next five to fifteen years. The Town completed its last comprehensive plan in 2002, more than
  11|             twenty years ago. The Lunenburg Planning Board initiated this new comprehensive plan pro-
  12|             cess, Plan Lunenburg, to explore and define the Town's shared vision and create an updated
  13|             framework for future Town goals and policies. In 2023, PlanLunenburg.org was developed to
  14|             be the host site of Lunenburg's Master Plan process. Iwo Public Meetings were conducted as
  15|             well as surveys in between. The MPSC worked closely with OverUnder to coordinate venues,
  16|             distribute surveys, and encourage community involvement. The MPSC, OverUnder, RKG,
  17|             Innes Associates, along with the energetic involvement of residents of Lunenburg, have suc-
  18|             cessfully kept the process on track with each phase originally outlined: Phase 1 in May 2023
  19|             focusing on discovery, Phase 2 in February 2024 focusing on testing and recommending, and
  20|             Phase 3 in August 2024 leading to a report. All throughout each phase, the MPSC actively
  21|             seeks the engagement of the residents of Lunenburg so that their voices can be heard in the
  22|             Master Plan process.
  23|             Report from OverUnder:
  24|             It has been a year since a notice to proceed (March 27, 2023) was issued to the Master Plan
  25|             consultant team (OverUnder, RKG Associates, and Innes Associates). In that time, we've held
  26|             three public meetings. Public meeting #1 was held at Town Hall on June 15, with 10 attendees.
  27|             The second public meeting was held at Smokestack Roasters on November 2, 2023 with 30
  28|             participants. The third meeting took place at the Adult Activity Center on February 2, 2024 in
  29|             two sessions, with a total of 35 participants. The team also attended two events on Homecom-
  30|             ing weekend in October 2023 (Shrieks & Krieks and Homecoming), during which we solicited
  31|             feedback and passed out information regarding the website and solicited feedback from the
  32|             community while answering questions about the master planning process. The Master Plan
  33|             Steering Committee was established in the fall of 2023, and has held five meetings, with the
  34|             sixth planned for April 5. In the past year, the following tasks have been accomplished:
  35|             • development of a community engagement plan,
  36|             • planning process branding, publishing of the planlunenburg, org website,
  37|             • construction of large scale (5' × 8') model of the Town,
  38|             • completion of the existing conditions report, and
  39|             • two surveys completed (survey #1: 163 respondents, survey #2: 457 respondents)

  11|                    The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows:
  12|                         1. To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town's Protective By-Laws as authorized in the By-laws.

  15|                              amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a permit or enforcement action from
  16|                              the Administrative Officer or Board in the Town, the Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town or other
  17|                               Administrative Official.
  18|                         4. To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter 40B.
  19|                              The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits submitted to the Board under Chapter
  20|                              40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing. These requests can, by law, circumvent local zoning
  21|                              ordinances.

===PAGE 116=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|                                                                           Lunenburg Public Schools
   2|                                                                                 Town Report 2023
   3|                                                                            Superintendent's Message
   4|             I am fortunate to have enjoyed six years working in partnership with the Lunenburg School Committee. Together
   5|             we have remained focused on serving the best interests of the students. I am appreciative for the support of the
   6|             Town Manager, Select Board and Finance Committee in our work to meet the educational needs of the children of
   7|             Lunenburg.
   8|             The district has remained focused on execution of the Strategy for Continuous Improvement, which is aligned with
   9|             our Mission and Vision:
  10|             The Mission of the Lunenburg Public Schools is to provide all students with the skills, confidence, and passion for
  11|             He-long learning so that they may find their own paths to successful careers, active citizenship, and rewarding
  12|             The Lunenburg Public Schools is committed to becoming a leader in education by focusing on its values of attend-
  13|             ing to the personal well-being of its students, providing high-quality curricula and student-centered instruction for
  14|             deeper learning at all levels, and promoting the development of attributes and skills necessary for continuous
  15|             learning for all.
  16|             Since the School Committee approved the Strategy for Continuous Improvement in November 2019, we have
  17|             worked with intent to achieve our vision, putting the necessary infrastructure to support the well-being and academ-
  18|             ic achievement of all students in place. The Strategy is a living document reviewed by the District Leadership
  19|             Team each August. In August 2023, the Strategy objectives were consolidated from four original objectives to a
  20|             singular objective for the next two to three years. The Strategy is now centered on Cultivating Climate & Culture.
  21|                         Cultivating Climate & Culture: To create a culture that values data use and fosters continuous learn-
  22|                        ing in a climate that is safe, caring, inclusive and reflective of our core values.
  23|             Over the last five years, we adhered to our Strategy action plans. Guided by those action plans and our 5-Year
  24|             Staffing Plan, we pursued and secured funding from grants to supplement our local budget in order to execute the
  25|             Strategy. As a result, the district was able to:
  26|             • Add a Director of Teaching & Learning; Title I Coordinator; Literacy, Math, and Co-Teaching Coaches
  27|             • Increase district behavioral health staffing from six (6) guidance counselors and one (1) social worker to six (6)
  28|             guidance counselors and five (5) social workers
  29|             • Adopt a standards-aligned, rigorous on-grade-level curriculum in ELA/Literacy K-8, math K-5 and pilot math
  30|             curriculum 6-8 for adoption in 2024-2025
  31|             • Adopt a districtwide Tier 1 SEL curriculum
  32|             • Implement district benchmark assessments in literacy and math
  33|             • Add assessment tools to gather data in order to determine need for intervention
  34|             • Create an intervention model for academics and behavioral health
  35|             • Create a referral process for intervention
  36|             • Increase common planning time
  37|                                                                                              116

===PAGE 117=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|                                                                            Superintendent's Message
   2|             Now, with staff, curriculum and best practices in place, we are seeing improved student outcomes. We must con-
   3|             tinue to strengthen differentiation in instruction to meet the needs of all learners at all levels.
   4|             The district continues working to build a more inclusive culture in our schools. We have participated in several
   5|             recruitment fairs to promote Lunenburg Public Schools employment opportunities to a more diverse pool of appli-
   6|             cants. We have also established a Culture & Climate Council comprised of parents/guardians, staff and high school
   7|             student representatives. This Council is currently working to articulate the roles of the student, parent/guardian,
   8|             and educator in the education of each student. We believe articulating the roles and responsibilities in the relation-
   9|             ship will ultimately foster more family engagement in the education of our students.
  10|             The School Committee has also maintained a focus on the aging Turkey Hill Elementary School. The building pre-
  11|             sents challenges and limitations for our teachers in terms of delivery of instruction in the modern era to elementary
  12|             school-aged students. Our challenges include limited electrical outlets in classrooms and lack of energy efficiency,
  13|             which is particularly evident with very warm or very cold weather conditions. The building has aging systems and
  14|             system failures is a concern. It is important for the administration, School Committee, and other town boards and
  15|             committees to continue to engage in discussion regarding the future of Turkey Hill Elementary School.
  16|             Over the last five years, we have utilized all available resources and worked with intent to impact student achieve-
  17|             ment and overall well-being in the post-COVID years and beyond. We have remained true to the Strategy for Con-
  18|             tinuous Improvement. There are now materials, structures, procedures, and expectations in place; however, if we
  19|             expect to see continued improvement in student achievement, proper staffing levels are necessary. This will be one
  20|             of our greatest challenges in the next few years as the additional grant funding sources wane.
  21|             "Determination is the product of three elements: a GOAL, a COMMITMENT, and a FOCUS. You have
  22|             to have something to strive for. Then you've got to be committed. You've got to believe in your goal." ~
  23|             Dr. Alan Zimmerman
  24|             I want to express my gratitude to the Lunenburg School Committee for trusting me to take their priorities and hopes for
  25|             the district and turn vision into action to bring that vision to life. I must acknowledge and thank our dedicated staff for
  26|             their professionalism, flexibility, and hard work. Their commitment and efforts are making the learning experience for
  27|             our students more equitable, meaningful, and impactful. Collectively, we set goals, committed to those goals and re-
  28|             mained focused on those goals to provide a quality education for the children of Lunenburg.
  29|             Finally, each year I conclude my report with the announcement of the Superintendent's Award. I am pleased to an-
  30|             nounce Brionna Bowser as the recipient of the 2024 Superintendent's Award. Brionna's academic accomplishments
  31|             include serving as National Honor Society President and receiving Academic Excellence Awards in Math, Spanish, Art,
  32|             English and U.S. History. She was also an Academic All-Star Award recipient in Soccer. Brionna has a passion for soc-
  33|             cer both as a player and as a coaching assistant for the youth league. She also volunteers her time at the food pantry,
  34|             Kylee's Kare Kits, and her church. The accomplishment she is most proud of is attaining her lifeguard certification.
  35|             Brionna believes in being present in the moment and when asked for a quote that best describes her she offered "Enjoy
  36|             your life today, because yesterday has gone and tomorrow may never come." — Alan Coren
  37|             Respectfully Submitted,
  38|             Dr. Kate Burnham, Superintendent

  27|                                                                   Lunenburg Public Schools IT Review
  28|             The Lunenburg Public Schools Information Technology (IT) Department played a critical role in addressing
  29|             the technology needs both inside and outside of the classroom. The Department also focused heavily on im-
  30|             proving the district's cybersecurity posture. The following projects were completed as part of an emphasis on
  31|             network security:
  32|                                Network Upgrade: Hardware that was no longer able to receive security updates was replaced in
  33|                        all buildings.
  34|                                 Segmentation: The district's network was separated from the town's network. As a result, both
  35|                        district and town are less vulnerable to security threats.
  36|                        3.     Consultation: A full penetration test and vulnerability report was performed by an outside vendor
  37|                        improvemed to the district. The results provided useful data points and recommendations for further
  38|                                                                                              119

===PAGE 120=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|             Solutions supporting student safety were also addressed this year. SaferWatch was made accessible to staff
   2|             throughout the district. This program can be installed on smart phones or PCs and acts as a distress button for
   3|             emergencies. In addition, the Department also implemented GoGuardian to keep students safer on the web.
   4|             Technology continued to be a prevalent tool in the classroom. To support the growing need for devices, the
   5|             district leveraged ECF (Emergency Connectivity Fund) to purchase new end-user devices for staff at Lunen-
   6|             burg Primary School. Grades 6 and 9 also received new Chromebooks.
   7|             The IT Department continued to manage and respond to all help desk requests with an extra focus on follow-
   8|             ing best practices, student technology usage and excellent customer service. The team will continue to direct
   9|             efforts towards improving end-user experience and protecting the district's technology environment from po-
  10|             tential cyber threats in the new year.

  14|                                               Town Report - FY23 (data taken from 2022-2023 school year)
  15|             From the March 1, 2023 report (SIMS Report 5-Enrollment Statistics - District Summary), there are 228 stu-
  16|             dents with disabilities receiving special education or related services in Lunenburg (last year there were 211).
  17|             This represents 14.6% of our total student population of 1,560 students (it was 13.2% last year). The state av-
  18|             erage from the 2022-23 Selected Populations Report (data was last updated on Dec. 1, 2022) (https://
  19|             profiles.doe.mass.edu/statereport/selectedpopulations.aspx) is 19.4%. From the March 1, 2023 report (SIMS
  20|             Report 7-SPED Enrollment Statistics - District Summary), the total number of students receiving services out-
  21|             side the district was 7 (last year was 8). Of the 8 students, 3 are placed in Collaborative Placements, 3 are in
  22|             Day Placements and 1 are in Residential Placements. Our Advanced Community Experience Program (ACE),
  23|             which serves students ages 18-22 years old, had 4 students enrolled last year. Additionally, from the March 1,
  24|             2022 report, 155 students identified with a disability received accommodations via 504 plans in Lunenburg
  25|             (last year was 130).For the 2023 school year, our Accountability Overall Classification was "not requiring
  26|             assistance or intervention". The reason for this classification was "Substantial Progress Towards Tar-
  27|             gets (57%)". Participation rates in MCAS this year were as follows: High Needs = 100%, Low Income =
  28|             100%, EL and Former EL = n/a%, and Students w/disabilities = 100%. This data can be found at the
  29|             DESE website:
  30|             http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/accountability/report/district.aspx?linkid=30&orgcode=01620000&orgtypecode=5&
  31|             Other data collected throughout the year is also important to consider when looking at the whole Special Services Department.
  32|             DESE requires us to collect data for certain Indicators they are targeting each year. For our Indicators, the following information
  33|             is available below and also can be found at: http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/gis/sped_map.aspx?orgcode=01620000&
  34|             Indicator 1 - Graduation Rate. For the 2021-2022 school year, the state target for Students with IEPs was 77.33% (was 76.33%
  35|             last year). Lunenburg's rate was 66.7% (was 55.6% last year) and the overall state rate was 82.1% (was 80.4% last year) for Stu-
  36|             dents with IEPs.
  37|             Indicator 2 - Dropout Rate. For the 2021-2022 school year, the state target for Students with IEPs was 13.47% (was 13.97% last
  38|             year). Lunenburg rate for Students with IEPs was 11.1% (it was 0.0" last year) and the overall state rate was 11.4% (was 10.4%
  39|             last year).
  40|             Indicator 3C - Proficiency Rate for Students with IEPs on MCAS. 2021-2022 was a benchmark year so there is no state target for
  41|             the Proficiency Rate for Students with IEPs on MCAS in English/Language Arts and in Mathematics.
  42|                                                                                              120

   1|              The 2021 district-level data on the performance of all students with IEPs on MCAS, including Next-Generation MCAS, Legacy
   2|              MCAS and the MCAS-Alt, is available at:
   3|             http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/mcas/subgroups2.aspx?linkid=25&orgcode=01620000&fycode=2021&orgtypecode=5
   4|             http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/mcas/achievement_alt_level.aspx?linkid=116&orgcode=01620000&fycode=2021&orgtypecode=5
   5|             Indicator 4 - Suspension (more than 10 days)/Expulsion for Students with IEPs. For Lunenburg, our rate was 0.0% (last year it
   6|             was 0.0%). The state rate was.1%. Additionally, Indicator 4B records significant discrepancies with the Suspension/Expulsion
   7|             rates by race or ethnicity. In 2022, Lunenburg was not found to have any significant discrepancies due to these factors.
   8|             Indicator 5 - Educational Environments for Students Aged 6-21 with IEPs. For 2021-2022, the state's rates are as follows: for Full
   9|             Inclusion of students = 66.9%, Partial Inclusion = 13.3%, Substantially Separate Placements = 13.6%, and for Separate Schools/
  10|              Residential Facilities/Homebound/Hospitals = 6.2%. In Lunenburg, our percentages are as follows: Full Inclusion = 69.7%, Par-
  11|              tial = 7.6%, Substantially Separate = 14.2% and Separate schools, Residential, etc = 8.5%.
  12|             Indicator 6 - Educational Environments for Students Aged 3-5 with IEPs. The 2021-2022 state rates showed the percent of stu-
  13|              dents receiving a majority of their special education and related services in a Full Inclusion early childhood program was 50.4%,
  14|             in a Partial Inclusion Program was 16.1%, a Substantially Separate Placement (special education class, separate school, or resi-
  15|              dential facility) was 19.9%, and students not attending an early childhood program but receiving only a related service (e.g.
  16|             speech, OT, PT) was 13.4%. In Lunenburg, our early childhood Full Inclusion rate was 25%, our Partial Inclusion rate was 18.8%,
  17|              Substantially Separate rate was 56.3%, and Related Services was 0%.
  18|             Indicator 7 - Early Childhood Outcomes. Districts participate in Indicator 7 data collection as part of a 4 year cycle in which they
  19|             report exit data on children for whom they have collected entry level data; exit may occur at any time during the subsequent
  20|             three year period as the children leave the preschool program. Lunenburg's 2021-2022 cohort reporting year results are sup-
  21|              pressed for districts reporting 10 or fewer students.
  22|             Indicator 8 - Parent Involvement. This indicator measures the % of parents with a child receiving special education who report
  23|              that school facilitated parent involvement as a means of improving services and results for students with IEPs. For 2021-2022
  24|              the state target was 89%. Lunenburg's rate was 7.3% and the state's rate was 10.7% (from information collected in the Spring
  25|             of 2022).
  26|             Indicator 9 & 10 - Both of these Indicators deal with Disproportionality in Special Education and in Special Education Categories.
  27|             In Lunenburg, the state determined that there was no disproportionality found in either case.
  28|             Indicator 11 - Initial Evaluation Timelines. This indicator measures the % of children with parental consent to evaluate, who
  29|             were evaluated within the State established timeline (2017-2018). Data is reported for all initial evaluations initiated in October,
  30|              November, and December of the reporting year. Lunenburg was rated 90% in compliance and the state average for this Indica-
  31|             tor was 100%.
  32|             Indicator 12 - Early Childhood Transition. This indicator measures the % of children referred by Part C, found eligible for special
  33|             education services, and who have an IEP developed and implemented by their third birthdays. The state target for this indicator
  34|             in 100% and Lunenburg is noted as "Suppressed" (last school year reviewed was 2017-18). Data is suppressed if the number of
  35|             students referred by Part C and found eligible for special education services is fewer than 10.
  36|             Indicator 13 - Secondary Transition. In all years, the state target for Secondary Transition is 100%. This indicator measures the
  37|              % of youth with IEPs aged 16 and above with an IEP that includes appropriate measurable postsecondary goals that are annually
  38|              updated and based upon an age appropriate transition assessment, transition services, including courses of study, that will rea-
  39|             sonably enable the student to meet those postsecondary goals, and annual IEP goals related to the student's transition service's
  40|              needs. Lunenburg was last reviewed in this area in 2017-2018 and was 83.3% in compliance.

   1|             Indicator 14 - Post-School Outcomes for Students with IEPs. _This indicator measures the % of students with IEPs who exited
   2|             high school during the 2021-2022 school year and self-reported post-school engagement in education or employment one year
   3|             after leaving high school. Engagement is defined through three measurements:
   4|                   Measurement A: Enrolled in higher education within one year of leaving high school.
   5|                   Measurement B: Enrolled in higher education or competitively employed within one year of leaving high school.
   6|                   Measurement C: Enrolled in higher education, or in some other postsecondary education or training program; or competi-
   7|                        tively employed, or in some other employment within one year of leaving high school.
   8|             Measurements are cumulative with Measurement C reflecting the total number of exiters engaged in a post-secondary activity
   9|             one year after leaving high school. For the 2021-2022 school year, state targets for the three measurements of Post-School Out-
  10|             comes are Measurement A: 50.2%; Measurement B: 79.6%; and Measurement C: 87.30%. Lunenburg rates are as follows (from
  11|             reporting year 2021-2022): Measurement A = 50%, Measurement B = 75%, and Measurement C = 75%.

  30|                                                             Teaching & Learning Models and PD Update
  31|                                                                                 Town Report 2023
  32|             Teaching and learning is the crux of our school system. We have been working diligently as a district to pro-
  33|             vide high-quality materials and instruction to students, provide targeted interventions and challenges to our
  34|             learners to support the learning process.
  35|             Using high-quality curriculum materials which are standards-aligned, inclusive, rigorous, and engaging is a
  36|             priority for the state of Massachusetts and for our district. We are in our second year of implementation for
  37|             our new English language arts and math curriculums, Into Reading and Into Math, at grades K-5. Teachers
  38|             have been making adjustments and refinements to practice, having taught all of the modules at least once. We
  39|             are also in our first year of implementation for Amplify, our new middle school language arts curriculum, and
  40|             we are in the process of evaluating and selecting a high-quality math program for our middle school as well.
  41|             Structured common planning time for teachers in grade level continues for grades K-8. Our district literacy
  42|             coaches meet with teachers during common planning time to support curriculum development and help foster
  43|             student growth. High school department heads structure department meeting time to facilitate the necessary
  44|             curriculum work needed to move each department forward and improve learning for students.
  45|                                                                                              122

===PAGE 123=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|               The Lunenburg Public School District is participating in the Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) Liter-
   2|               acy Academy, the Inclusive Practices Academy (based on the Universal Design for Learning), and the Social
   3|               Emotional and Behavioral Academy (SEB) through the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education
   4|               (DESE). All three academies provide coaching sessions and technical assistance in and assist our district
   5|               with creating action plans.
   6|                Lunenburg Public Schools uses multiple assessments to evaluate student achievement and growth and to be
   7|               able to provide supports and targeted interventions. Students in grades K-8 completed the fall and winter
   8|               iReady diagnostic assessment in both reading and math, and teachers are participating in data cycles to ana-
   9|               lyze data, plan for targeted support, and adjust instruction. Personalized learning platforms through iReady
  10|               provide differentiated instruction, systematic practice, and scaffolded feedback in both reading and math.
  11|               We are also using DIBELS (Dynamic Indicators of Basic Literacy Skills) along with i-Ready to determine
  12|               how students are performing on the critical skills necessary for reading: phonemic awareness, phonics, fluen-
  13|               cy, vocabulary, and comprehension. In math, we use the Forefront Universal Screener for Number Sense.
  14|               Lunenburg Primary and Turkey Hill have implemented a multi-tiered support system (MTSS) that provides
  15|               each school with a lead interventionist and two tutors to support students who are struggling to meet grade-
  16|               level expectations in reading and math. In order to meet the needs of all students, a team of educators work
  17|               with our literacy and math specialist to analyze multiple data points so that targeted interventions for support
  18|               and/or enrichment can be provided. Student progress is carefully monitored and adjustments are made as
  19|               necessary.
  20|               For professional development, we have been working with Dr. Kalise Wornum, owner of KW diversity. Dr.
  21|               Wornam has provided district staff with multiple sessions on cultural competence, and we have received
  22|               much positive feedback around the impact of this work. We have also been working to improve inclusion
  23|               and safety for our LGBTQ students. Students in our Gender and Sexuality Alliance (GSA) club at LHS
  24|               worked with district staff during our November PD day to present and answer questions, and we recently had
  25|               Landon Callahan from DESE's Safe Schools for LGBTQ Students present to our faculty from grades 6-12.
  26|               We have continued with our After School Program at Turkey Hill Elementary School, Lunenburg Middle
  27|               School, and Lunenburg High School. The program provides targeted support in literacy and math in addition
  28|               to academic support for students' current classes and with executive functioning skills.
  29|               We continue to review and pursue grant opportunities which are presented by the Department of Elementary
  30|               and Secondary Education and have received the Student Opportunity Act - Evidence-Based Practice Grant,
  31|               the Arts and Cultural Vitality Index Grant, the Deeper Learning Grant, and the Accelerating Literacy Grant
  32|               for FY24.
  33|               Respectfully Submitted,
  34|               Annica Scott
  35|               Director of Teaching & Learning

  19|                In September of 2023 Massachusetts legislative leaders announced that universal free school meals would be
  20|                permanent for K-12 students in Massachusetts regardless of household income. Ensuring all students have
  21|                access to the nutrition they need during the school day. As one of the few states continuing this
  22|                transformative program, Massachusetts is leading the way in addressing child nutrition, child hunger, and
  23|                taking a more holistic approach to student needs. As a direct result of universal free meals the Lunenburg
  24|                school lunch program participation has grown substantially.
  25|                Lunenburg School Lunch Program continues to participate in Harvest of the month promoting a different
  26|                Massachusetts-produced food each month in our cafeterias. This year we partnered with Fairview Orchards a
  27|                small family farm located in Groton, Massachusetts. A third generation run business, which began in 1945.
  28|                The family's mission is to bring local fresh fruit to schools in our community and surrounding areas. The
  29|                students and staff have enjoyed the delicious apples from Fairview Orchids.
  30|                We welcome some new employee's to our team Brenda Mahar and Kathy Dunlop.

  34|                                                                    LUNENBURG PRIMARY SCHOOL
  35|                                                                                   Town Report 2023
  36|                  The Lunenburg Primary School is a student focused, early childhood, learning community serving
  37|                  students enrolled in preschool through grade 2. Student ages range from three to eight years of age. Our
  38|                  community is composed of students, families, instructional and support staff. Our focus is on the
  39|                  academic, social emotional growth, and development of the whole child.
  40|                                                                                              124

===PAGE 125=== (ocr, 34 lines)
   1|               The October 1, 2023 student enrollment reported to the Department of Elementary and Secondary Educa-
   2|               tion was 394 students. With 48 preschool students, 107 kindergarten students, 116 first grade students and
   3|               123 second grade students. Class sizes range in 5 kindergarten classes from 19-23 students, from 18-22 stu-
   4|               dents in five grade one classes, and from 20-23 in six grade two classes.
   5|               Our professional development has centered on inclusive practices and continuing to refine our instruction
   6|               using the HMH Into Reading and Into Math curriculums. Common Planning Time continues to be a priority
   7|               for our building schedule and the reallocation of existing support staff to address student needs based on stu-
   8|               dent performance data. We have been able to access consultants as well district curriculum coordinators so
   9|               that teachers have an opportunity as a whole grade level to develop and provide evidence based instruction,
  10|               common learning experiences, student data analysis and share effective teaching strategies to support stu-
  11|               dent academic growth.
  12|                Our staff works to maintain a school culture which is positive, inclusive, and supportive. We continue to
  13|               implement the Responsive Classroom model in all of our classrooms. We have increased our use of Positive
  14|               Behavioral Interventions and Supports (PBIS). Which is
  15|               an evidence-based three-tiered framework for improving and integrating all of the data, systems, and prac-
  16|               tices affecting student outcomes every day. We have added monthly themes for students and staff to discuss
  17|               and complete an activity about. Working with our SRO and his emotional support canine, Hank, we recog-
  18|               nize students monthly with Hank's Helper awards. The awards are presented to students who exemplify the
  19|               qualities of being a helper and consistently showing kindness and respect to themselves, their building, and
  20|              to the people in their school community. During these assemblies we also select students to have Snack with
  21|               the Principal, a way to connect with small groups of students about their school experiences. We continue to
  22|               use Trails to Wellness, which is a whole-school/district-wide approach to teaching social emotional learn-
  23|              ing. It is designed to help students recognize, understand, describe, communicate and regulate their emo-
  24|               tions and provide them with the tools they need to create and maintain positive social interaction with their
  25|               peers.
  26|               The Primary School is a Title One school and we have continued our tiered instructional system to ensure
  27|               that all students have the services they need to be successful. Common assessments are completed in the fall,
  28|               winter, and spring for all children in grades K-2 in the areas of reading, writing, and math. These common
  29|               assessments serve to monitor the effectiveness of teacher instruction and the progress of students. Struggling
  30|               students are identified based on assessment data for access to our current Multi Tiered Support System
  31|               (MTSS) in Reading, Math, Speech, Occupational and Physical Therapy. The MTSS model is also used to
  32|               provide social emotional support for students that are referred to our building Behavioral Health Support
  33|               Team.
  34|                                                                                              125

===PAGE 128=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|             Staff continually work on the development of the whole child by supporting students' development and growth
   2|             in the areas of social emotional learning, literacy, math, social studies and science. We are able to meet stu-
   3|             dent needs with the support of the district curriculum of teaching and learning and the two coaches which ena-
   4|             bles teachers to have the opportunity to meet at grade levels to develop and provide evidence based instruction,
   5|             common learning experiences, student data analysis and share effective teaching strategies to support student
   6|             academic growth. Students continue to have benchmark assessments three times throughout the school year
   7|             (beginning, middle and end of year) that are used to help us celebrate students' academic growth and effort.
   8|             The data provided by these assessments also helps refine our focus for interventions.
   9|             This fall our fifth grade students had their annual trip to Nature's classroom. It was a successful week with
  10|             good weather, community building, and the creation of fond memories of a unique time together, thanks to the
  11|             combined efforts of our entire fifth grade staff.
  12|             We held an in-person open house in early September. It was a well-attended event and for many parents their
  13|             first opportunity to be physically present in our building. It was accompanied by a book fair facilitated by our
  14|             new librarian, Mrs. Mack. Parent conferences in October offered both in person and virtual options and ap-
  15|             pointments were fully booked within a few days.
  16|             Other highlights from the fall season included the Girls on The Run program, a research based program de-
  17|             signed to help girls understand themselves better, value their relationships and teamwork, and recognize how
  18|             they can shape the world to be a better place! Over 30 girls participated, with the assistance of many parents
  19|             and volunteers, headed by Amber Gothie and assistant coaches Kathy Jones and Principal Squier.
  20|             We held our annual Penny Wars fundraiser, it was a huge success and the money raised will be put toward the
  21|             development of our outdoor classroom. As always, our PTO assisted in the weeklong collection, headed by
  22|             parent Alex Lind; we could not be successful without their efforts. We were able to bring back a fifth grade
  23|             band and all students will be performing in an upcoming spring concert under the direction of our new music
  24|             teacher, Mr. Anthony Balester.
  25|             Turkey Hill also held its annual Turkey Trot in November. This year due to the weather we had to pivot and
  26|             held the race indoors with a portion outside.
  27|             Our PBIS committee has led the staff in the effective implementation of both PBIS and restorative justice at
  28|             Turkey Hill. Positive Behavioral Intervention Supports (PBIS) involves using evidence-based prevention and
  29|             intervention practices to support academic, social, emotional and behavioral competence of all students. This
  30|             year our students voted on a mascot name "Tom Turkey" and rotation stations were held in January to rein-
  31|             force the expected behaviors in school. Our students are caught "SOARing" which stands for Be Safe, Own it,
  32|             Act Responsibly, & Be Respectful. Restorative justice focuses on mediation and agreement rather than
  33|             punishment. It's about accepting responsibility for harm and making restitution. Our focus has shifted from a
  34|             penalty to "how can we make it right?" Students have been remarkably engaged in offering apologies, clean-
  35|             ing up, and making appropriate amends.

===PAGE 132=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|                                                                            Lunenburg Middle School
   2|                                                                                 Town Report 2023
   3|             Lunenburg Middle School provides a rigorous academic curriculum as well as strong programs that support
   4|             students' social and emotional growth. These programs ensure that each child becomes a self-motivated, crea-
   5|             tive, lifelong learner in a safe, stimulating environment that encourages diversity and open honest communica-
   6|             tion. The dedicated and accomplished staff of professionals within the school embraces the philosophy of ed-
   7|             ucating the whole child academically, socially, and emotionally. This philosophy is a means by which all stu-
   8|             dents become positive community members that possess the skills to be successful in high school and beyond.
   9|              Lunenburg Middle School is a co-located middle - high school serving grades six through eight. Our effective,
  10|             student centered middle school embraces interdisciplinary teams of teachers that enable teachers to work with
  11|             smaller groups of students. Smaller groups of students allow teachers to learn and engage students in their pre-
  12|             ferred learning style, making them more confident and successful.
  13|             Our effective middle school model is student centered and provides opportunities to explore a variety of differ-
  14|             ent interests and talents through special areas and enrichment classes and extracurricular activities. One of our
  15|             goals is for students to discover something they are passionate about and want to continue exploring as they
  16|             move through high school and beyond. We will continue to seek out opportunities for students that foster criti-
  17|             cal thinking and problem solving skills that prepare them for high school and beyond or where their passion
  18|             may lead them to.
  19|             Our grade eight team of teachers continue to implement a schedule that mirrors the high school schedule. Our
  20|             philosophy is to ease the difficult transition from middle school to the first year of high school by developing
  21|             student's independent skills they will require to be successful in high school. Grade eight teachers function as
  22|             one inclusive team where students take classes with a variety of different teachers as they would in a high
  23|             school setting. This differs from our two interdisciplinary team of teachers that have been a successful model
  24|             in grades six and seven.
  25|             LMS continues to utilize the i-Ready learning platform to provide an additional learning component in addi-
  26|             tion to classroom instruction. i-Ready is being implemented during our intervention blocks and provides
  27|             teachers with individualized learning paths based upon the students individual learning needs. Parents will be
  28|             provided fall, winter and spring benchmark assessment reports created by I ready so they too can monitor stu-
  29|             dent progress.
  30|             Lunenburg Middle School has benefited from a top notch guidance support team. Two guidance counselors, a
  31|             school social worker coupled with our shared school psychologist has been able to provide a level of social
  32|             emotional support to our students and their families that we have not been accustomed to. Data supports the
  33|             need for these positions as during the pandemic, the number of students experiencing anxiety and depression
  34|             has significantly increased nationwide and in our own community. Our guidance team has been able to meet
  35|             the needs of our students while preparing them to be available for academic learning.
  36|                                                                                              132

  15|                                                                              Lunenburg High School
  16|                                                                                 Town Report 2023
  17|             Lunenburg High School and its faculty continue to focus on rigorous curriculum, high standards and opportu-
  18|             nities for students, both academic and extracurricular, so that every student feels supported and included in our
  19|             community. LHS faculty strives to reflect on their teaching practices, to learn and to change in order to best
  20|             serve all of our students.
  21|                                                                              2023-2024 School year
  22|              Lunenburg High School returned this August with a renewed philosophy of support for our school communi-
  23|             ty. We have assembled an outstanding, talented faculty with a shared vison of supporting students academical-
  24|             ly, socially and emotionally as they tackle a rigorous and challenging curriculum. With our renewed sense of
  25|             support, students are able to acquire the skills necessary to earn a high school diploma as well as be successful
  26|             in post-secondary education as well as in the work force.
  27|             One pillar of support that has proven to be successful for students is the LHS Intervention Teacher. This teach-
  28|             ing position provides direct instruction to students who were identified by their teachers as to needing addi-
  29|             tional support to be successful in the classroom. Students report to the Interventionist during their study skills
  30|             block and receive assistance to the specific class or classes they are struggling in. The data show the students
  31|             participating in the intervention classroom show substantial improvement in the completion of classwork and
  32|             in their overall grades.
  33|                                                                                              135

===PAGE 136=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|             We have reorganized our Algebra classes to ensure all students are successful. With the addition of a class-
   2|             room teacher we were able to reduce class sizes in Algebra II and allow teachers to provide more personalized,
   3|             individualized instruction while working with smaller groups of students in a traditionally academically de-
   4|             manding course. This has been an effective strategy as you can see though our results in the MCAS table be-
   5|             low.
   6|              The addition of faculty and courses at Lunenburg High School have allowed students a more varied options
   7|             for course selection, provided individualized and programmatic support and structure for students to be suc-
   8|             cessful.
   9|                                                                                 MCAS RESULTS
  10|                                               Lunenburg High School - Spring 2023 MCAS Results (Grade 10)
  11|                             Subject Test                           Exceeding Expectations /                          State Average (EE/ME)
  12|                                   ELA                                              62%                                              58%
  13|                                  Math                                              65%                                              50%
  14|                                Science                                             52%                                              47%
  15|             The results indicate the LHS percentage scores for meeting and exceeding expectations are high, higher than
  16|             the state average and in mathematics, over 15 percentage points higher than the state average when compared
  17|             to schools who serve students in similar grade levels. Investing in a sixth math teacher that reduced class sizes
  18|             and provided students with additional individual classroom support was a successful strategy. The percentage
  19|             of grade 10 students scoring in the meeting and exceeding expectations category in mathematics is the highest
  20|             percentage in the district.
  21|             We were also very pleased to learn that 30 students at Lunenburg High School received the state's John and
  22|             Abigail Adams Scholarship this year. The scholarship is available to students whose MCAS performance puts
  23|             them at the top 25 percent of their district. To be eligible, students must either score Advanced on one exam
  24|             and Proficient on the other, or Advanced on both the English and Math exams. That is approximately 30% of
  25|             grade 10 students receiving the award.
  26|                                      HOMECOMING, ATHLETICS AND EXTRACURRICULAR ACTIVITIES
  27|             Homecoming weekend was a wonderful and well attended by students, families and alumni. We were lucky
  28|             we were able to reschedule the event and get such nice weather. The parade was a huge success with the col-
  29|             lection of candy the biggest hit with the children in town. Many community members noticed the care and time
  30|             that were placed into the floats at each grade level. The Blue Knights played well and were victorious in the
  31|             football game and we always enjoy watching the LHMS Marching Band perform.
  32|                                                          Lunenburg High School Athletic Accomplishments
  33|             The athletics department has had a great fall season. Girls soccer, boys soccer, and field hockey all won their
  34|             league titles. Our cheer team competed in the Wachusett Invitational competition and took home a 2nd place
  35|             trophy, their first such accomplishment since 2008. Golf placed 3rd overall in the league match. Cross Coun-
  36|             try competed in the Mid-Wach championship meet prior to all of the homecoming festivities. Football fin-
  37|             ished their season with a 4-7 record. Girls soccer and field hockey took their seasons all the way to the Sweet
  38|             16, while boys soccer made it to the Elite 8! What an accomplishment by all three teams.

  27|                                                                                    CURRICULUM
  28|             Teachers and administrators continue to refine, revise and create curriculum that is aligned to the state frame-
  29|             works and is rigorous and challenging. This year's focus is to work in vertical content area teams to ensure a
  30|             smooth transition for students as they advance grade by grade. This process also ensures that students will
  31|             have and build upon the educational skills that they will need to be successful in future grades. Scope and se-
  32|             quence maps have been created in order to assist in this grade level curriculum mapping.
  33|             Lunenburg High School continues to provide a high quality education that ensures student success and promotes a passion for life-
  34|             long learning. The key components to our success continue to be our talented faculty, our parents and our community members
  35|             who support our teaching and learning. Most of all, our students who inspire us through their commitment to learning. Together
  36|             we continue to make the difference in the lives of students.
  37|             Respectfully Submitted,
  38|             Tim Santry, Principal

===PAGE 141=== (ocr, 47 lines)
   1|                                    Letter from Leadership
   2|                                                          As a first-year Superintendent, I could not have asked for a better, more productive 2022-
   3|                                                          2023 school year. While I have been a part of this extraordinary educational community
   4|                                                         for ten years, the perspective from which I am now able to view our work, our
   5|                                                         accomplishments, and certainly our student achievements, has shifted and given me new
   6|                                                         purpose. I am honored and humbled to lead Montachusett Regional Vocational
   7|                                                         Technical School District, and am proud to share a few highlights in the following report.
   8|                                                         Workforce training continues to be a priority at the local, state and federal levels, with
   9|                                                         additional funding meant to incentivize schools like Monty Tech to get creative, and
  10|                                                         develop new training programs for high school students and adult learners seeking a
  11|                                                         career change. Monty Tech School of Continuing Education has expanded outreach to
  12|                                                         market all-new, no-cost vocational training programs to unemployed or underemployed
  13|                      individuals. Having received more than $1.6M in workforce training funds from Commonwealth Corporation to
  14|                      support this effort, the School of Continuing Education now offers free vocational training programs in Carpentry,
  15|                       CNC Operation, Culinary Arts, Electrical, HVAC Repair, Plumbing, and Welding. These programs are designed
  16|                      to provide participants with opportunities to earn valuable industry-recognized credentials, hands-on learning
  17|                      experiences, and job placement services upon program completion. At press, 42 adult learners have successfully
  18|                      completed their programs, been placed in jobs that align with their newfound skillset, and have been successfully
  19|                      employed for more than 3 months. This is an effort we continue to be so proud of.
  20|                      While we have enjoyed expanding access to our instructional spaces, training equipment, and technology to serve
  21|                       our evening students, we remain committed to seeking competitive grant funding that will allow us to improve and
  22|                      update instructional spaces so that our high school students stay ahead of the curve in their training and career
  23|                      aspirations. With more than 230 students participating in the popular co-op program, working and learning from
  24|                      experienced tradesmen and women in our area, our commitment to align our training programs to serve the needs
  25|                       of our region's workforce has never been more important. In 2022-2023, we heard from a local metal fabrication
  26|                      employer who asked us to consider adding a specific certitication to our curriculum, so our graduates would be
  27|                      better prepared to enter the workforce without a delay or need for additional training. So without hesitation, our
  28|                      instructors added the necessary lessons, and embedded an OSHA 10 Construction certification into the program.
  29|                      We heard from a local restaurant owner who asked if we could expand our training to include more sous vide
  30|                      preparation and improved knife skills to the students' training regimen. That request was honored immediately,
  31|                      and a student is curently working with that restaurant owner, demonstrating his improved skillset. We know that
  32|                      training students on new and improved equipment, while adapting and modifying our curiculum to address
  33|                      immediate workforce needs will, in the end, serve our students best - our ultimate goal. Monty Tech is steadfast
  34|                      in its commitment to providing a highly-skilled workforce to its industry partners throughout our region.
  35|                      Finally, and perhaps the most transformative accomplishment in 2022-2023, is an innovation that does not serve
  36|                      the immediate Monty Tech school community, but rather will benefit high school students enrolled in Fitchburg,
  37|                       Gardner and Narragansett Regional School Districts. While we have significantly expanded and updated our
  38|                      training programs, there are still so many young people without access, who would benefit from meaningfil career
  39|                      and technical education. To that end, we researched, applied for, and received a $1.9M grant that will allow us to
  40|                      develop an all-new vocational training space, designed to serve high school students during the day, and even more
  41|                      adult learners in the evening. Dubbed the Montachusett Vocational Partnership (MVP) Academy, this new training
  42|                      space will open its doors to more than 80 students in Fall 2024. We are honored to lead this trailblazing partnership,
  43|                      and expect it to become a model other vocational schools will emulate in the coming years.
  44|                       Thank you for your continued support of Monty Tech - a school that I am so honored to lead.
  45|                      Respectfully submitted,
  46|                      Thomas R. Browne, Superintendent-Director
  47|                                                                                               141

   7|                       In an effort to develop a cost-effective budget for the fiscal year 2022-2023, a great deal of effort was put forth
   8|                       by the School Committee, administration, and staff. The final fiscal year 2022-2023 Educational Plan totaled
   9|                       $30,796,896 which represents a 7.6% increase over the 2021-2022 Educational Plan which is driven largely in
  10|                       part by the 7.6% increase in the District's required minimum spending established by the Department of
  11|                        Elementary and Secondary Education. The District's FY23 budget only exceeds the minimum spending
  12|                       required by Massachusetts General Law Chapter 70 by $60,000 or 0.20%.
  13|                        To supplement the local budget, school officials pursue grant funding to provide valuable educational and
  14|                       social services to the diverse student population. For fiscal year 2023, state and tederal grant sources provided
  15|                       the school with $3,552,996. Programs funded by these grants include: Elementary and Secondary Schools
  16|                       Emergency Relief Funds, Improving Teacher Quality, Special Education Services and Program Improvement,
  17|                       Title I Support, Perkins Occupational Education, Vocational Capital Equipment Initiatives, Life Sciences,
  18|                       Student Support and Academic Enrichment and Marine Corp Junior Reserve Officer Training.
  19|                        The District will participate in an annual financial audit, conducted by the accounting firm of Melanson, Heath
  20|                       and Co. from Greenfield, Massachusetts. School officials anticipate a good report.

   1|                                                                                                  While students attending Monty Tech benefit from
   2|                                                                                                 project-based, hands-on learning often associated with a
   3|                                                                                                 vocational-technical high school experience, they must
   4|                                          Academics                                              also complete all of the same academic requirements
   5|                                                                                                 that their peers in comprehensive districts accomplish.
   6|                                                                                                 At Monty Tech, we believe a well-rounded high school
   7|                          experience includes rigorous and meaningful academic lessons. We are proud of our students'
   8|                          accomplishments in the classroom, as well as in their vocational shops.
   9|                          During the Spring of 2023, 10th grade students took the Next Generation MCAS in English Language Arts,
  10|                          Mathematics, and Biology. Select 9th graders also sat for the Next Generation MCAS Biology exam. Students
  11|                          performed well across all subject areas, with remarkable passing rates of 98% in English Language Arts, 98%
  12|                          in Mathematics, and 98% in Biology.
  13|                          Monty Tech's academic programs continue to evolve to prepare graduates for diverse and challenging college
  14|                          and career pathways, and are widely known to effectively prepare students at all levels to reach their goals. A
  15|                          focus in recent years has been the incorporation of new and updated materials and coursework to expand STEM
  16|                          opportunities for students. In FY23, Monty Tech was awarded a Massachusetts Life Science Center grant,
  17|                          developed in collaboration with our post-secondary partners at Fitchburg State University. Using the $110,000
  18|                          award, Monty Tech instructors worked collaboratively to align course expectations with FU's Anatomy &
  19|                          Physiology class, laying a foundation for a first-ever articulation agreement with FSU. Additionally, students
  20|                          who successtully complete the course will earn 4 college credits at FSU, saving them time and as much as
  21|                          $1,900 as they consider pursuing STEM college pathways. This grant has also provided funds to help
  22|                          modernize another science lab classroom to meet 21st Century lab requirements.
  23|                                                                                        2019               2020              2021               2022              2023
  24|                           Total AP Students                                             113               106                142                120               184
  25|                            Number of Exams                                              148               146                189                162               244
  26|                           AP Students with Scores 3+                                     69                75                 61                82                104
  27|                            % of Total AP Students with Scores 3+                      61.06%            70.75%             42.96%            68.33%             56.52%
  28|                          In May of 2023, our students participated in Advanced Placement (AP) exams and student performance
  29|                          showed outstanding results. A record number of students (184) participated in AP Exams, taking 244 AP
  30|                          Exams collectively. 104 of these students were eligible to receive college credit with qualifying scores of
  31|                          3t. Although the percentage of qualifying scores at 57% is lower than last year's percentage, the total number
  32|                          of students earning qualifying scores is the highest we've seen in 5 years. With regard to specific exams, AP
  33|                          Chemistry had the highest number of test takers in 5 years with 88% of students receiving a qualifying score
  34|                          (14 total). AP English Language and Composition had 72 test takers (an increase of 32 since 2022) and the
  35|                          highest number of exams since 2019. Finally, we had 17 AP Scholars - an AP Scholar earns a qualifying score
  36|                          on at least 3 AP exams.
  37|                          To support the expansion of AP course offerings, Monty Tech added AP Seminar as an elective course for
  38|                         juniors. Notably, 94% of students who took the AP Seminar exam in 2023 earned qualifying scores (18
  39|                          students) - outstanding results for the first year of implementation! Monty Tech continues to provide the

===PAGE 146=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|                                                 Vocational Training
   2|                         Preparing students for high-skill, high-wage careers requires technical proficiency,
   3|                        an ability to break down complex lessons into "bite sized" concepts, and a lot of
   4|                        patience. Our team of vocational instructors are not only skilled tradesmen and women, they
   5|                        patient, collaborative and talented teachers, responsible for training the next generation's workforce. Their
   6|                         commitment to student achievement, community service, and advanced technical knowledge and skills is
   7|                        unmatched, and we are so grateful that they have chosen this profession, sharing their expertise with our
   8|                         students. 2022-2023 was a busy year across the District, with students working and learning in shops, on off-
   9|                         campus construction sites, and with area business leaders, refining their skills. We are proud to highlight some
  10|                         notable accomplishments.
  11|                         Advanced Manufacturing: Advanced Manufacturing students now work and learn in a state-of-the-art
  12|                         machine shop, and may earn a mumber of industry-recognized credentials to demonstrate their technical
  13|                         capacity. All 15 Junior students completed NC3 Precision Measurement Instruments (PMI) training and earned
  14|                         6 stackable credentials in measurement. The program's 6 Seniors earned an additional 6 credentials in
  15|                         Advanced Measuring Instrument (AMI), demonstrating expertise in advanced measuring skills. All 14
  16|                         Sophomores passed MACWIC level 1, while all Freshman students successfully completed safety training,
  17|                         earning an OSHA 10-hour general industry certification. Six Seniors and eleven Juniors earned co-op
  18|                         placements, working and learning in area machine shops. (Total student enrollment: 52)
  19|                         Auto Body Collision Repair Technology: Monty Tech Auto Body students earned valuable industry
  20|                         credentials in the 2022-2023 School year. Students earned the OSHA 10 hour general industry card, EPA 6H
  21|                         spray certification, and MACS Air Conditioning Certificates. Seniors completed I-Car Pro Level One in
  22|                         Refinishing and in Non-Structural Repair. Two Seniors earned co-op placements, and were reported to be
  23|                         valuable contributors in local repairs shops. At the SkillsUSA District Competition, Sophomore students won
  24|                         Ist, 2nd & 3rd in collision repair, and one advanced to the state competitions, taking home a bronze medal.
  25|                         The program's training equipment was upgraded, with the replacement of the frame measuring system, now
  26|                        allowing students to learn on the latest technology, which will lead to more career opportunities. (Total student
  27|                         enrollment: 65)
  28|                         Automotive Technology: Ten Seniors in Automotive Technology earned their NC3/Snap On Apollo D8 Scan
  29|                         Tool Certification, and all Juniors earned the NC3/Snap on 525F Digital Multimeter certification. The
  30|                         Sophomores received the NC3 Shopkey Pro Service & Repair Information Level 2, while the program's
  31|                         Freshmen earned the OSHA 10 Safety Certification & the NC3 Shopkey Pro Service & Repair Information
  32|                         Level 1 certification. These industry-recognized credentials will demonstrate the students' level of competency
  33|                         within the automotive industry for years to come. Eight Seniors and six Juniors earned co-op placements. The

   1|                           Cooperative Education
   2|                          The Monty Tech Cooperative Education Program is an extension of the student's technical education that
   3|                         combines classroom instruction with on-the-job-training. The popular Co-op Program provides students with
   4|                         an opportunity to fither develop academic, technical and employability skills in an industry-related work
   5|                          environment. All students are eligible to participate in the Co-op Program, provided they satisfy state and
   6|                          school grade, attendance and performance requirements, and all Co-op students must have completed the
   7|                          OSHA recognized Career Safe online health and safety course prior to being placed in a work environment..
   8|                                               Students Participating in the Cooperative Education Program
   9|                           250
  10|                                                                                                                                                     236
  11|                          200                                                                                                                   184
  12|                                                                                                                            178
  13|                                                                                                                       162
