  22|                                            51
  23|                            50
  24|                                       33
  25|                                        September                 October                  December                 February                   May
  26|                          The Cooperative Education Program is beneficial for both student and employer. For the student, it is a chance
  27|                         to gain on-the-job training that improves both their technical proficiency and employability skills, and making
  28|                         industry connections enhances post-graduate employment opportunities. The Co-op Program also allows
  29|                         students to earn while they learn, which has allowed many students to learn valuable life skills, financial
  30|                         literacy skills, and gain a deeper understanding of the importance of career advancement and self-advocacy.
  31|                          Employer benefits include addressing workforce needs in an efficient manner. Co-op students provide a pool of
  32|                         trained talent, ready to assume temporary, part-time or even full-time opportunities, thus reducing employer training costs.
  33|                          Co-op work hours may also be tailored to suit the needs of partnering employers.
  34|                         In 2022-2023, the school saw a return to the workplace, and significant increases in the number of students
  35|                         placed in this valuable training program. With approximately 100 area employers participating in Cooperative
  36|                         Education, providing valuable experience to 236 students by the end of the 2022-2023 school year, school
  37|                         officials remain grateful for their continued support, and look forward to future collaborations..
  38|                                                                                              152

===PAGE 153=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|                          Student Support Services
   2|                                                             Monty Tech's Department of Student Support Services has prioritized the district's
   3|                                                                   effort to educate the whole child, ensuring the young adults we serve have
   4|                                                                     access to support in academic and vocational programming, as well as
   5|                                                                     services related to social and emotional wellness. We have focused support
   6|                                                                     around the growing concerns and struggles with age appropriate interactions,
   7|                                                                     use of social media and need for meaningful connections. Creating safe and
   8|                                                                     supported opportunities, we have embraced themes and models that challenge
   9|                                                                     our students to reflect, grow, and learn in all areas of their experiences. The
  10|                       TECH                                          department continues its important mission to break the stigma of mental
  11|                                                                   health, shifting our perspective to find balance and mental wellness. This
  12|                                                            unified approach, support, and collaboration helps our student body display
  13|                                                       resilience, grow individually and meet success.
  14|                        At Monty Tech, we strive to implement thoughtful tiered services that promote a feeling of comfort for students
  15|                        when accessing this support. In addition to the department's current school-based counseling services, Monty
  16|                        Tech and LUK, Inc. collaborated throughout 2022-2023 with the Department of Public Health to provide
  17|                        services to at-risk students and their families. The district also partnered with LUK to create a direct pipeline
  18|                        for telehealth referrals and increased access to counseling support, beyond the traditional school day. Programs
  19|                        such as the Hope Matters, a peer to peer mental wellness and suicide prevention program, Project AMP, a
  20|                        program to prevent substance abuse among youth, and Students Taking A New Direction (STAND), a program
  21|                        to support students displaying at-risk behaviors, have all been implemented successfully. In 2022-2023, Monty
  22|                        Tech also joined forces with CareSolace, an organization committed to matching individuals with mental
  23|                        health and/or substance abuse counselors. CareSolace Coordinators address the growing need to locate support
  24|                        and match individuals with therapists saving students and families hours of time, phone calls, waitlists and
  25|                        frustration. CareSolace supports the matching process and follows up with individuals to ensure each
  26|                        connection was a success. Most importantly, CareSolace Match Services are FREE to our students, family,
  27|                        staff and community. https://caresolace.com/site/montytech
  28|                        The Department of Student Support Services has taken the necessary measures to ensure Social Emotional
  29|                        Learning (SEL) is in the forefront of conversations when supporting students. Courses and activities designed
  30|                        to promote mindfulness, understanding, and social emotional wellness are part of the many course offerings
  31|                        within our school. Yoga and Meditative Art, Visual Arts, Directed Studies with Social Emotional Learning
  32|                        lessons are all part of the efforts being implemented to ensure we address the needs of all students and support
  33|                        their individual growth and development.
  34|                        During the 2022-2023 school year, Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District provided
  35|                        specialized services to approximately three hundred students - measuring progress of over two hundred
  36|                        students on Individual Education Plans (IEPs) and approximately one hundred students adhering to
  37|                        individualized Section 504 plans. While the Student Support Services Department encompasses special
  38|                        education, the department provides support that is available to all Monty Tech students, who have access to
  39|                        direct tutoring both before and after school. School counselors are available to assist students with vocational
  40|                        placement and post-secondary planning, and are equipped with the skills to engage students in workshops and
  41|                        groups that support the development of social resilience and finding balance, for those experiencing struggles.
  42|                        The Student Support Services department includes a full-time nursing staff that continues to respond to CDC
  43|                        advisories and noted trends, administers medications, performs state-mandated health screening exams, and
  44|                                                                                              153

===PAGE 154=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|                        provides, when necessary, health information to the special education team for a student's IEP meetings. The
   2|                        department benetits from a full-time school social worker that participates in departmental meetings and assists
   3|                        students who have needs concerning finances, family issues, homelessness, maternity, health issues, proper
   4|                        food and clothing, and military status deployments. The school is also fortunate to have on statt a full-time
   5|                        psychologist, who evaluates all students referred for an initial evaluation or who may require a three-year re-
   6|                        evaluation. In addition, we have a full-time speech language pathologist, available to assist students with
   7|                        disabilities, assess these students and consult with teachers. Finally, our students also have access to the
   8|                        services of a full-time adjustment counselor and part-time school psychologist. All of these individuals are
   9|                        available for scheduled counseling sessions, mental health emergency treatment, medical/mental health re-
  10|                        entry and transition support services, as well as crisis intervention.
  11|                                       Technology @ Monty Tech
  12|                         Technology and learning go hand-in-hand at Monty Tech, and 2022-2023 was no exception. Monty Tech's
  13|                        talented Technology Department led the transition to a new Student Information System - PowerSchool. The
  14|                        new system, coupled with the school's previous adoption of Performance Matters and Google Classroom has
  15|                        led to increased communications between school and home. For teachers, student outcomes are easier to track
  16|                        and analyze, while noted accomplishments and areas of concern can be brought to the attention of
  17|                        parents/ guardians quite easily through these new technologies. The Director of Technology, School Principal,
  18|                        and Director of Academic Programs traveled to Nashville, TN to participate in training so that they could
  19|                        provide in-house support and important solutions, strategies, and techniques to the Monty Tech faculty and
  20|                        staff who would be new to this comprehensive new educational tool
  21|                         The Technology Department was also called upon to work with the Dean's Office
  22|                        in an effort to mitigate vaping issues. Verkada vape sensors were installed,
  23|                        notitying the Deans and security personnel in the event a sensor is
  24|                        activated. Student safety efforts are ongoing and remain a priority for the
  25|                        technology team, who also installed a comprehensive guest registration
  26|                        system, which uses the guest's state issued ID to run a preliminary
  27|                        background check on any guest entering the school before he/she may be
  28|                        admitted. Interactive learning displays, all new computers in vocational
  29|                        areas, new fiber lines, and an upgraded phone system rounded out the
  30|                        team's 2022-2023 efforts, resulting in a more efficient, more secure
  31|                        working and learning environment for all.
  32|                        Finally, Department personnel was expanded to include a second Educational
  33|                        Technologist to the team. The two technologists provide valuable, personalized instruction to both academic
  34|                        and vocational teachers, as they expand their instructional delivery models and rely on available technology to
  35|                         provide and deliver important school-to-home communications.
  36|                                                                                              154

===PAGE 155=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|                                       Service Learning
   2|                                                 The Monty Tech Marine Corps Junior Reserve Officer Training Corps (MCJROTC), is a
   3|                                                 leadership program based on the foundations of instilling the values of citizenship, service,
   4|                                                 personal responsibility, and a sense of accomplishment. The program provides students
   5|                                                 with countless opportunities to serve as ambassadors in their communities, through the
   6|                                                 routine demonstration of student leadership development, volunteerism, perseverance, and
   7|                                                 dedication.
   8|                      ANIOR                      During the 2022 - 2023 school year, Monty Tech MCJROTC Cadets participated in more
   9|                       than 2,000 hours of community service and volunteerism. Cadets began the busy school year supporting two
  10|                       separate running events - the Fitchburg Veterans 5K and the Rock n Roll 5K at the Wachusett Brewery-
  11|                       providing assistance with vehicle parking, run route coordination, and vendor support. Another community
  12|                       event that was a highlight for our students was the Annual Blacksmith Festival held in Fitchburg, MA. Monty
  13|                       Tech cadets assisted competitors and vendors, and while doing so, learned a great deal about community
  14|                       volunteerism and the craft of blacksmithing as a fine art.
  15|                       Cadets were able to sponsor and participate in a Veterans Day and Marine Corps Birthday Celebration. This
  16|                       event included a full USMC Birthday ceremony, marching in the USMC birthday cake cutting ceremony, and
  17|                       recognition of the guest of honor, Mr. Matt LaRose.
  18|                       As in years past, Cadets were actively involved with assisting the Salvation Army, and proudly collected more
  19|                       than $98,000 in donations throughout the 2023 holiday season. Their efforts greatly contributed to the much
  20|                       needed support of those less fortunate throughout the Monty Tech district. Cadets were also invited to
  21|                       participate in the Gardner Centennial Parade celebrating its 100th year as a city, and were asked to provide
  22|                        crowd and traffic control along various places of the parade route.
  23|                       Assisting with color guards, presenting the Nation's Colors and the Marine Corps Battle Colors, is a particular
  24|                       honor and highlight for our young cadets. In 2022-2023, these special events were numerous, with as many
  25|                       as fifteen color guards to include all of the Monty Tech home football games, Fitchburg State
  26|                       graduate and undergraduate ceremonies, local civic ceremonies, as well as numerous
  27|                       veteran ceremonies in and around veteran holidays.
  28|                       Finally, more than 150 Monty Tech cadets made their way to Fall River to
  29|                       experience Battleship Row. During this visit, cadets were able to walk on and
  30|                       around the USS Massachusetts and the maritime museum. Visits to local
  31|                       historic attractions are one of the many reasons cadets pursue this leadership
  32|                       program. In the Summer of 2023, 70 additional cadets travelled to Prince
  33|                       William Forest Campground located in Triangle, VA for a weeklong
  34|                       leadership trip that included team building skills, physical fitness, and visits
  35|                       to local area attractions. Additionally, 15                                cadets attended          three
  36|                       separate leadership trips where they learned basic aviation, Cyber/Stem awareness,
  37|                       and advanced leadership techniques. These week-long leadership trips took place in
  38|                       Boswell, PA and Fork Union, VA.
  39|                                                                                              155

===PAGE 156=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|                                           Evening Programo
   2|                        In recent years, Monty Tech's popular School of Continuing Education, has been the recipient of more than
   3|                        $1.5M in funding from Commonwealth Corporation, to provide unemployed or underemployed individuals
   4|                        with no-cost training and job placement services in the region's most critical industries/occupations. These
   5|                        grant-funded training opportunities are an example of the collaboration between the MassHire Workforce
   6|                       Investment Board, the Executive Office of Labor and Workforce Development, and generous sponsorship from
   7|                        Commonwealth Corporation, making it possible for Monty Tech to offer meaningful job
   8|                       readiness training in occupations with high demand and self-sustaining wages.
   9|                        In 2022-2023, school leaders were pleased to offer 200 hour training
  10|                       programs in culinary, electrical, CNC operation, and welding, at no cost to
  11|                        qualified participants. These FY23 training cohorts resulted in 33
  12|                        individuals having secured full-time employment in their chosen
  13|                        occupations. In addition to these grant-funded opportunities, the School of
  14|                        Continuing Education, continues to offer the area's most affordable and
  15|                        comprehensive cosmetology training program, effectively preparing
  16|                        participants to sit for the Massachusetts State Board examinations. In FY23,
  17|                       four cosmetology students graduated from the program, and all have passed
  18|                        the licensing exam.
  19|                        Recognizing a need to support social emotional wellness of our non-traditional
  20|                        students, many of whom would be returning to a classroom after many years of not participating in academic
  21|                        endeavors, program staff initiated a creative and beneficial monthly "Mental Health Breaktime," providing
  22|                        with food and community resources for enrolled students. This was a well-attended addition to the evening
  23|                        program, and is expected to become a regularly scheduled event in the coming years.
  24|                        Finally, Monty Tech held its annual public job fair on May 4, 2023. The event was extremely well-attended,
  25|                        with more than sixty (60) area employers visiting on-site with both day and evening students seeking job
  26|                        opportunities. The strength of any Monty Tech training program is dependent upon mutually beneficial
  27|                       relationships between the school and our business partners. Serving more than 1,400 community members
  28|                        annually, the School of Continuing Education is proud to offer meaningful training to a non-traditional student
  29|                        population, while working to develop new and important relationships with business partners in need of a
  30|                       talented candidate pool.
  31|                        The Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School Practical Nursing Program strives to create and
  32|                        provide a high-quality educational experience to each individual student seeking to establish a successful
  33|                        career in nursing. 2023 marked the Practical Nursing Programs silver anniversary. Since its inception, the
  34|                        Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School Practical Nursing Program has graduated more than 700
  35|                        nursing professionals.
  36|                        Our program stresses the importance of developing the clinical judgement and skills needed to function safely,
  37|                        ettectively, and productively in an ever changing technical and diverse society. By providing our students with
  38|                        an exceptional nursing education, we graduate outstanding healthcare professionals with the ability to make a
  39|                        difference for our local communities and beyond.
  40|                        Nurses at all levels play a vital role in providing care and compassion to patients in need. Monty Tech Practical
  41|                        Nursing Program graduates continue to seek admission to the "LPN to BSN (Bachelor of Science in Nursing)
  42|                                                                                              156

===PAGE 157=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                        Bridge" with Fitchburg State University (ESU). This opportunity provides a seamless pathway for our
   2|                        graduates to enhance their clinical skills, deepen their theoretical knowledge, and embrace leadership roles in
   3|                        the nursing profession.
   4|                                                  The graduating class of 2023 volunteered for the United Way Days of Caring. The student
   5|                                                        nurses engaged in various community activities including painting playgrounds and
   6|                                                               community buildings, as well as neighborhood and garden clean-ups. The
   7|                                                                 collaboration between student nurses and United Way exemplifies the spirit of
   8|                                                                altruism and the pursuit of practical knowledge in service to society. It's a
   9|                                                                reciprocal relationship enriching both the students' learning experiences and the
  10|                                                                 communities served.
  11|                                                                 On June 22, 2023, a class of 30 students graduated ready to enter the nursing
  12|                                                                profession. The class of 2023 has achieved a 90% pass rate on the NCLEX-PN
  13|                                                               exam (National Council Licensure Examination for Practical Nurses). The 2023
  14|                                                       program graduates are currently employed working in various health care settings, such
  15|                                                  as long-term care, sub-acute care, acute care, acute rehabilitation care, mental
  16|                                           health/substance abuse facilities, physicians' offices, correctional medicine, pediatric extended
  17|                        care, and school nursing.
  18|                                                    Looking Ahead
  19|                        Monty Tech is known for its innovation and creative programs that prepare young people for high skill and
  20|                        high wage job opportunities. While we are so proud of our annual accomplishments, school leaders maintain
  21|                        an eye to the future - always seeking improvement. School leaders are confident that our efforts to develop
  22|                        and maintain a responsible local budget, coupled with our pursuit of competitive grant funding will ensure ou
  23|                        training programs stay current and aligned with industry standards. Our relationships with area business
  24|                        leaders, community service agencies and post-secondary partners will improve student outcomes, whether our
  25|                        graduates choose college or career pathways upon graduation. Our endeavors, as the region's leading
  26|                        workforce training provider, to increase access to valuable training opportunities remain a top priority. A few
  27|                        of the more notable initiatives that we expect to have a positive impact on our school and students in the
  28|                        coming years include:
  29|                        New partnerships to increase access to vocational programs: In FY23, Monty Tech
  30|                        proudly entered into a partnership with Fitchburg Public Schools, Gardner Public
  31|                        Schools, and Narragansett Regional School District, in an effort to provide
  32|                        students in those districts with meaningful vocational training opportunities.
  33|                        Monty Tech found and secured a vacant property close to campus, and was
  34|                        awarded a record-setting $1.88M grant to renovate and equip the tacility,
  35|                        turning it into a state-of-the-art training center for carpentry, electrical and
  36|                        plumbing occupations. Students and instructors are tasked with building
  37|                        out the new training spaces throughout the 2023-2024 school year, so that                                           MVP
  38|                        doors can open to the Montachusett Vocational Partnership (MVP) Academy                                             ACADEMY
  39|                        in September 2024. School leaders will continue to promote the innovative
  40|                        partnership and modified "After Dark" schedule, as a model program that, if
  41|                        funded properly, could satisfy concerns for lack of access to vocational
  42|                        programming across the Commonwealth. Monty Tech officials look forward to
  43|                                                                                              157

===PAGE 158=== (ocr, 31 lines)
   1|                         communicating with local and state leaders, advocating for increased funding for such endeavors, and building
   2|                         upon this exceptional partnership that is expected to serves as many as 125 students when all programs are in
   3|                         operation.
   4|                         Grant funding to improve and expand training programs: In FY23, Monty Tech applied for its ninth Skills
   5|                         Capital Grant, in the amount of $500,000. The school's Development Coordinator worked closely with
   6|                         Culinary Arts instructors to craft a comprehensive proposal that, if funded, will bring much needed
   7|                        improvements to the program. School leaders will be able to purchase commercial kitchen equipment that is
   8|                        updated, more efficient, and reflects the same equipment used in our partner's kitchens, and to purchase a food
   9|                        truck, which will allow instructors to develop all-new curricula in street food, quick service and community
  10|                         service opportunities. All equipment will be used to improve the existing day program, and to establish all-
  11|                        new, 100-hour evening training programs for adult learners. School officials look forward to award
  12|                        notification in 2023-2024 school year, with enhancements to the program beginning Fall 2024.
  13|                            Manty Tech Leadership
  14|                         The district continues to benefit from a talented leadership team whose varied educational
  15|                         backgrounds, professional experiences, and areas of expertise contribute to the success of the school.
  16|                         Working collaboratively, and under the direction of the Superintendent, the team has transformed the
  17|                         school into one of the most sought-after high schools in North Central Massachusetts.

  36|             ARTICLE 21. To see if the Town will vote to authorize the Select Board, on behalf of the Town, to peti-
  37|             tion the General Court for the enactment of special legislation substantially as provided below, provided that
  38|             the General Court, with advance approval by the Select Board, which is hereby authorized, may reasonably
  39|             vary the form and substance of the requested legislation within the scope of the general public objectives of the
  40|             petition:
  41|             AN ACT AUTHORIZING THE TOWN OF LUNENBURG TO ESTABLISH A MEANS TESTED SENIOR
  42|             CITIZEN PROPERTY TAX EXEMPTION.
  43|             SECTION 1. With respect to each qualifying parcel of real property classified as class one, residential in the
  44|             town of Lunenburg, there shall be an exemption from the property tax in an amount to be set annually by the
  45|             board of assessors as provided in section 3. The exemption shall be applied to the domicile of the taxpayer on-
  46|             ly. For the purposes of this act, "parcel" shall mean a unit of real property as defined by the board of assessors
  47|             under the deed for the property and shall include a condominium unit. The exemption provided for herein shall
  48|             be in addition to any other exemptions allowed under the General Laws.
  49|                                                                                              170

===PAGE 171=== (ocr, 47 lines)
   1|             SECTION 2. The board of assessors of the town of Lunenburg may deny an application for exemption if the
   2|             board finds that an applicant has excessive assets that place the applicant outside of the intended recipients of
   3|             the senior citizen tax exemption created by this act. Real property shall qualify for the exemption under section
   4|             1 if all of the following criteria are met:
   5|                              1. The qualifying real property is owned and occupied by an Applicant who has applied for and
   6|                              been granted the State's Senior Circuit Breaker Tax Credit;
   7|                              2. Each Applicant named in an application who has attained the eligibility age of
   8|                              70 years for an elderly exemption in Luneburg under M.G.L. Chapter 59, Section 5, Clause 41(C)
   9|                              must have applied for and been granted this exemption;
  10|                              3. Each Applicant named in an application has attained the age of 65 years by the close of the cal-
  11|                              endar year immediately preceding the year of application;
  12|                              4. The Applicant has owned and occupied a home in the Town of Lunenburg, as his or her primary
  13|                              residence, for at least 15 consecutive years pre-dating and including the date of application;
  14|                              5. The Applicant has demonstrated to the satisfaction of the Lunenburg Board of Assessors that (s)
  15|                              he, and each co-applicant, owns no other significant assets such as, by way of nonlimiting example,
  16|                              a second home, a trust fund, securities, equities, and bonds.
  17|                              6. The amount of relief granted to each Applicant under this program shall not exceed 100 percent
  18|                              of the Circuit Breaker Tax Credit for which the Applicant qualified in the previous year.
  19|             SECTION 3. The board of assessors of the town of Lunenburg shall annually set the exemption amount pro-
  20|             vided for in section 1 relative; provided, however, that the amount of the exemption shall not exceed the
  21|             amount of the circuit breaker income tax credit pursuant to subsection (k) of section 6 of chapter 62 of the
  22|             General Laws for which the applicant qualified in the previous year. The total amount exempted by this act
  23|             shall be allocated proportionally within the tax levy on all residential taxpayers and shall not exceed 1 percent
  24|             of the town-wide real property tax levy. To the extent that the total value of exemption requests timely re-
  25|             ceived by the board of assessors exceeds 1 percent of the levy, the exemption amount for which each applicant
  26|             has otherwise established eligibility shall be proportionally reduced by multiplying the established eligibility
  27|             amount by a quotient equal to 1 percent of the levy divided by the total town-wide dollar value of exemption
  28|             requests for which Applicants in the aggregate would be eligible in the absence of the cap represented by 1
  29|             percent of the levy.
  30|             SECTION 4. A person who seeks to quality for the exemption under section 1 shall, betore the deadlıne estab-
  31|             lished by the board of assessors of the town of Lunenburg, tile an application, on a form to be adopted by the
  32|             board of assessors, with the supporting documentation of the applicant's income and assets as described in the
  33|             application. An applicant must submit an application, and demonstrate eligibility, for each year for which the
  34|             applicant seeks the exemption.
  35|             SECTION 5. No exemption shall be granted under this act until the department of revenue certifies a residen-
  36|             tial tax rate for the applicable tax year where the total exemption amount is raised by a burden shift within the
  37|             residential tax levy.
  38|             SECTION 6. This act may be revoked by an affirmative vote of a majority of town meeting voters at any annu-
  39|             al or special town meeting. Any such revocation shall take effect 30 days after an affirmative vote of town
  40|             meeting. or take any other action relative thereto. (Submitted by the Town Manager)
  41|             FINANCE COMMITTEE RECOMMENDATION: Recommend Approval 4-1
  42|             SELECT BOARD RECOMMENDATION: Recommend Approval 4-0 VOTED (77 YES, 11 NO) [with
  43|             amendment (passing 74 to9) to eliminate "residential" in the 2"' sentence of Section 3] to authorize the
  44|             Board of Selectmen, on behalf of the Town, to petition the General Court for the enactment of special
  45|             legislation substantially as printed in the Annual Town Meeting Booklet to authorize the Town of
  46|             Lunenburg to establish a means tested senior citizen property tax exemption.

  33|             ARTICLE 9. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Code of Lunenburg, Section 250-4.9, which is a
  34|             Section of the Protective Bylaw entitled "Water Supply Protection District," by deleting the same in its entirety
  35|             and by substituting therefor a new Section 250-4.9, as follows:
  36|             250-4.9 WATER SUPPLY PROTECTION DISTRICT
  37|             1. PURPOSE OF DISTRICT
  38|                   The purpose of this Water Supply Protection District is to:
  39|                  promote the health, safety, and general welfare of the community by ensuring an adequate quality and quantity of drinking water
  40|                   for the Town of Lunenburg.
  41|             • preserve and protect existing and potential sources of drinking water;
  42|             • conserve natural resources in the Town of Lunenburg; and
  43|             • prevent temporary and permanent contamination of the environment.
  44|                                                                                              173

===PAGE 174=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|             SCOPE OF AUTHORITY
   2|                   The Water Supply Protection District is an overlay district superimposed on the zoning districts. This overlay dis-
   3|                   trict shall apply to all new construction, reconstruction, or expansion of existing buildings and new or expanded us-
   4|                  es. Applicable activities and uses in a portion of one of the underlying zoning districts that fall within the Water
   5|                  Supply Protection District must additionally comply with the requirements of this bylaw. Uses prohibited in the un-
   6|                  derlying zoning districts shall not be permitted in the Water Supply Protection District.
   7|             DEFINITIONS
   8|                   Automobile Graveyard: An establishment that is maintained, used, or operated for storing, keeping, buying, or sell-
   9|                  ing wrecked, scrapped, ruined, or motor vehicle parts as defined in MGL c.140B, s.1.
  10|                   Aquifer: A geologic formation composed of rock, sand or gravel that contains significant amounts of potentially re-
  11|                   coverable water.
  12|                   CMR: Code of Massachusetts Regulations.
  13|                  Commercial Fertilizer: Any substance containing one or more recognized plant nutrients which is used for its plant
  14|                  nutrient content and which is designed for use, or claimed to have value in promoting plant growth, except un-
  15|                   manipulated animal and vegetable manures, marl, lime, limestone, wood ashes, and gypsum, and other products ex-
  16|                   empted by state regulations.
  17|                   Discharge: The accidental or intentional disposal, deposit, injection, dumping, spilling, leaking, pouring, or placing
  18|                   of toxic or hazardous material or hazardous waste upon or into any land or water such that it may enter the surface or
  19|                   ground waters.
  20|                   Dry Well: A subsurface pit with open-jointed lining or holes through which storm-water drainage from roofs, base-
  21|                   ment floors, foundations or other areas seep into the surrounding soil.
  22|                  Water Supply Protection District: The land area consisting of aquifers, Zone II recharge areas, and Zone IIl areas, all
  23|                   ass approved by MassDEP and identified on a map and adopted pursuant to this bylaw.
  24|                   Hazardous Material: Any substance in any form which because of its quantity, concentration, or its chemical, corro-
  25|                   sive, flammable, reactive, toxic, infectious or radioactive characteristics, either separately or in combination with one
  26|                   or more substances, constitutes a present or potential threat to human health, safety, welfare, or to the environment,
  27|                  when improperly stored, treated, transported, disposed of, used, or otherwise managed. Hazardous material includes,
  28|                   without limitation, synthetic organic chemicals, petroleum products, heavy metals, radioactive or infectious materi-
  29|                   als, and all substances defined as toxic or hazardous under MGL c. 21E. This term shall not include hazardous
  30|                   waste or oil.
  31|                  Historical High Groundwater Table Elevation: A groundwater elevation determined from monitoring wells and his-
  32|                   torical water table fluctuation data compiled by the United States Geological Survey.
  33|                   Hazardous Waste: A substance or combination of substances, which because of quantity, concentration, or physical,
  34|                  chemical or infectious characteristics may cause, or significantly contribute to an increase in mortality or an increase
  35|                  in serious irreversible, or incapacitating reversible illness or pose a substantial present or potential hazard to human
  36|                  health, safety, or welfare or to the environment when improperly treated, stored, transported, used or disposed of, or
  37|                  otherwise managed. This term shall include all substances identified as hazardous pursuant to the Hazardous Waste
  38|                   Regulations, 310 CMR 30.000.
  39|                   Impervious Surface: Material or structure on, above, or below the ground that does not allow precipitation or surface
  40|                   water runoff to penetrate into the soil.
  41|                  Interim Wellhead Protection Area (IWPA): The MassDEP designated protection radius around a public water well
  42|                   that lacks a Zone II.
  43|                                                                                              174

===PAGE 175=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                  Junkyard: An establishment that is maintained, operated, or used for storing, keeping, buying, or selling junk, or for
   2|                  the maintenance or operation of an automobile graveyard, as defined in MGL c.140B, s.1.
   3|                  Landfill: A facility established in accordance with a valid site assignment for the purposes of disposing solid waste
   4|                  into or on the land, pursuant to the Solid Waste Regulations, 310 CMR 19.006.
   5|                   MassDEP: Massachusetts Department of Environmental Protection.
   6|                  MGL: Massachusetts General Law.
   7|                  Petroleum Product: Includes, but not limited to, fuel oil; gasoline; diesel; kerosene; aviation jet fuel; aviation gaso-
   8|                  line; lubricating oils; oily sludge; oil refuse; oil mixed with other wastes; crude oils; or other liquid hydrocarbons
   9|                  regardless of specific gravity. Petroleum product shall not include liquefied petroleum gas including, but not limited
  10|                   to, liquefied natural gas, propane or butane.
  11|                   Non-Sanitary Wastewater: Wastewater discharges from industrial and commercial facilities containing wastes from
  12|                  any activity other than collection of sanitary sewage including, but not limited to, activities specified in 310 CMR
  13|                   15.004(6).
  14|                  Open Dump: A facility operated or maintained in violation of the Resource Conservation and Recovery Act 42
  15|                  U.S.C. 4004(a)(b), or state regulations and criteria for solid waste disposal.
  16|                   Recharge Areas: Land areas, such as a Zone II or an Interim Wellhead Protection Area, where precipitation and sur-
  17|                  face water infiltrates into the ground to replenish groundwater and aquifers used for public drinking water supplies.
  18|                   Septage: The liquid, solid, and semi-solid contents of privies, chemical toilets, cesspools, holding tanks, or other
  19|                  sewage waste receptacles. This term shall not include any material that is a hazardous waste, as defined by 310 CMR
  20|                   30.000.
  21|                   Sludge: The solid, semi-solid, and liquid residue that results from a process of wastewater treatment or drinking wa-
  22|                  ter treatment including wastewater residuals. This term shall not include grit, screening, or grease and oil which are
  23|                  removed at the headworks of a facility.
  24|                  Treatment Works: Any and all devices, processes and properties, real or personal, used in the collection, pumping,
  25|                   transmission, storage, treatment, disposal, recycling, reclamation, or reuse of waterborne pollutants, but not includ-
  26|                   ing any works receiving a hazardous waste from off the site of the works for the purpose of treatment, storage, or
  27|                   disposal.
  28|                   Utility Works: Regulated activities providing for public services, including roads, water, sewer, electricity, gas, tele-
  29|                   phone, transportation and their associated maintenance activities. This term shall include the installation of detention
  30|                  and retention basins for the purpose of controlling storm water.
  31|                   Very Small Quantity Generator: Any public or private entity, other than residential, which produces less than 27 gal-
  32|                  lons (100 kilograms) a month of hazardous waste or waste oil, but not including any acutely hazardous waste as de-
  33|                   fined in 310 CMR 30.136.
  34|                   Waste Oil Retention Facility: A waste oil collection facility for automobile service stations, retail outlets, and mari-
  35|                  nas which is sheltered and has adequate protection to contain a spill, seepage, or discharge of petroleum waste prod-
  36|                   ucts in accordance with MGL c.21. s.52A..
  37|                  Zone II: The delineated recharge area to a public drinking water well as approved by MassDEP and defined under
  38|                  the Massachusetts Drinking Water Regulations 310 CMR 22.00.
  39|                  Zone III: An area beyond a Zone II recharge area, as approved by MassDEP and defined under the Massachusetts
  40|                   Drinking Water Regulations 310 CMR 22.00.
  41|                                                                                              175

===PAGE 176=== (ocr, 35 lines)
   1|             ESTABLISHMENT AND DELINEATION OF WATER SUPPLY PROTECTION DISTRICT
   2|                   For the purposes of this bylaw, there are hereby established within the Town of Lunenburg_certain water supply pro-
   3|                   tection areas consisting of aquifers or recharge areas (Zone II) and certain protected areas beyond those Zone II areas
   4|                  (Zone III). These areas are delineated on a map entitled Lunenburg Water District A quifer Protection Zones dated
   5|                  July 2021 which is hereby made part of the Water Supply Protection District Bylaw and is on file in the office of the
   6|                   Town Clerk.
   7|              DISTRICT BOUNDARY DISPUTES
   8|                   If the location of the Water Supply Protection District in relation to a particular parcel is in doubt, resolution of the
   9|                   boundary dispute shall be through a Special Permit application to the Special Permit Granting Authority. Any appli-
  10|                   cation for a special permit for this purpose shall be accompanied by adequate documentation.
  11|                   Burden of proof shall be upon the land owner to demonstrate that the location of the Water Supply Protection Dis-
  12|                  trict with respect to a particular parcel(s) of land is uncertain. At the request of the land owner, the Town may en-
  13|                   gage a professional engineer, hydrologist, geologist, or soil scientist to determine more accurately the boundaries of
  14|                  the Water Supply Protection District with respect to a particular parcel(s) of land, and may charge the owner for the
  15|                  cost of the investigation. Changes to the Water Supply Protection District require town meeting approval. Changes
  16|                  to the Zone II boundary requires approval from the MassDEP.
  17|                   Where the Zone II boundary line of the Water Supply Protection District divides a lot or parcel, the requirements es-
  18|                  tablished by this bylaw shall apply to the land 20 feet beyond the Zone II boundary.
  19|              PERMITTED USES
  20|                   The following uses are permitted within the Water Supply Protection District, provided that all necessary permits,
  21|                  orders, or approvals required by local, state, or federal law are also obtained:
  22|             • conservation of soil, water, plants, and wildlife;
  23|             • outdoor recreation, nature study, boating, fishing, and hunting where otherwise legally permitted;
  24|             • foot, bicycle and/or horse paths, and bridges;
  25|             • normal operation and maintenance of existing water bodies and dams, splash boards, and other water control, supply
  26|                  and conservation devices;
  27|             • maintenance, repair, and enlargement of any existing structure, subject to Section 7 and Section 8 of this bylaw;
  28|             • residential development, subject to Sections 7 and 8 of this bylaw;
  29|             • farming, gardening, nursery, conservation, forestry, harvesting, and grazing, subject to Section 7 and Section 8 of this
  30|                  bylaw;
  31|             • construction, maintenance, repair, and enlargement of drinking water supply related facilities such as, but not limited
  32|                  to, wells, pipelines, aqueducts, and tunnels; and
  33|               any use permitted in the underlying zoning by-right or by special permit, except for those uses specifically prohibited
  34|                  in Sections 7 and 8 of this bylaw.
  35|                                                                                              176

===PAGE 177=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|             PROHIBITED USES
   2|                   The following land uses and activities are prohibited in Zone II unless designed in accordance with the specified per-
   3|                   formance standards:
   4|             • landfills and open dumps;
   5|             • automobile graveyards and junkyards;
   6|               landfills receiving only wastewater residuals and/or septage, including those approved by MassDEP pursuant to
   7|                   MGL c. 21 s.26 through s.53, MGL c.111 s.17, and MGL c.83 s.6 and s.7;
   8|                  facilities that generate, treat, store, or dispose of hazardous waste that are subject to MGL c.21C and 310 CMR
   9|                   30.000, except for:
  10|               very small quantity generators as defined under 310 CMR 30.000;
  11|             • household hazardous waste centers and events under 310 CMR 30.390;
  12|             • waste oil retention facilities required by MGL c. 21, s.52A; and
  13|             • water remediation treatment works approved by MassDEP for the treatment of contaminated waters.
  14|             • petroleum, fuel oil, and heating oil bulk stations and terminals including, but not limited to, those listed under North
  15|                   American Industry Classification System (NAICS) Codes 424710 and 454311, except for liquefied petroleum gas.
  16|             • storage of liquid hazardous materials and/or liquid petroleum products unless such storage is above ground level and
  17|                   on an impervious surface and either:
  18|             • in container(s) or above ground tank(s) within a building; or
  19|             • outdoors in covered container(s) or above ground tank(s) in an area that has a containment system designed and op-
  20|                  erated to hold either; 10% of the total possible storage capacity of all containers or 110% of the largest container's
  21|                   storage capacity, whichever is greater.
  22|                              however, these storage requirements shall not apply to the replacement of existing tanks or systems for the
  23|                              keeping, dispensing or storing of gasoline provided the replacement is performed in a manner consistent
  24|                              with state and local requirements;
  25|             • storage of sludge and septage, unless such storage is in compliance with 310 CMR 32.30 and 310 CMR 32.31;
  26|             • storage of deicing chemicals, with the exception of small quantities stored in containers and ready for application,
  27|                  unless such storage, is within a structure designed to prevent the generation and escape of contaminated runoff or
  28|                  leachate
  29|                storage of animal manure, with the exception of small quantities stored in containers and ready for application, un-
  30|                  less contained within a structure designed to prevent the generation and escape of contaminated runoff or leachate;
  31|             • storage of commercial fertilizers, with the exception of small quantities stored in containers and ready for applica-
  32|                  tion, unless such storage is within a structure designed to prevent the generation and escape of contaminated runoff
  33|                   or leachate;
  34|             • stockpiling and disposal of snow and ice containing deicing chemicals brought in from outside the Water Supply
  35|                   Protection District;
  36|                                                                                              177

===PAGE 178=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|             • earth removal, consisting of the removal of soil, loam, sand, gravel, or any other earth material to within 4 feet of
   2|                  historical high Water Supply as determined from monitoring wells and historical water table fluctuation data com-
   3|                  piled by the United States Geological Survey, except for excavations for building foundations, roads, utility works or
   4|                  wetland restoration work conducted in accordance with a valid Order of Condition issued pursuant to MGL c. 131,
   5|                  s.40; and
   6|             • treatment or disposal works subject to 314 CMR 5.00, for non-sanitary wastewater, including those activities listed
   7|                  under 310 CMR 15.004(6), except for:
   8|             1. treatment works approved by MassDEP designed for the treatment of contaminated ground or surface water and op-
   9|                   erating in compliance with 314 CMR 5.05(3) or 5.05(13); and
  10|             2. publicly owned treatment works.
  11|              USES AND ACTIVITIES REQUIRING A SPECIAL PERMIT
  12|                  In Zone II the following uses and activities are permitted only upon the issuance of a Special Permit by the Special
  13|                  Permit Granting Authority (SPGA) under such conditions as they may require:
  14|             1. enlargement or alteration of existing uses that do not conform to the Water Supply Protection District;
  15|             2. except as prohibited under Section 7 of this bylaw, activities that involve the handling of toxic or hazardous materials
  16|                   in quantities greater than those associated with normal household use and which are permitted in the underlying zon-
  17|                  ing district;
  18|             3. rendering impervious any lot or parcel more than 15% or 2,500 square feet, whichever is greater; unless artificial
  19|                  recharge, that will not degrade water quality, is provided using methods demonstrated to be capable of removing
  20|                  contaminants from storm water and which are consistent with methods described in MassDEP's Stormwater Hand-
  21|                   book, Vol. I, Il and III, as amended.
  22|             4. In Zone III, the aforementioned uses and activities are permitted only upon the issuance of Site Plan Approval by the
  23|                   Planning Board under such conditions as they may reasonably require. Site Plan Approval will be granted, with the
  24|                  input of the Building Commissioner, Board of Health, Conservation Commission, and Department of Public Works,
  25|                  that the intent of the Bylaw, as well as its specific criteria, are met. The submitted Site Plan Approval application
  26|                  will comply with the requirements of Section 9.5 of this Bylaw. If there are material changes to the categories of po-
  27|                  tentially hazardous materials to be used or stored on the premises in quantities greater than those associated with nor-
  28|                  mal household use after Site Plan Approval is granted, the owner/operator will file updated information on those cat-
  29|                  egories of hazardous materials with the Fire Department, Board of Health, and the Planning Board.
  30|              PROCEDURES FOR ISSUANCE OF SPECIAL PERMIT
  31|             The Special Permit Granting Authority (SPGA) under this bylaw shall be the Planning Board.
  32|             1. A special permit shall be granted if the SPGA determines, with advisement from the Lunenburg Water District,
  33|                   Building Commissioner, Conservation Commission, Department of Public Works, and Board of Health, that the intent of
  34|                  this bylaw, as well as its specific criteria, are met. The SPGA shall not grant a special permit under this section un-
  35|                  less the petitioner's application materials include, in the SPA's opinion, sufficiently detailed, definite, and credible
  36|                  information to support positive findings in relation to the standards given in this section.
  37|             2. The SPGA shall document the basis for any departures from the recommendations of the other municipal boards, de-
  38|                   partments or commissions in its decision.
  39|                                                                                              178

===PAGE 179=== (ocr, 34 lines)
   1|             3. The SPGA may grant the required special permit upon finding that the proposed use meets the following standards,
   2|                  those specified in Section 7 of this bylaw, and any regulations or guidelines adopted by the SPGA. The proposed use
   3|                   must:
   4|                  in no way, during construction or thereafter, materially adversely affect the quality or quantity of the water supplies
   5|                              protected by the Water Supply Protection District; and
   6|                   be designed to avoid substantial disturbance of the soils, topography, drainage, vegetation, and other water-related
   7|                              natural characteristics of the site to be developed, except as depicted on an approved site plan.
   8|             4. The SPGA may adopt controls to govern design features of projects. Such controls shall be consistent with the Town's
   9|                   subdivision regulations, Site Plan Approval, and Stormwater Bylaws.
  10|             5. The applicant shall file 1 0 copies of a site plan and attachments. The site plan shall be drawn at a proper scale as de-
  11|                  termined by the SPGA and be stamped by a professional engineer. All additional submittals shall be prepared by
  12|                  qualified professionals. The site plan and all supporting materials must meet the requirements outlined in the Plan-
  13|                  ning Board Rules & Regulations for Site Plan Approval and Special Permit and at a minimum include the following
  14|                  information where pertinent:
  15|                a complete list of the categories of chemicals, pesticides, herbicides, fertilizers, fuels, and other potentially hazardous
  16|                   materials to be used or stored on the premises in quantities greater than those associated with normal household use;
  17|             • for activities using or storing hazardous materials or wastes, a management plan shall be prepared and filed with the
  18|                   Fire Chief and the Board of Health. The plan will be consistent with the requirements of Section 7 and shall include:
  19|             • provisions to protect against the discharge of hazardous materials or wastes to the environment due to spillage, acci-
  20|                   dental damage, corrosion, leakage, or vandalism, including spill containment and clean-up procedures;
  21|             • provisions for indoor, secured storage of hazardous materials or wastes with impervious floor surfaces;
  22|             • evidence of compliance with the Massachusetts Hazardous Waste Regulations 310 CMR 30.000; and
  23|             • proposed down-gradient location(s) for groundwater monitoring wells), should the SPGA deem the activity a poten-
  24|                  tial groundwater threat.
  25|             6. The SPGA shall hold a hearing, in conformity with the provision of MGL c.40A s. 9, within 65 days after the filing of
  26|             the application. Notice of the public hearing shall be given by publication and posting and by first-class mailings to
  27|             "parties of interest" as defined in MGL c.40A s.11. The decision of the SPGA and any extension, modification, or re-
  28|             newal thereof shall be filed with the SPGA and Town Clerk within 90 days following the closing of the public hearing.
  29|             Failure of the SPGA to act within 90 days shall be deemed as a granting of the permit.
  30|             7. If there are material changes to the categories of potentially hazardous materials to be used or stored on the premises
  31|             in quantities greater than those associated with normal household use after the Special Permit has been granted, the own-
  32|             er/operator will file updated information on those categories of hazardous materials with the Fire Department, the Board
  33|             of Health, and the Planning Board.

===PAGE 180=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|             ENFORCEMENT
   2|                   Written notice of any violations of this bylaw shall be given by the Building Commissioner
   3|                   to the responsible person as soon as possible after detection of a violation or a continuing violation. Notice to the
   4|                  assessed owner of the property shall be deemed notice to the responsible person. Such notice shall specify the re-
   5|                   quirement or restriction violated and the nature of the violation, and may also identify the actions necessary to re-
   6|                  move or remedy the violations and preventive measures required for avoiding future violations and a schedule of
   7|                   compliance.
   8|                   A copy of such notice shall be submitted to the Land Use Director, Building Commissioner, Conservation Commis-
   9|                   sion, Department of Public Works, and Board of Health. The cost of containment, clean-up, or other action of compli-
  10|                   ance shall be borne by the owner/operator of the premises.
  11|                    The Building Commissioner may use any portion of Section 250-8.2 of these Zoning Bylaws to assist in the en-
  12|                   forcement of the requirements of this Bylaw.
  13|             SEVERABILITY
  14|                  If any provision of this bylaw is held invalid by a court of competent jurisdiction, the remainder of the bylaw shall
  15|                   not be affected thereby. The invalidity of any section or sections or parts of any section or sections of this bylaw
  16|                  shall not affect the validity of the remainder of this bylaw.

===PAGE 182=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|             ARTICLE 14. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Code of Lunenburg, Section 250-6.4, which is a Section of
   2|             the Protective Bylaw entitled "Driveways and entrances," Subsection C, entitled "Off-street parking design standards;
   3|             general parking and loading provisions," by deleting further Subsection (5)(k) thereof in its entirety and by and substitut-
   4|             ing therefor the following new further Subsection 7:
   5|             (7) Common Driveways
   6|             A common driveway is an access drive to be used for vehicular access to two or more primary dwellings or places of
   7|             business. No person will develop or construct a driveway serving two or more lots or properties without first obtaining a
   8|             Special Permit from the Planning Board in accordance with this Section 250-6.4C(7).
   9|             The Planning Board may issue a Special Permit to allow a common driveway upon finding that the driveway will:
  10|             Promote efficient traffic flow,
  11|             Reduce traffic hazards from numerous individual driveways,
  12|             In the judgment of the Planning Board, such an arrangement will be more advantageous to the neighborhood than indi-
  13|                        vidual driveways,
  14|             Consolidate access to lots across/near wetland resources, and
  15|             Provide sufficient access to emergency vehicles and responders.
  16|             (b)                   Special Permit Requirements
  17|             The driveway will be located entirely within the lots being served.
  18|             A common driveway will be considered satisfactory only if:
  19|             It has been constructed in accordance with the provisions of the Special Permit
  20|             There is a recorded access easement outlining the right of access and including clear provision for maintenance and snow
  21|                        removal; running with the land.
  22|             A common driveway will not be used to provide lot frontage required by this Bylaw or by the Subdivision Control Law.
  23|             Where the access to structures or uses provided by the driveway is substantially different from that which would be pro-
  24|                        vided through the required lot frontages, the Special Permit will not be issued unless the Planning Board finds
  25|                        that the proposed shared driveway and its location are in the public interest.
  26|             Each lot that shares a common driveway will meet the minimum dimensional requirements for the Zoning District in
  27|                        which it is located as outlined in this Bylaw. Specitically, each lot will have the required amount of trontage lo-
  28|                        cated on the street which connects to the common driveway.
  29|             The Planning Board will submit a copy of the Special Permit and approved plans to the Building Commissioner. No
  30|                        building permit will be issued until receipt of the Special Permit and plan.
  31|             No occupancy permit shall be issued for any uses or structures utilizing the common driveway until evidence of record-
  32|                        ing of the required utility, access and maintenance easement has been filed with the Building Commissioner and
  33|                        Planning Board.
  34|             (c)Design Criteria
  35|                  i.Residential
  36|                 Minimum pavement width 18 feet
  37|                  Minimum easement width 24 feet
  38|                 Max grade 10%
  39|                 Curb Cut no closer than 100 feet from centerline on intersection
  40|                                                                                              182

===PAGE 183=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                   Max number of lots 4
   2|                   Max length 1200 feet
   3|                    ii.              Commercial
   4|                   Minimum pavement width 24 feet
   5|                   Minimum easement width 40 feet
   6|                   Max grade 10%
   7|                   Curb Cut no closer than 100 feet from centerline on intersection
   8|                   Max number of lots 6 with public water available, 4 without public water available
   9|                   Max length 1800 feet with public water available, 1200 feet without public water available
  10|                   111.              No grade will exceed 2% within 75 feet of the street right -of-way lines.
  11|                   iv. All common driveways will be rounded at street intersection or cutback to provide a curb radius of not less than
  12|                          30 feet.
  13|                    v. The address of all buildings accessed off the common driveway shall be posted on a sign at the entrance of the
  14|                          driveway that is visible for residents, employees, visitors, and public safety
  15|                    vi. The common driveway will be constructed of materials resistant to erosion and frost heaving and sufficient to
  16|                          support an axle load of 34,000 pounds under all weather conditions. The common driveway will be paved with
  17|                          a 3 inch minimum thickness layer of bituminous concrete.
  18|                    vii. Turnouts will be provided along the common driveways at intervals of 300 feet. The turnouts will be 8 feet
  19|                          wide and 25 feet long.
  20|                   vili. All shared driveways will be provided with a provisions for drainage sufficient to prevent deterioration of the
  21|                          driveway and to prevent any erosion, flooding, or other problems on any property beyond that of the owners of
  22|                          the driveway including the street which it intersects.
  23|               All curves will be designed with a twenty-five foot minimum inside turning radius.
  24|               The common driveway shall be clear of vegetation and overhead obstructions allowing for a vertical clearance of not
  25|                         less than 13ft, bin.
  26|              The SPGA will require that utilities (if any) to the lots be located directly adjacent to or within the common driveway.
  27|              Each branch of the common driveway will be designed such that a twenty-one-foot wheelbase fire truck or other emer-
  28|                          gency vehicle can safely enter and exit the branch.
  29|               A common driveway must originate on approved frontage and must observe a twenty-five-foot setback from the side
  30|                          line between the lot of origin and a lot not served by the common driveway.
  31|               Entrance to driveway shall have a sign indicating the addresses located on the common driveway, to include a diagram
  32|                          map showing their locations. Additionally, each branch of the Common Driveway will have a sign at the inter-
  33|                          section with the main driveway noting the address of the property.
  34|              or take any other action relative thereto. (Submitted by the Planning Board) FINANCE COMMITTEE RECOMMEN-

   1|             ARTICLE 15. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Code of Lunenburg, Sections 250-4.5 and 250-4.6, which
   2|             are Sections of the Protective Bylaw entitled "Commercial District" and "Office Park/Industrial District," respectively,
   3|             by adding thereto new Sections 250-4.5A(6) and 250-4.6A(4), respectively, both as follows:
   4|             Solar Parking Canopy: a special application of a ground-mounted solar energy system that is installed on top of a func-
   5|             tional parking surface that maintains the function of the area beneath the canopy.
   6|             A Solar Parking Canopy may be allowed by Special Permit from the Planning Board in the Commercial and Office/
   7|             Industrial Park Districts. In approving a Special Permit the Planning Board must find the following:
   8|             Equipment associated with the solar canopy system is adequately screened from the parking area and neighboring
   9|                   properties. Required screening must be met using natural planted materials in conjunction with a secure fence.
  10|             Security lighting for parking areas is in place and adequate.
  11|             The installation of the solar canopy system does not reduce the number of parking spaces below the minimum required
  12|                   for the uses present or intended
  13|             A minimum of five percent (5%) of parking spaces in the parking lot will have universal charging facilities for EVs.
  14|                   Brand specific charging facilities are allowed only when they are in addition to the minimum number of universal
  15|                   chargers. A minimum of ten percent (10%) of each type of EV charging spaces must be accessible and comply
  16|                   with the Massachusetts Architectural Access Board Regulations (521 CMR).
  17|             The maximum height for solar canopies is twenty feet (20').
  18|             Solar canopies and all appurtenant structures to a solar canopy system shall meet the requirements of the Zoning Bylaw
  19|                   concerning setbacks and other dimensional requirements.
  20|             All appurtenant structures, including but not limited to equipment shelters, storage facilities, transformers, and sub-
  21|                   stations, must be architecturally compatible with each other and the existing building(s) on-site.
  22|             The installation of the racking system for the solar canopy system considers and provides provisions for changes in
  23|                   grade and how drainage is addressed to prevent flooding and icing below the canopy system.
  24|             The applicant will provide a form of surety, either through an escrow account, bond or otherwise, to cover the cost
  25|             of removal in the event the Town must remove the installation and remediate the landscape, in an amount and form
  26|             determined to be reasonable by the development plan review authority, but in no event to exceed more than 125%
  27|             of the cost of removal and compliance with the additional requirements set forth herein, as determined by the pro-
  28|             ject proponent. Such surety will not be required for municipally or state-owned facilities. The project owner/
  29|             operator shall submit a fully inclusive estimate of the costs associated with removal, prepared by a qualified engi-
  30|             neer. The amount shall include a mechanism for calculating increased removal cost due to inflation. or take any
  31|             other action relative thereto. (Submitted by the Planning Board) FINANCE COMMITTEE RECOMMENDA-
  32|             TION: No Direct Financial Impact SELECT BOARD & PLANNING BOARD RECOMMENDATION: Approval.
  33|             VOTED (62 YES, 6 NO 45 needed for 2/3rds majority) to amend the Code of Lunenburg, Sections 250-4.5
  34|             and 250-4.6, which are Sections of the Protective Bylaw entitled "Commercial District" and "Office Park/
  35|             Industrial District," respectively, by adding thereto new Sections 250-4.5A(6) and 250-4.6A(4), respectively,
  36|             as printed in the Special Town Meeting Warrant Booklet with the following further amendment: adding a
  37|             new sentence to the end of Subsection (e), stating "In granting the special permit, the Planning Board may
  38|             approve a higher height where the Board determines such height is needed for emergency vehicle or snow
  39|             removal services for the subject site."
